Servant Leadership Guide

Learning to lead by serving · One day at a time

Contents

Choose a week, then tap any day. The answer sheet follows Day 130.

paragonpdxContents
001

Week 1 · The Prophetic Profile (Isaiah's Servant Songs)

Monday – Greatness Comes Through Serving

Week opening

Long before Jesus was born, the prophet Isaiah painted a picture of the kind of leader God was sending. This Servant would not shout or push people around. He would be gentle with the hurting — He would not break a bruised reed or snuff out a small flame. This week you will meet that Servant up close, and you will see why Martin Luther King Jr. said anybody can be great, because anybody can serve. The journey starts here: greatness comes through serving.

"Everybody can be great…because anybody can serve. You don't have to have a college degree to serve. You don't have to make your subject and verb agree to serve. You only need a heart full of grace. A soul generated by love." — Martin Luther King Jr., from his sermon "The Drum Major Instinct" (Ebenezer Baptist Church, February 4, 1968)
Bible (ESV): "Behold my servant, whom I uphold, my chosen, in whom my soul delights; I have put my Spirit upon him; he will bring forth justice to the nations. ... A bruised reed he will not break, and a faintly burning wick he will not quench; he will faithfully bring forth justice." — Isaiah 42:1, 3

Long before Jesus was born, the prophet Isaiah painted a picture of God's chosen Servant. Notice something surprising: this Servant does not lead by shouting or pushing people around. He does not break the bruised reed — a reed that is cracked and weak — and he does not quench a candle wick that is barely still burning. That means he is gentle with people who are hurting and almost out of strength. Martin Luther King Jr. said that anyone can be great because anyone can serve. You do not need a fancy degree or a big title. You only need a heart full of grace and a soul filled with love. Think about what this looks like in real life. At home, it means speaking kindly to a tired spouse or a frustrated child instead of snapping back. At work, it means helping a new coworker who keeps making mistakes instead of rolling your eyes. At church or in your neighborhood, it means noticing the person who sits alone and going to sit with them. Serving people gently does not mean being weak. The Servant in Isaiah is strong enough to bring justice to the whole world, but he chooses to be tender with the wounded. That is the kind of leader God honors. This week, ask yourself: who is a "bruised reed" in my life — someone bending under pressure? How can I help them stand tall again instead of pushing them down? When you choose gentleness over harshness, you are leading the way Isaiah's Servant leads, and the way Jesus leads too.

Prayer:

Dear God, You are so gentle with us when we are weak — You never break the bruised reed or snuff out the small flame. Make our hearts soft like Yours. Tomorrow morning, when our child is cranky and slow getting ready for school, help us speak kindly instead of snapping. At work, when a coworker messes up again, keep us from rolling our eyes and teach us to help instead. Show us the bruised reeds in our lives — the people bending under pressure — and give us tender hands to lift them up. Make us leaders who help people stand tall again. In Jesus' name, Amen.

Study questions

1What does the "bruised reed" in Isaiah 42:3 stand for?

  1. A powerful king
  2. A weak or hurting person
  3. A type of sword
  4. A strong building

2According to Martin Luther King Jr., what do you need in order to serve?

  1. A college degree
  2. Perfect grammar
  3. A heart full of grace and a soul generated by love
  4. A big bank account

3Which of these does the Servant in Isaiah 42 NOT do?

  1. Bring justice to the nations
  2. Shout and raise his voice in the streets
  3. Care for the weak
  4. Carry God's Spirit

4In real life, being gentle like the Servant means…

  1. Ignoring people who are hurting
  2. Speaking kindly to a tired family member instead of snapping
  3. Never telling the truth
  4. Only helping people you like

5Isaiah 42 says the Servant will bring what to the nations?

  1. Justice
  2. Money
  3. Fear
  4. War

002

Week 1 · The Prophetic Profile (Isaiah's Servant Songs)

Tuesday – Called and Chosen for a Purpose

"I alone cannot change the world, but I can cast a stone across the waters to create many ripples." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): "The LORD called me from the womb, from the body of my mother he named my name. ... I will make you as a light for the nations, that my salvation may reach to the end of the earth." — Isaiah 49:1, 6

Isaiah 49 gives us the second Servant Song, and it begins with something amazing: God called the Servant before he was even born. From his mother's womb, God had a plan for his life and a name for him. That tells us something wonderful about you too. You were not an accident. God knew you before you were born, and He made you for a reason. Mother Teresa said, "I alone cannot change the world, but I can cast a stone across the waters to create many ripples." One small stone can send ripples farther than you can see. In the same way, God told the Servant, "It is too small a thing for you to only help one group of people. I will make you a light for the nations, so that my salvation may reach to the end of the earth." One faithful servant became a blessing for the whole world. You may feel small. You may think your life does not matter much. But God loves to use small, faithful people to do big things. The meal you share with a lonely neighbor, the patient words you give your child, the honest work you do when no one is watching — these are stones dropped into water. You may never see all the ripples. A kind word you say today might lift someone who then lifts someone else, and on and on. Servant leaders do not wait until they feel important. They serve right where they are, trusting God to carry the ripples farther than they can reach. This week, remember that you were called before you were born, and drop your stone with joy.

Prayer:

Dear Father, thank You for calling us before we were ever born and giving each of us a name. When we feel too small to matter, remind us that one little stone can send ripples farther than we can see. Help us drop our stones with joy — the meal we share with the lonely neighbor next door, the patient words we give our child at bedtime, the honest work we do when nobody is watching. We may never see how far the ripples go, but You do. Use our small acts of love to carry Your light to people who need it most. In Jesus' name, Amen.

Study questions

1When did God call the Servant in Isaiah 49:1?

  1. When he turned thirty
  2. Before he was born, from his mother's womb
  3. After he became famous
  4. He was never called

2What does Mother Teresa's stone in the water show us?

  1. Small acts of love can spread farther than we can see
  2. Stones are very heavy
  3. Water is pretty to look at
  4. Only big actions matter

3God said the Servant would be a light for…

  1. Only his own family
  2. The Gentiles, so salvation could reach the ends of the earth
  3. Nobody at all
  4. Only kings and rulers

4Which of these is a "small stone" of service?

  1. Sharing a meal with a lonely neighbor
  2. Buying a fancy sports car
  3. Winning an argument
  4. Ignoring a hurting friend

5Servant leaders serve…

  1. Only when they feel important
  2. Right where they are, trusting God with the results
  3. Only famous people
  4. Only on holidays

003

Week 1 · The Prophetic Profile (Isaiah's Servant Songs)

Wednesday – Steady When the Road Is Hard

"We must learn to regard people less in the light of what they do or omit to do, and more in the light of what they suffer." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer, Letters and Papers from Prison
Bible (ESV): "I gave my back to those who strike, and my cheeks to those who pull out the beard; I hid not my face from disgrace and spitting. But the Lord GOD helps me; therefore I have not been disgraced; therefore I have set my face like a flint, and I know that I shall not be put to shame." — Isaiah 50:6–7

The third Servant Song, in Isaiah 50, shows us a Servant who faces terrible treatment. People beat his back, pulled out his beard, mocked him, and spit in his face. Yet he did not run away or give up. He said, "Because the Sovereign LORD helps me, I will not be disgraced. I have set my face like flint." Flint is a very hard stone. Setting your face like flint means deciding, deep inside, that you will keep going no matter what. Dietrich Bonhoeffer, a pastor who stood up for what was right even when it cost him his life, said we must see people less by what they do wrong and more by what they suffer. In other words, a servant leader looks at people's pain before judging their mistakes. This matters so much. When someone is rude to you, it is easy to write them off. But a servant leader asks, "What is this person going through?" Maybe the grumpy coworker just lost a loved one. Maybe the angry teenager is scared inside. Serving people is not always easy or fun. Sometimes the people you help do not thank you. Sometimes doing the right thing costs you time, money, or comfort. The Servant shows us that we can stay steady because God helps us. You do not have to be strong on your own. When serving feels hard, lean on God's strength. Set your face like flint, keep your heart soft, and keep going. That is what servant leaders do.

Prayer:

Dear God, thank You for the Servant who set His face like flint and did not turn back, even when people hurt Him. Give us that same steady courage. When a coworker is rude to us at the start of a hard day, help us wonder what pain they are carrying instead of writing them off. When we serve and nobody says thank you, be our strength so we do not quit. Teach us to look at others through their suffering, not just their mistakes. Keep our hearts soft and our feet steady, and help us keep going — because You help us, and You never leave us alone. In Jesus' name, Amen.

Study questions

1In Isaiah 50:6, what did the Servant NOT hide his face from?

  1. Mocking and spitting
  2. Praise and honor
  3. Gifts and rewards
  4. Food and drink

2"Setting your face like flint" means…

  1. Turning into a stone statue
  2. Firmly deciding to keep going no matter what
  3. Running away from trouble
  4. Stopping caring about others

3Why could the Servant stay steady through suffering?

  1. He was never really hurt
  2. Because the Sovereign LORD helped him
  3. He was rich and powerful
  4. People were always nice to him

4Bonhoeffer says we should see people more in the light of…

  1. What they do wrong
  2. What they suffer
  3. The clothes they wear
  4. The money they have

5When serving feels hard, servant leaders…

  1. Quit right away
  2. Lean on God's strength and keep going
  3. Blame everyone else
  4. Only serve easy people

004

Week 1 · The Prophetic Profile (Isaiah's Servant Songs)

Thursday – Carrying the Pain of Others

"What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." — Nelson Mandela (90th birthday celebration of Walter Sisulu, May 18, 2002)
Bible (ESV): "But he was pierced for our transgressions; he was crushed for our iniquities; upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed. All we like sheep have gone astray; we have turned—every one—to his own way; and the LORD has laid on him the iniquity of us all." — Isaiah 53:5–6

The fourth Servant Song, in Isaiah 52:13 through chapter 53, is the most moving picture of all. Here the Servant does something shocking: He takes our place. "He was pierced for our transgressions, he was crushed for our iniquities; upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed." Transgressions are the wrong things we do. Iniquities are our sins and broken ways. The Servant did nothing wrong, yet He carried the pain that belonged to us. That is the heart of servant leadership: carrying the weight so someone else does not have to. Nelson Mandela, who spent twenty-seven years in prison for fighting against the injustice of apartheid in South Africa, said that what makes a life count is the difference we make in the lives of others. Mandela gave up his freedom so others could be free. In a smaller way, we can do the same every day. When you sit with a friend who is grieving and simply listen, you are carrying part of their sadness. When you help a coworker who is drowning in work, you are lifting their load. When you stand up for someone who is being picked on, you are taking a little of their pain onto yourself. It will cost you time and comfort. But that is exactly what Jesus did for us — and so much more. He carried our sins all the way to the cross. When we carry one another's burdens, we show the world what Jesus looks like. This week, look for someone with a heavy load, and help them carry it.

Prayer:

Dear Lord, thank You that Jesus was pierced for our wrongs and crushed for our sins — that by His wounds we are healed. He carried the weight so we would not have to. Teach us to carry weight for others in our everyday lives. When a friend is grieving, give us the grace to sit quietly beside them and listen, even when we do not know what to say. When a coworker is drowning in work, help us step in and lift some of the load. It will cost us time and comfort — but that is what love does. Make our lives count by the difference we make for others. In Jesus' name, Amen.

Study questions

1Isaiah 53:5 says the Servant was pierced for…

  1. Our transgressions
  2. His own crimes
  3. Fun and games
  4. Money and fame

2"With his wounds we are healed" means…

  1. The Servant needed a doctor
  2. His suffering brings us healing and peace
  3. Wounds are a good thing
  4. Nobody ever gets sick

3Mandela said the significance of our lives is determined by…

  1. How long we live
  2. What difference we make in the lives of others
  3. How much money we earn
  4. How famous we become

4Carrying someone's burden could look like…

  1. Sitting with a grieving friend and listening
  2. Ignoring someone who is hurting
  3. Laughing at someone's pain
  4. Walking away from trouble

5Who does the suffering Servant point us to?

  1. A made-up hero
  2. Jesus, who carried our sins for us
  3. A rich and powerful king
  4. No one in particular

Week 1 · The Prophetic Profile (Isaiah's Servant Songs)

Friday – The Servant Who Looks Like Jesus

"Do not waste time bothering whether you 'love' your neighbor; act as if you did. As soon as we do this we find one of the great secrets. When you are behaving as if you loved someone, you will presently come to love him." — C.S. Lewis, Mere Christianity
Bible (ESV): "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many." — Mark 10:45

All week we have looked at Isaiah's four Servant Songs: the gentle Servant who will not break a bruised reed, the called Servant who becomes a light to the nations, the steady Servant who sets his face like flint, and the suffering Servant who carries our pain. Every one of these pictures points to Jesus. Jesus said it plainly in Mark 10:45: "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many." The King of kings came as a servant. He washed His disciples' feet. He touched people no one else would touch. And He gave His life on the cross to set us free. C.S. Lewis wrote that we should not waste time asking whether we feel love for our neighbor — we should simply act as if we do. When we behave as if we love someone, we will soon come to truly love them. That is such good news. You do not have to wait until you feel like serving. Just start acting in love — help, listen, give, forgive — and your heart will follow. Servant leadership is not a title you earn. It is a way of living. It means choosing gentleness over harshness, purpose over comfort, steadiness over quitting, and other people's good over your own ease. This week, pick one person and serve them the way Jesus serves you. Make the meal. Write the note. Say the prayer. Offer the ride. Then watch what God does. You were made to be a servant leader, and the world needs the light you carry.

Prayer:

Dear God, thank You for this whole week — for the gentle Servant, the called Servant, the steady Servant, and the suffering Servant. Thank You most of all for Jesus, who did not come to be served but to serve, and gave His life for us. We do not always feel love, but teach us to act in love anyway — to make the meal, write the note, say the prayer, give the ride — and watch our hearts follow. Tonight at the kitchen table, show each of us one person to serve this week, and give us the joy of doing it. Make our whole lives look like Yours. We love You. In Jesus' name, Amen.

Study questions

1In Mark 10:45, Jesus says He came to…

  1. Be served by everyone
  2. Serve, and to give His life as a ransom for many
  3. Rule as an earthly king
  4. Avoid all suffering

2C.S. Lewis says that when we act as if we love someone…

  1. Nothing ever changes
  2. We will presently come to love him
  3. We become fake and dishonest
  4. People will dislike us

3Which of these is NOT one of Isaiah's Servant Songs?

  1. The gentle Servant in Isaiah 42
  2. The called Servant in Isaiah 49
  3. The Servant who only serves rich people
  4. The suffering Servant in Isaiah 52–53

4Servant leadership is…

  1. A title you earn one day
  2. A way of living every single day
  3. Only for pastors and priests
  4. All about being in charge

5This week, you are encouraged to…

  1. Wait until you feel like serving
  2. Pick one person and serve them the way Jesus serves you
  3. Serve only yourself
  4. Do nothing at all
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay question: Write a short essay of 5–8 sentences. Which of Isaiah's four Servant Songs speaks to you the most, and why? Describe one specific way you will put what you learned into practice by serving someone in your family, your neighborhood, or your church this week.

Looking back

This week you saw God's Servant the way Isaiah saw Him — chosen, gentle, and strong enough to bring justice to the whole world. You learned that you are called and chosen for a purpose, that steady faith holds up when the road gets hard, and that a true servant carries the pain of others. The Servant Isaiah described looks exactly like Jesus. Next week, you will watch that Servant lay everything down.

Week 2 · The Incarnation and Emptying (Philippians 2)

Monday – The Choice to Lay It Down

Week opening

Jesus had everything — He was in the very form of God. And He laid it all down. This week walks through Philippians 2, where Paul shows us a King who emptied Himself and took the form of a servant. You will learn what it means to choose the low place on purpose, to live for others instead of yourself, and to lift people up the way Jesus lifted us. Even small hands can do big good when they serve.

"If you are humble nothing will touch you, neither praise nor disgrace, because you know what you are." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): "who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men." (Philippians 2:6–7)

Picture the throne of heaven. That is where Jesus was. He was in the form of God — all power, all glory, all praise, forever.

But he did not hold onto it. He did not use his position to gain more for himself. Instead, he emptied himself. He laid down his crown and stepped into our world. He took the form of a servant. He was born in the likeness of men — flesh and blood, hunger and thirst, tears and tiredness — just like you and me.

Why does this matter for leaders? Because most leaders do the opposite. Most leaders climb to get more: more praise, more power, more comfort. Jesus climbed down. True servant leadership starts with this choice: laying down what you could claim, so that others can rise.

Mother Teresa lived this. She could have lived a comfortable life, but she chose to serve the poorest of the poor. And she said something wise: "If you are humble nothing will touch you, neither praise nor disgrace, because you know what you are." When you know who you are in God — deeply loved, nothing more and nothing less — you are free. Praise does not make your head big. Criticism does not break your heart. You do not have to prove anything to anyone.

So what does laying it down look like in real life? It looks like the manager who stays late to help clean up after the meeting instead of leaving it for others. It looks like the parent who says, "I was wrong, please forgive me," to their own child. It looks like the team captain who does the hardest drill right beside the weakest player. It looks like you, this week, taking the low task nobody wants and doing it with joy.

A leader who has laid down their ego leads from peace, not pride. And people feel safe around a leader like that. They grow. They heal. They begin to serve others too. That is how Jesus changes the world — one laid-down life at a time.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, You had everything — all glory, all power — yet You laid it all down and took the form of a servant. Teach us to lay down our pride too. When we want to win an argument at home and prove we are right, help us let it go. When we are tempted to take credit at work, give us the grace to share it instead. Remind us that we are deeply loved by You, so praise does not puff us up and criticism does not break us. Today, show us the low task nobody wants — and help us do it with joy, the way You would. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Philippians 2:6–7, what did Jesus do even though he was "in the form of God"?

  1. He demanded that everyone worship him
  2. He stayed in heaven and sent angels instead
  3. He emptied himself and took the form of a servant
  4. He used his power to gain more glory for himself

2What does Mother Teresa's quote teach about a humble person?

  1. A humble person is afraid of people
  2. A humble person is not shaken by praise or disgrace because they know who they are
  3. A humble person never feels joy
  4. A humble person tries to be invisible

3In the lesson, which of these is an example of laying down your ego in real life?

  1. Taking the low task nobody wants and doing it with joy
  2. Making sure everyone knows about your good deeds
  3. Refusing to apologize so you look strong
  4. Leaving the cleanup for someone else

4Why is a leader who has laid down their ego safe for others?

  1. Because they never make mistakes
  2. Because they always agree with everyone
  3. Because they lead from peace, not pride
  4. Because they have no feelings

5What does it mean that Jesus "emptied himself"?

  1. He stopped being God
  2. He laid down his glory and rights to become a servant like us
  3. He disappeared for a while
  4. He only pretended to be human

Week 2 · The Incarnation and Emptying (Philippians 2)

Tuesday – Greatness Through Serving

"Everybody can be great, because everybody can serve." — Martin Luther King Jr., from the sermon "The Drum Major Instinct," preached at Ebenezer Baptist Church, February 4, 1968
Bible (ESV): "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many." (Mark 10:45)

One Sunday morning in 1968, Dr. Martin Luther King Jr. stood in his church and talked about a hunger that lives in all of us: the "drum major instinct." That is the want to be out in front, to be seen and praised and called great.

King had just read the story in Mark 10, where two of Jesus' disciples, James and John, asked for the best seats in his kingdom — one at his right hand and one at his left. They wanted the top spots. And Jesus answered with words that turned greatness upside down: "But it shall not be so among you. Instead, whoever would be great among you must be your servant, and whoever would be first among you must be slave of all."

Then King said it plainly: "Everybody can be great, because everybody can serve." You do not have to have a college degree to serve. You do not have to be rich or famous or powerful to serve. You only need a heart full of grace and a soul full of love.

Think about what this means for your life. The world tells you greatness looks like a corner office, a big title, a crowd cheering your name. Jesus says greatness looks like a towel and a basin, like washing feet. It looks like giving your life — your time, your energy, your care — as a ransom for many, to set others free.

Who can be great? The teenager who spends Saturday mornings at the food bank. The coworker who patiently teaches the new hire the ropes. The neighbor who checks on the elderly woman next door. The parent who reads one more bedtime story when they are tired. Every one of them is living greatness.

But be careful about one thing: do not serve just to be seen. King warned about that too. True service does not need a spotlight. Serve because you love people, not because you want applause. The drum major instinct wants the front of the parade — but the servant of Christ finds joy at the back, helping everyone else march.

Prayer:

Father God, we all want to be great — to be first, to be seen. Take that hunger and turn it toward service. Give us hearts full of grace and souls full of love. Show us that true greatness looks like a towel and a basin, not a crowd cheering our name. Help us serve quietly: the teenager bagging groceries at the food bank on a Saturday morning, the neighbor checking in on the elderly woman across the street, the tired parent reading one more bedtime story. Forgive us when we serve just to be noticed. Teach us to love people, not applause. In Jesus' name, Amen.

Study questions

1In King's sermon, what is the "drum major instinct"?

  1. A love of music and parades
  2. The human hunger to be out front, seen, and called great
  3. A fear of leading others
  4. The desire to play an instrument

2What did Jesus say in Mark 10 about becoming great?

  1. Whoever would be great must be your servant
  2. Whoever wants to become great must gather the most followers
  3. Whoever wants to become great must never serve anyone
  4. Whoever wants to become great must be born into a rich family

3According to the lesson, what do you need in order to serve?

  1. A college degree and lots of money
  2. A famous name and a big platform
  3. A heart full of grace and a soul full of love
  4. Permission from a leader

4What does the lesson warn against?

  1. Serving only to be seen and praised
  2. Serving people you do not know
  3. Serving more than once a week
  4. Serving without telling anyone

5What does "give his life as a ransom for many" mean in this lesson?

  1. Jesus paid money to free prisoners
  2. Jesus gave his time, his care, and even his life to set others free
  3. Jesus asked his followers to pay him back
  4. Jesus ran away from danger

Week 2 · The Incarnation and Emptying (Philippians 2)

Wednesday – Living for Others

"The church is the church only when it exists for others." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer, Letters and Papers from Prison (written from his prison cell, 1944)
Bible (ESV): "Do nothing from selfish ambition or conceit, but in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others." (Philippians 2:3–4)

Dietrich Bonhoeffer was a pastor in Germany during the dark days of World War II. He watched many churches close their doors to the suffering around them. They cared about protecting themselves — their buildings, their image, their safety. Bonhoeffer was thrown into prison for standing up for what was right. And from his prison cell, he wrote these powerful words: "The church is the church only when it exists for others."

Read that again slowly. A church is only truly a church when it lives for others, not for itself. The same is true for every leader, every family, every team. We are not at our best when we exist to serve ourselves. We are at our best when we exist for others.

Paul wrote the same truth long before Bonhoeffer: "In humility count others more significant than yourselves." That does not mean you are worthless — far from it. You are made in God's image and deeply loved. It means you choose to put the good of others ahead of your own comfort. "Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others."

What does that look like in real life? It looks like the boss who asks her team, "What do you need to do your best work?" It looks like the basketball player who passes the ball to the open teammate instead of forcing a bad shot for his own glory. It looks like the friend who puts down the phone and listens — really listens — instead of waiting for a turn to talk. It looks like a church that opens its doors to feed hungry neighbors instead of only feeding itself.

Selfish ambition says, "What can I get?" Conceit says, "Look at me." But the servant leader says, "What do you need?" and "How can I help?" That one small change in the question changes everything. Try it this week: in every conversation, in every decision, ask how it can serve the other person. You will be amazed at what God does through a life lived for others.

Prayer:

Lord, forgive us for living only for ourselves. Forgive our selfish ambition and our need to be noticed. Teach us to put others first the way You did. At work, help us ask, "What do you need to do your best?" instead of looking out for ourselves. At home, help us put down our phones and really listen to the people we love, instead of waiting for our turn to talk. Give us Bonhoeffer's courage to stand with those who suffer. Make our homes, our teams, and our churches places that exist for others — every single day. Amen.

Study questions

1Where did Bonhoeffer write the words "The church is the church only when it exists for others"?

  1. In a letter to a king
  2. In his prison cell during World War II
  3. In a newspaper article
  4. In a speech at a wedding

2What did Bonhoeffer see many churches doing in his day?

  1. Giving away all their money
  2. Protecting themselves instead of helping the suffering
  3. Traveling to other countries
  4. Building bigger buildings for the poor

3According to Philippians 2:3–4, what should we do instead of acting out of selfish ambition?

  1. Compete harder to win
  2. In humility, count others more significant than ourselves and look to their interests
  3. Ignore other people's needs
  4. Focus only on our own goals

4What does "count others more significant than yourselves" mean in this lesson?

  1. That you are worthless and unimportant
  2. That you choose to put the good of others ahead of your own comfort
  3. That you should never rest or take care of yourself
  4. That other people are always right

5In the lesson, what question does the servant leader ask?

  1. "What can I get?"
  2. "Look at me — aren't I great?"
  3. "What do you need?" and "How can I help?"
  4. "Why should I care?"

Week 2 · The Incarnation and Emptying (Philippians 2)

Thursday – A Life That Lifts Others

"What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." — Nelson Mandela, at the 90th birthday celebration of Walter Sisulu, Johannesburg, May 18, 2002
Bible (ESV): "Have this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus, who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men. And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross." (Philippians 2:5–8)

Nelson Mandela spent 27 years in prison. He lost his freedom, his family time, his youth. When he finally walked out, he could have come out full of hate. Instead, he chose to lift up a whole nation. He said, "What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead."

Stop and think about that. One day, each of us will leave this world. The question will not be how long we lived, how much money we made, or how many awards we won. The question will be: what difference did we make to the lives of others? That is the true measure of a life.

Jesus is the perfect picture of this. Paul tells us to have "the same mind as Christ Jesus." What was his mind? He was in the form of God, yet he did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped. He emptied himself. He became a servant. He humbled himself to the point of death on a cross. Every step down was a step toward lifting us up. His whole life was difference-making.

A servant leader measures success the same way. Not by how many people follow them, but by how many people they lift. Not by how high they climb, but by how many they help climb.

Look around you. The teacher who stays late so a struggling student can finally pass. The father who works long hours so his children can go to school. The leader who opens doors for others instead of walking through them alone. These are significant lives.

This week, ask yourself one question each morning: "Whose life will be better today because of me?" Then go and do one thing — one kind, brave, humble thing — that makes the answer real. That is how a life becomes significant. Not by living for yourself, but by pouring yourself out for others, just as Christ did for you.

Prayer:

God of mercy, thank You for Jesus, who poured Himself out all the way to the cross so we could be lifted up. Teach us to measure our lives the way You do — not by what we gain, but by the difference we make for others. Forgive us for chasing things that do not last. Give us Mandela's heart, choosing love over hate, and Christ's heart, choosing the cross over comfort. Each morning, help us ask, "Whose life will be better today because of me?" — then give us the courage to do one kind, brave, humble thing that makes the answer real. May our lives lift others, to Your glory. Amen.

Study questions

1How many years did Mandela spend in prison, according to the lesson?

  1. 5 years
  2. 10 years
  3. 27 years
  4. 40 years

2According to Mandela's quote, what determines the significance of a life?

  1. How long we live
  2. How much money we make
  3. What difference we have made to the lives of others
  4. How many awards we win

3What was Jesus' mindset, according to Philippians 2:5–8?

  1. To use his power for his own advantage
  2. To avoid suffering at all costs
  3. To empty himself, become a servant, and humble himself even to death on a cross
  4. To stay in heaven where it was safe

4How does a servant leader measure success?

  1. By how many people follow them
  2. By how many people they lift up
  3. By how high they climb
  4. By how much they are praised

5What daily question does the lesson suggest asking each morning?

  1. "How can I get ahead today?"
  2. "Whose life will be better today because of me?"
  3. "What will make me happy today?"
  4. "Who should serve me today?"

Week 2 · The Incarnation and Emptying (Philippians 2)

Friday – Small Hands, Big Good

"Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): "Therefore God has highly exalted him and bestowed on him the name that is above every name, so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, in heaven and on earth and under the earth, and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father." (Philippians 2:9–11) "And your Father who sees in secret will reward you." (Matthew 6:4)

Here is the beautiful ending of the story. After Jesus humbled himself all the way down — lower than anyone had ever gone — God lifted him all the way up. "Therefore God exalted him to the highest place." Every knee will bow. Every tongue will confess that Jesus Christ is Lord.

Do you see the pattern? Jesus went down, and God brought him up. This is how God's kingdom works: those who humble themselves will be lifted up. We do not lift ourselves. We do not fight for the top. We go low in love, and God does the lifting.

Desmond Tutu understood this. He helped bring down apartheid in South Africa — not with armies, but with courage, truth, and love. He said, "Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world."

Notice he did not say, "Do a huge, famous thing." He said, "Do your little bit." Bring the soup to the sick neighbor. Share your lunch with the lonely kid. Write the thank-you note. Forgive the small hurt. Apologize first. Little bits of good, done every day, pile up until they overwhelm the darkness.

Leaders sometimes wait for the big moment — the big speech, the big project, the big stage. But Jesus' greatness came from daily obedience, one act of love at a time. He washed feet. He touched lepers. He ate with outcasts. Small hands. Big good.

And here is the promise: God sees what you do in secret. The kindness nobody notices. The sacrifice nobody applauds. God sees, and God honors it. You do not need a stage. You do not need a title. You only need willing hands and a loving heart.

You have walked through the mind of Christ this week: he laid down his glory, he became a servant, he lived for others, he poured out his life, and God lifted him high. Now it is your turn. Go and live it — one little bit of good at a time.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, we end this week in awe of You. You went down so we could be lifted up. Teach us Your pattern: go low in love, and let You do the lifting. Show us our little bit of good today — the soup carried to the sick neighbor, the lunch shared with the lonely kid, the thank-you note written, the small hurt forgiven. Help us do these things quietly, without telling everyone or posting about them, knowing You see every secret kindness and will honor it. We do not need a stage. We only need willing hands and loving hearts. Fill our world with little bits of good until Your love overwhelms the darkness. Amen.

  2. God highly exalted him and bestowed on him the name that is above every name
  3. God punished him for going too low
  4. God told him never to serve again

2What pattern does the lesson say runs through God's kingdom?

  1. Those who push others down will rise
  2. Those who humble themselves will be lifted up
  3. Those who are loudest will be heard
  4. Those who serve will always stay low

3What did Desmond Tutu say about doing good?

  1. Only do big, famous things
  2. Wait until you are powerful to help
  3. Do your little bit of good where you are; little bits of good put together overwhelm the world
  4. Let someone else do the good

4According to the lesson, how did Jesus' greatness grow?

  1. Through one big famous moment
  2. Through daily obedience, one act of love at a time
  3. Through demanding honor from others
  4. Through avoiding small tasks

5What does Matthew 6:4 promise about secret kindness?

  1. It does not matter
  2. God sees in secret and will reward it
  3. Only people can reward it
  4. It only counts if you post it online

Looking back

This week you watched Jesus lay down His glory and pick up a servant's towel. You learned that greatness comes through serving, that living for others is the whole point, and that a life poured out lifts everyone around it. Even the smallest act of kindness carries big weight in God's hands. Next week, you will find out why only a secure heart can truly serve.

Week 3 · Secure Identity as a Prerequisite for Service

Monday – Loved Before You Ever Lift a Finger

Week opening

Before Jesus did a single miracle, His Father said, ‘This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased.’ He was loved before He ever lifted a finger. This week you will learn why that matters for you: you cannot give away what you do not have. A leader who knows they are loved does not serve to earn love — they serve because they are already full. When your heart is secure, your hands are free to be kind without keeping score.

"There is no pit so deep that God's love is not deeper still." — Corrie ten Boom, The Hiding Place
Bible (ESV): "And behold, a voice from heaven said, 'This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased.'" — Matthew 3:17. "We love because he first loved us." — 1 John 4:19

Before Jesus ever preached a sermon, healed a sick person, or fed a hungry crowd, something beautiful happened. At His baptism, the sky opened and God the Father spoke: "This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased." Think about that. Jesus had not done a single miracle yet. He had earned nothing. And God was already proud of Him. God gave Jesus His love and His approval first — before any work, before any success, before anyone was watching.

That is exactly how God treats you. Your worth does not come from how much you do. It comes from how deeply you are loved. The Bible says, "We love because he first loved us." He loved you first — not after you proved yourself, not when you got your life together, but first. Your identity was settled before you ever lifted a finger.

So many of us serve backwards. We say yes to everything, hoping people will notice. Hoping we will feel important. Hoping God will finally be pleased. But serving to earn love is like pouring water into a cup with a hole in the bottom. It never stays full. You end up tired, hurt, and empty, always chasing the next thank-you.

Corrie ten Boom learned this in the darkest place imaginable — a Nazi prison camp. Everything was taken from her: her freedom, her family, her comfort. And there, in that pit, she discovered that God's love goes deeper still. Nothing could take it away, because she never earned it in the first place.

When you know you are already loved, serving changes completely. You do not need applause. You can do the quiet job nobody sees and still feel joy, because your heart is already full. That is what it means to serve from a secure heart. Rest today in this simple truth: you are God's child, and He is pleased with you.

Prayer:

Dear Father, thank You for loving me first — before I preached, healed, or served, before I did a single thing for You. When I look at myself, You see Your child, and You are pleased. This morning, when I am tempted to say yes to everything just to feel important, remind me that I do not have to earn Your love. When my heart chases the next thank-you, fill it with You instead. Teach me to serve from the love I already have — to love freely, kindly, and without keeping score. Let my cup overflow from Yours, so love spills out on everyone around me. I pray in the name of Jesus, amen.

Study questions

1What did God say about Jesus at His baptism, before Jesus had done any miracles?

  1. He told Jesus to work harder
  2. "This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased."
  3. He said nothing until Jesus proved Himself
  4. He told Jesus He needed to earn His love

2According to 1 John 4:19, why are we able to love others?

  1. Because people always deserve it
  2. Because we feel like it
  3. Because he first loved us
  4. Because we want to be praised

3What is the problem with serving to earn love?

  1. It works perfectly every time
  2. It makes God proud of us
  3. It leaves us tired and empty, because it is never enough
  4. It helps other people more

4What did Corrie ten Boom learn in the prison camp?

  1. That God's love is deeper than any pit
  2. That she had to be perfect to be loved
  3. That love only exists on good days
  4. That serving others is a waste of time

5What does it mean to "serve from a full heart"?

  1. To serve only when others are watching
  2. To serve out of the love God has already given you
  3. To serve only people who thank you
  4. To serve so you can feel important

Week 3 · Secure Identity as a Prerequisite for Service

Tuesday – Kindness Without Keeping Score

"There are three ways to ultimate success: The first way is to be kind. The second way is to be kind. The third way is to be kind." — Fred Rogers
Bible (ESV): "For am I now seeking the approval of man, or of God? Or am I trying to please man? If I were still trying to please man, I would not be a servant of Christ." — Galatians 1:10

Have you ever done something kind, and then felt hurt when nobody thanked you? Have you ever helped someone and watched them thank everyone but you? If we are honest, most of us have felt that sting. That hurt feeling is a quiet sign that we were serving for applause, not from love. There is nothing wrong with the act — but the reason behind it was shaky.

Fred Rogers spent his whole life being kind — to children, to neighbors, to strangers. He said the way to true success is simple: be kind, be kind, be kind. He did not say "be famous." He did not say "be praised." Kindness itself is the win. And kindness only stays sweet when you do not need something back for it.

Paul understood this deeply. He wrote, "For am I now seeking the approval of man, or of God? Or am I trying to please man? If I were still trying to please man, I would not be a servant of Christ." Paul is drawing a clear line. You cannot serve two masters. If your heart is chasing people's approval, every act of service becomes a performance. You will smile while you help, but inside you will be counting who noticed and who did not.

But when your identity is secure — when you already know that God loves you and is pleased with you — you are free. You can help set up chairs, wash dishes, or visit a lonely neighbor, and it does not matter if anyone claps. You are not working for human masters. Your audience is One, and He already spoke His approval over your life.

So here is a small challenge for this week: do one kind thing that no one will ever know about. Do not tell anyone. Do not post about it. Then notice how it feels — not because someone praised you, but because your heart knows you did it for God. That quiet joy is what secure serving feels like.

Prayer:

Lord, I confess that too often I serve so people will notice me. Forgive me for chasing applause instead of chasing You. Teach my heart to rest in Your approval alone. When I set up the chairs or wash the dishes and nobody says thank you, help me smile anyway — because You saw it, and Your smile is enough. Make me kind three times over: in my words, in my actions, and in my thoughts. This week, help me do one kind thing that no one will ever know about, and let the quiet joy of it remind me that my audience is One. In Jesus' name, amen.

  4. Be smart, be fast, be first

  1. Be a servant of Christ
  2. Be kind to your family
  3. Go to church
  4. Pray

  1. You feel completely peaceful
  2. You start counting who noticed
  3. You forget about yourself entirely
  4. You feel closer to God

  2. You should perform for crowds
  3. You serve for God, who already approves of you
  4. You should always serve alone

  2. To discover the joy of serving without needing praise
  3. Because kindness is embarrassing
  4. So no one will copy you

Week 3 · Secure Identity as a Prerequisite for Service

Wednesday – Ordinary People, God-Sized Purpose

"Everybody can be great, because everybody can serve. You don't have to have a college degree to serve. You don't have to make your subject and your verb agree to serve. You only need a heart full of grace. A soul generated by love." — Dr. Martin Luther King Jr., "The Drum Major Instinct" sermon, February 4, 1968
Bible (ESV): "For we are his workmanship, created in Christ Jesus for good works, which God prepared beforehand, that we should walk in them." — Ephesians 2:10

Dr. Martin Luther King Jr. once preached that everybody can be great, because everybody can serve. You do not need a college degree. You do not need perfect grammar. You do not need money or fame. You only need "a heart full of grace" and "a soul generated by love." That is wonderful news, because it means no one is disqualified from serving — not you, not anyone.

Many people quietly believe that serving is for special people — pastors, missionaries, heroes. The rest of us feel too ordinary. Too young. Too old. Too broken. Too busy. But the Bible tells a different story: "For we are his workmanship, created in Christ Jesus for good works, which God prepared beforehand, that we should walk in them." The word "workmanship" means a work of art, shaped by hand. You are God's handmade creation. And before you were even born, God prepared good works for you to do — works that fit exactly who He made you to be.

Notice the order of things. God did not say, "Do great things, and then I will love you." He said, "I made you, I love you, and I already prepared a place for you to serve." Your identity comes first. Your service flows out of it like a river flows from a spring.

When you truly believe this, comparison loses its power over you. You stop measuring yourself against the person with the bigger gift or the louder voice. A grandmother praying quietly for her grandchildren is doing a prepared good work. A teenager mowing an elderly neighbor's lawn is doing a prepared good work. A friend who simply shows up and listens is doing a prepared good work.

You do not have to be impressive. You just have to be willing. God already made you enough — now go and be His hands in a world that needs them.

Prayer:

Creator God, thank You for shaping me with Your own hands — Your handiwork, made on purpose and for a purpose. When I scroll through my phone and feel too ordinary next to everyone else's gifts, remind me that You prepared good works for me before I was even born. Take away my habit of comparing myself to the person with the louder voice or the bigger gift. Give me a heart that serves with grace and a soul that overflows with love, and open my eyes to simple ways I can serve today — a prayer for my family, a hand for my neighbor. My real, everyday life is exactly where You want to use me. In Jesus' name, amen.

  1. Something cheap and careless
  2. A work of art, shaped by hand
  3. A job you do with tools
  4. Something you buy in a store

  1. After you proved yourself
  2. Before you were even born
  3. Only after you became perfect
  4. When you turned eighteen

  1. Prayer

  4. Going to church

  3. Simple, everyday acts like praying for family or helping a neighbor
  4. Only things that make you famous

Week 3 · Secure Identity as a Prerequisite for Service

Thursday – Small Things, Great Love

"Don't look for big things, just do small things with great love." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): "Whatever you do, work heartily, as for the Lord and not for men, knowing that from the Lord you will receive the inheritance as your reward. You are serving the Lord Christ." — Colossians 3:23-24

Mother Teresa spent her life caring for the poorest people in the streets of Calcutta, India. She washed wounds. She held the hands of dying strangers. She did work that most people would never notice and never praise. Her advice was simple: "Don't look for big things, just do small things with great love."

That is a hard teaching, because our world worships big things. Big stages. Big crowds. Big numbers. When we serve, part of us wants it to be big enough to matter. But Mother Teresa is telling us that the size of the act is not what makes it great — the size of the love behind it is. A small act done with great love can change a person's whole day, and sometimes their whole life.

The Bible says the same thing: "Whatever you do, work heartily, as for the Lord and not for men, knowing that from the Lord you will receive the inheritance as your reward. You are serving the Lord Christ." Did you catch that last part? When you serve your neighbor, you are actually serving Christ Himself. Jesus said that when we feed the hungry, welcome the stranger, or visit the lonely, it is as if we did it for Him.

Think about what this means for the small, unseen jobs. Setting out chairs before church. Brewing the coffee. Wiping down tables. Calling a friend who is hurting. None of these will ever make the news. But done with great love, each one is done for Jesus Himself.

A secure heart does not need credit. It does not need a thank-you card or a round of applause, because it is already full. And a full heart can pour out freely. This week, do one small thing with great love — and do it only for God.

Prayer:

Jesus, thank You for seeing the small things I do when no one else does. Forgive me for chasing big moments instead of loving well in small ones. Teach me Mother Teresa's secret: that a tiny act done with great love is never tiny to You. When I brew the coffee, set out the chairs, or wipe down the tables, remind me that I am serving You — that when I feed the hungry or sit with the lonely, it is as if I did it for You. Fill my ordinary tasks with Your extraordinary love, and let my quiet faithfulness bring You joy. In Your holy name, amen.

Week 3 · Secure Identity as a Prerequisite for Service

Friday – Secure Enough to Kneel

"He who loves his dream of a community more than the Christian community itself becomes a destroyer of the latter, even though his personal intentions may be ever so honest and earnest and sacrificial." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer, Life Together
Bible (ESV): "Jesus, knowing that the Father had given all things into his hands, and that he had come from God and was going back to God, rose from supper. He laid aside his outer garments, and taking a towel, tied it around his waist. Then he poured water into a basin and began to wash the disciples' feet and to wipe them with the towel that was wrapped around him." — John 13:3-5

On the night before He died, Jesus did something shocking. The Bible says, "Jesus, knowing that the Father had given all things into his hands, and that he had come from God and was going back to God, rose from supper. He laid aside his outer garments, and taking a towel, tied it around his waist. Then he poured water into a basin and began to wash the disciples' feet and to wipe them with the towel that was wrapped around him."

Look closely at the order of events. Because Jesus knew exactly who He was — where He came from and where He was going — He could kneel down and do the lowest job in the room. Washing feet was a servant's task, the job nobody wanted. But Jesus was not threatened by it, because His identity was completely settled. Only a person who is fully secure can take the lowest place without feeling low.

Insecure people cannot do this. They need the best seat, the loudest praise, the most important title. They may serve, but only if it makes them look good. Deep down, their service is really about them, not about the people they claim to help.

Dietrich Bonhoeffer warned about this danger in his book Life Together. He wrote that if you love your dream of a community more than the real Christian community itself, you will end up destroying it — even with the best intentions. In other words, if you love your perfect picture of people more than the actual, messy people in front of you, you will hurt them. Real service means loving real people — with their flaws, their slowness, and their differences — the way Jesus loved His disciples, even the one who would betray Him that very night.

This is the beating heart of servant leadership. Know who you are in God. Then kneel. Then love the actual people God placed in front of you. That is how Jesus served, and that is how He calls us to serve too.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, You knew exactly who You were — where You came from and where You were going — and still You knelt to wash dirty feet. Give me that same deep security. When I am tempted to chase titles or to dream of perfect people instead of loving real ones, bring me back to the towel and the basin. Help me love the actual people in my life — the relative who gets on my nerves, the friend who let me down — the way You loved Your disciples, even the one who betrayed You that night. Make my heart so sure of Your love that I can take the lowest place with joy. In Your name, amen.

Study questions

1What did Jesus know about Himself before He washed the disciples' feet (John 13:3)?

  1. He was confused about who He was
  2. That the Father had given all things into His hands, and He had come from God and was going back to God
  3. He needed the disciples' approval
  4. Washing feet would make Him famous

2Why could Jesus take the lowest place without feeling low?

  1. He didn't understand what He was doing
  2. His identity was completely secure
  3. Nobody was watching Him
  4. He wanted to be praised for His humility

3What did Bonhoeffer warn about in Life Together?

  1. Loving your dream of community more than real people destroys community
  2. Community is not important
  3. You should only love perfect people
  4. Serving others is a waste of time

4What do insecure people need when they serve?

  1. The lowest place
  2. Nothing at all
  3. The best seat, the loudest praise, the most important title
  4. To be left completely alone

5How did Jesus love His disciples at the table?

  1. Only the ones who deserved it
  2. He loved real, flawed people — even the one who would betray Him
  3. From a safe distance
  4. Only when they thanked Him
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay Question: In 5–8 sentences, describe a time when you served someone and no one noticed — or imagine what that would feel like. How would knowing that God saw you change the way it felt? What would change in your daily life if you truly believed your worth was already settled in Christ?

Looking back

This week you learned the secret underneath all serving: you are loved before you ever lift a finger. A secure leader gives kindness without keeping score, trusts ordinary days with God-sized purpose, and does small things with great love. Secure enough to kneel — that is the goal. Next week, you will see how Jesus turned the whole world's idea of leadership upside down.

Week 4 · The Cultural Context of Ancient Leadership

Monday – Power That Rules from Above

Week opening

In Jesus' day, leaders ruled from above. They used power to get honor, and everybody played the honor game. Then Jesus said, ‘It shall not be so among you.’ This week you will step into that ancient world and feel how shocking His words were. You will learn the difference between power that grabs and strength that kneels — and you will end the week at the basin, watching Jesus pick up a towel.

"Everybody can be great... because anybody can serve." — Martin Luther King Jr. (from his sermon "The Drum Major Instinct," preached February 4, 1968)
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:42-45 — "And Jesus called them to him and said to them, 'You know that those who are considered rulers of the Gentiles lord it over them, and their great ones exercise authority over them. But it shall not be so among you. But whoever would be great among you must be your servant, and whoever would be first among you must be slave of all. For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many.'"

In the ancient world, power had a very clear shape. Kings sat on thrones high above everyone else, and ordinary people served them. The Roman emperor lived in a palace far away, and his soldiers and governors made sure everyone obeyed his orders. If you were a ruler, you gave commands, and other people did the hard work. Nobody questioned this. It was simply how life worked.

In that world, leaders did not serve the people. The people served the leaders. The greater you were, the more others carried your load, cooked your food, and bowed when you walked by. Power meant being lifted up while everyone else stayed down.

Then Jesus came, and He turned this whole picture upside down. He told His followers, "Those who are considered rulers of the Gentiles lord it over them... But it shall not be so among you." Those words — "it shall not be so among you" — changed everything. In God's kingdom, greatness does not come from sitting on top. It comes from kneeling down to help. The greatest person in the room is the one who serves everyone else.

This still speaks to us today, because the old way of thinking is still alive. We chase titles, promotions, and the chance to be in charge. We want people to notice us and thank us. But Jesus calls us to a different road.

Servant leadership begins with one simple question: "How can I help?" At home, it looks like doing the dishes without being asked. At work, it looks like helping a coworker finish a hard job. At church, it looks like greeting new faces and stacking chairs after the service. You do not need a title to serve. You just need a willing heart. That is where true greatness begins.

Prayer:

Father in heaven, thank You for showing us what true power looks like — Jesus, who came to serve instead of to be served. Forgive us for the times we chased titles and praise instead of looking for ways to help. Teach us to lead the way Jesus led. At home, help us do the dishes without being asked. At work, help us pitch in when a coworker is buried under a hard job. Give us eyes to notice the needs around us and hands that are quick to help. May our homes, our workplaces, and our churches be filled with the spirit of service. In Jesus' name we pray. Amen.

Study questions

1In the ancient world, what did power usually look like?

  1. Leaders served the people first
  2. Leaders sat above others and were served by them
  3. Everyone shared power equally
  4. Kings washed the feet of their people

2What did Jesus say to His followers about the rulers of the Gentiles?

  1. "Copy them"
  2. "Obey them in everything"
  3. "But it shall not be so among you"
  4. "Become like them"

3According to Mark 10:43, whoever would be great must be:

  1. The boss of everyone
  2. Rich and famous
  3. Your servant
  4. A king on a throne

4Where can servant leadership be practiced?

  1. Only at church
  2. Only at work
  3. Only by pastors
  4. At home, at work, and at church

5What simple question starts servant leadership?

  1. "What will I get out of this?"
  2. "Who is in charge here?"
  3. "How can I help?"
  4. "When will someone thank me?"

Week 4 · The Cultural Context of Ancient Leadership

Tuesday – The Honor Game

"He will not be thinking about humility: he will not be thinking about himself at all." — C.S. Lewis (from Mere Christianity, in the chapter "The Great Sin")
Bible (ESV): Luke 14:11 — "For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and he who humbles himself will be exalted." Mark 12:38-39 — "And in his teaching he said, 'Beware of the scribes, who like to walk around in long robes and like greetings in the marketplaces and have the best seats in the synagogues and the places of honor at feasts.'"

In the ancient world, honor was like money. It was the most valuable thing a person could own. Leaders worked hard to collect it. They wore special robes so everyone would notice them. They loved being greeted with respect in the marketplace. At meals, they fought for the best seats, the places closest to the host. Every title, every greeting, every seat showed the world how important they were.

Jesus watched all of this, and He warned His followers not to play the honor game. He said, "Watch out for the teachers of the law," because they loved the robes, the greetings, and the best seats. Then He gave the rule that turns honor upside down: "All those who exalt themselves will be humbled, and those who humble themselves will be exalted." If you lift yourself up, God will bring you down. But if you lower yourself to serve, God will lift you up.

This is where C.S. Lewis helps us. He said that a truly humble person is not even thinking about himself at all. He is too busy taking a real interest in other people. The proud person is always asking, "How do I look? Did they notice me? Did I get the credit?" The humble person is asking, "How are you? What do you need? How can I help?"

We still play the honor game today. We want the credit. We want our name first on the list. We want people to know how hard we worked. But a servant leader steps out of the spotlight and lets others shine. Let someone else take the best seat. Praise a coworker in front of others. Do good work and let God notice. When we stop chasing honor, we find something better: the quiet joy of lifting others up.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, You watched the leaders of Your day chase honor — the robes, the greetings, the best seats — and You showed us a better way. Forgive us for playing the honor game: for wanting our name first on the list, for bringing up our hard work in meetings so people will notice. Teach us the quiet joy of stepping out of the spotlight. Help us let someone else take the best seat, and praise a coworker in front of others without needing any of it back. When we are tempted to lift ourselves up, remind us of Your promise: You lift up the humble. We want to look like You. In Your name we pray. Amen.

Study questions

1In the ancient world, what was honor like?

  1. Nobody cared about it
  2. It was the most valuable thing a person could own, like money
  3. Only kings were allowed to have it
  4. It was given only to servants

2What did the teachers of the law love, according to Mark 12:38-39?

  1. Washing feet and serving quietly
  2. Giving away all their money
  3. Flowing robes, respectful greetings, and the best seats
  4. Sitting in the lowest places

3What happens to those who exalt themselves, according to Luke 14:11?

  1. They will be praised forever
  2. They will become kings
  3. They will be humbled
  4. They will be given more honor

4According to C.S. Lewis, what is a truly humble person thinking about?

  1. Himself all the time
  2. How humble he is
  3. He is not thinking about himself at all, but taking interest in others
  4. How to get more praise

5What does a servant leader do with the spotlight?

  1. Stands in the middle of it
  2. Steps out of it and lets others shine
  3. Fights others for it
  4. Buys a bigger one

Week 4 · The Cultural Context of Ancient Leadership

Wednesday – Giving Without Strings Attached

"The church is the church only when it exists for others." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer (from Letters and Papers from Prison, written in 1944)
Bible (ESV): Matthew 6:1-4 — "'Beware of practicing your righteousness before other people in order to be seen by them, for then you will have no reward from your Father who is in heaven. Thus, when you give to the needy, sound no trumpet before you, as the hypocrites do in the synagogues and in the streets, that they may be praised by others. Truly, I say to you, they have received their reward. But when you give to the needy, do not let your left hand know what your right hand is doing, so that your giving may be in secret. And your Father who sees in secret will reward you.'"

In the ancient world, even kindness came with strings attached. Rich men called patrons gave money, food, and protection to poorer people called clients. But these gifts were really deals. The client had to pay the patron back with loyalty, public praise, and favors. If a patron helped you, everyone expected you to praise his name in the marketplace and support him in return. Giving was a way to buy honor and control.

Jesus taught a completely different way to give. He said, "When you give to the needy, sound no trumpet before you... do not let your left hand know what your right hand is doing, so that your giving may be in secret." No trumpet. No crowd. No applause. Just quiet love, given freely, with no strings attached. The only reward that matters comes from our Father in heaven, who sees in secret.

Dietrich Bonhoeffer put it this way: "The church is the church only when it exists for others." A servant leader does not help people to get something back. He helps because people matter. She gives because love gives. The question is never, "What will this do for me?" The question is always, "What does this person need?"

This is how we live it out. Help someone who can never pay you back. Encourage a person who cannot return the favor. Give your time, your money, or your attention quietly, without telling everyone about it. Do not post it. Do not brag about it. Just do it. When we give without strings, we look like Jesus — and we remind the world that love is not a deal. It is a gift.

Prayer:

Gracious Father, You give to us so freely — sunshine, breath, forgiveness, love we could never earn. Forgive us for giving with strings attached, hoping for a thank-you or a favor in return. Teach us to give the way You give: quietly and freely, with no trumpet and no crowd. Help us notice the coworker who needs a hand but can never pay us back, and meet the need without telling anyone about it. Meet it, walk away quietly, and expect nothing back — just love. Remind us that our lives exist for others, just as Jesus lived for others. May our giving bring You honor and bring hope to hurting people. In Jesus' name we pray. Amen.

Study questions

1In the ancient world, what did patrons expect in return for their gifts?

  1. Nothing at all
  2. Loyalty, public praise, and favors
  3. To be washed feet
  4. To be ignored

2According to Matthew 6:2, how should we NOT give to the needy?

  1. Quietly
  2. With a cheerful heart
  3. By announcing it with trumpets, like the hypocrites
  4. In secret

3What does "do not let your left hand know what your right hand is doing" mean?

  1. Give so quietly that you are not doing it for show
  2. Never use your left hand
  3. Only give with your right hand
  4. Tell everyone about your giving

4According to Dietrich Bonhoeffer, the church is the church only when it:

  1. Has a beautiful building
  2. Exists for others
  3. Collects the most money
  4. Has the most members

5What is the servant leader's question when helping someone?

  1. "What will this do for me?"
  2. "Will anyone notice?"
  3. "What does this person need?"
  4. "How soon will they pay me back?"

Week 4 · The Cultural Context of Ancient Leadership

Thursday – Strength That Kneels

"Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): Philippians 2:5-8 — "Have this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus, who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men. And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross."

The Roman Empire ruled the ancient world with force. Its power came from armies, swords, and fear. The emperor called himself lord, and his soldiers nailed his enemies to crosses along the roads as a warning to everyone else. In that world, strength meant crushing anyone who stood in your way. The strong took. The weak suffered. That was power.

Then came Jesus — and He was stronger than any emperor. The Bible tells us that Jesus was "in the form of God." He had all the power of heaven. But He "did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped." Instead, "he emptied himself by taking the form of a servant." The King of heaven became a servant. And then He went even lower: "he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death — even death on a cross!" The cross, Rome's cruelest weapon, became God's greatest act of love.

This is what real strength looks like. Real strength does not crush people. It carries them. Real strength does not demand to be served. It kneels down to serve. Desmond Tutu said, "Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." Jesus proved this true. His one life of service, given all the way to the cross, changed the world forever.

We can follow His example in small ways every day. Stand up for someone who is being treated unfairly. Listen to a friend who is hurting. Do the hard, hidden work that nobody thanks you for. Strength that kneels is stronger than strength that demands. When we serve with the heart of Jesus, our little bits of good join His great work — and the world begins to change.

Prayer:

Almighty God, we stand in awe of Jesus, who had all the power of heaven yet chose the path of a servant — obedient all the way to the cross. Forgive us for using our strength to push others down instead of lifting them up. Teach us what real strength looks like: strength that kneels, strength that carries, strength that loves. Give us courage to stand up for someone treated unfairly, and humility to do the hard, hidden work nobody thanks us for. Help us trust that our little bits of good, joined to Your great work, can change the world. In Jesus' mighty name we pray. Amen.

Study questions

1How did the Roman Empire show its power?

  1. By washing people's feet
  2. Through armies, swords, fear, and crosses
  3. By giving gifts with no strings attached
  4. By serving the poor

2According to Philippians 2:6, what did Jesus NOT do with His equality with God?

  1. He did not use it to His own advantage
  2. He did not share it with anyone
  3. He did not believe in it
  4. He did not talk about it

3What did Jesus take on, according to Philippians 2:7?

  1. The very nature of a king
  2. The form of a servant
  3. The very nature of an emperor
  4. The very nature of a soldier

4How far did Jesus' humility go, according to Philippians 2:8?

  1. He became a teacher for one day
  2. He became obedient to the point of death — even death on a cross
  3. He moved to a palace
  4. He stopped serving people

5What does real strength do, according to this lesson?

  1. Crushes anyone in the way
  2. Demands to be served
  3. Kneels down to serve and carries others
  4. Shows off its power

Week 4 · The Cultural Context of Ancient Leadership

Friday – The Towel and the Basin

"What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." — Nelson Mandela (spoken at the 90th birthday celebration of Walter Sisulu, May 18, 2002)
Bible (ESV): John 13:14-15 — "If I then, your Lord and Teacher, have washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another's feet. For I have given you an example, that you also should do just as I have done to you."

This week we have traveled back two thousand years to see how the ancient world understood power. Kings ruled from thrones. Leaders collected honor like treasure. Patrons gave gifts with strings attached. Empires ruled by force and fear. And into that world stepped Jesus, who turned every rule upside down: the greatest must be the servant, the humble will be lifted up, giving must be done in secret, and true strength kneels.

On the night before He died, Jesus showed us what all of this looks like in real life. He laid aside His outer garments, and taking a towel, tied it around His waist, and washed His disciples' dusty feet. Foot-washing was the lowest job in the house, the work of the lowest servant. But the Lord and Teacher did it Himself. Then He said, "I have given you an example, that you also should do just as I have done to you."

Jesus was not giving us a nice idea to think about. He was handing us a towel. Servant leadership is not a title or a technique. It is a daily choice to pick up the towel and look for feet to wash — to notice who is tired, who is hurting, who is overlooked, and to serve them with love.

Nelson Mandela said, "What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." So here is our call as this week ends: choose one act of service and do it. Visit someone lonely. Help a neighbor. Forgive someone. Encourage a child. Then do it again next week. Pick up the towel, and keep picking it up. That is how servant leaders are made — and that is how the world is changed.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, thank You for picking up the towel and showing us how to lead. You washed feet, touched the hurting, and gave Your life for us — and then You handed us the towel. We want to follow Your example. This week, show each of us one pair of feet to wash: the lonely person who needs a visit, the neighbor who needs a helping hand, the friend we need to forgive, the child who needs our time. Give us the courage to act, and fill our hearts with Your love so that serving becomes our joy, not our duty. Make us servant leaders who look like You. In Your precious name we pray. Amen.

Study questions

1What was foot-washing in the ancient world?

  1. The job of kings
  2. A great honor everyone wanted
  3. The lowest job in the house, done by the lowest servant
  4. A game people played at meals

2What did Jesus say after washing His disciples' feet?

  1. "Never do this for anyone"
  2. "I have given you an example, that you also should do just as I have done to you"
  3. "Only I can do this"
  4. "This was a one-time event"

3Which of these is NOT part of the upside-down kingdom Jesus taught this week?

  1. The greatest must be the servant
  2. The humble will be lifted up
  3. Leaders should collect honor like treasure
  4. True strength kneels to serve

4According to Nelson Mandela, what determines the significance of the life we lead?

  1. How long we live
  2. How much money we make
  3. What difference we have made to the lives of others
  4. How famous we become

5What is servant leadership, according to this lesson?

  1. A title you earn
  2. A technique for getting ahead
  3. A daily choice to pick up the towel and serve others with love
  4. Something only pastors do
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay question: Think about everything you learned this week about how Jesus turned ancient ideas of power upside down. Describe one specific way you will "pick up the towel" and serve someone in your own life this week. Who will you serve, what will you do, and how does your action reflect the servant leadership of Jesus?

Looking back

This week you saw how the world does leadership — power from above, honor chased, strings attached to every gift. And you saw Jesus flip it: whoever would be great must be a servant. Strength that kneels is stronger than power that struts. You ended at the towel and the basin. Next week, Jesus' own words in Matthew 5 will show you what a servant leader looks like from the inside out.
