021

Week 5 · The Beatitudes as Leadership Traits (Matthew 5)

Monday – Meekness: Strength Under Control

Week opening

Jesus sat down on a mountainside and described the kind of people God blesses — the meek, the merciful, the peacemakers, the pure in heart. The world calls these people weak. Jesus calls them leaders. This week you will walk through the Beatitudes one by one and find out why meekness is strength under control, why hungering for what is right changes everything, and why mercy and peacemaking are leadership superpowers.

"Darkness cannot drive out darkness; only light can do that. Hate cannot drive out hate; only love can do that." – Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Matthew 5:5 – "Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth."

When most people think of a strong leader, they picture someone loud and powerful, someone who always wins every fight. Jesus teaches something different. He says the meek are blessed. Meek does not mean weak. Meek means strong enough to control your strength. Think of a big, gentle horse. It has huge power, but it lets a small child ride on its back. That is meekness. A servant leader has power but chooses kindness instead of force.

Dr. King showed this in real life. He was arrested, beaten, and threatened, but he never fought back with hate. He answered hate with love, and that changed the world. When someone at work is rude to you, meekness means you stay calm and answer with respect. When your family is upset and loud, meekness means you do not shout back. You listen first. A meek leader does not need to prove who is the boss. They use their strength to lift others up, not push them down. This is what it looks like to follow Jesus: to be strong, gentle, and full of love, even when the world around you is not. A leader like this makes people feel safe, and safe people grow into strong people too.

Prayer:

Dear God, thank You for showing us that true strength is gentle. Today when someone cuts me off in traffic or speaks to me with a sharp tongue at work, help me breathe and answer with calm kindness instead of snapping back. When my family gets loud at the dinner table, teach me to listen first and keep my voice soft. I pray for everyone facing hate and meanness right now. Fill their hearts with Your peace. Make me a leader who uses strength to lift people up, never to push them down. Amen.

Study questions

1What does meekness mean in this lesson?

  1. Being weak and afraid
  2. Being strong enough to control your strength
  3. Always winning every fight
  4. Staying silent all the time

2According to Matthew 5:5, what happens to the meek?

  1. They will be forgotten
  2. They will always lose
  3. They will inherit the earth
  4. They will become rich

3How did Dr. King answer hate?

  1. With more hate
  2. With love
  3. By running away
  4. By ignoring everyone

4What does a meek leader do when someone is rude?

  1. Shouts back louder
  2. Answers with respect and stays calm
  3. Tells everyone about it
  4. Gets even later

5Why does meekness help others grow?

  1. It makes people afraid
  2. It shows who is in charge
  3. It makes people feel safe
  4. It keeps people quiet

Week 5 · The Beatitudes as Leadership Traits (Matthew 5)

Tuesday – Hungry for What Is Right

"Not all of us can do great things. But we can do small things with great love." – Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Matthew 5:6 – "Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be satisfied."

Think about how hungry you feel when you skip a meal. Your stomach growls and your mind cannot stop thinking about food. Jesus says we should want goodness and fairness in the world that badly. He calls it hungering and thirsting for righteousness. A servant leader does not just want to be good for their own self. They want what is right for everyone, and they want it so much that it drives them to act.

Mother Teresa fed hungry people and cared for the sick in the streets of Calcutta. She could not fix the whole world, but she never stopped doing small things with great love. Each small act came from a big hunger for what is right. You can do the same. When you see someone treated unfairly at school or work, do not look away. Stand up for them. When a neighbor needs help carrying groceries, help them. When you see a problem in your community, ask how you can help fix it. Greatness in Jesus' eyes is not about big titles or big stages. It is about a heart that cannot rest while wrong things are happening. The more you feed that hunger by doing good, the more God fills your heart with joy and purpose. Your small acts of love are food for a hungry world.

Prayer:

Heavenly Father, I come to You hungry for what is right. When I see a coworker treated unfairly or a kid at school being picked on, do not let me walk past and pretend I did not see. Give me courage to speak up with kindness and stand beside the one who is hurting. Bless the quiet helpers in my town who feed the hungry and visit the lonely. Use my small hands and my small voice today. Keep my heart restless until wrong things are made right, and fill me with joy as I serve. Amen.

Study questions

1What does it mean to hunger and thirst for righteousness?

  1. To want food and water badly
  2. To want what is right and fair for everyone
  3. To be hungry all the time
  4. To only care about yourself

2According to Mother Teresa, what can we do?

  1. Only big, famous things
  2. Nothing at all
  3. Small things with great love
  4. Things that make us famous

3What should a servant leader do when someone is treated unfairly?

  1. Look away
  2. Stand up for them
  3. Laugh about it
  4. Join in

4What fills the heart with joy in this lesson?

  1. Getting big titles
  2. Doing small acts of love
  3. Earning lots of money
  4. Being on a big stage

5According to Matthew 5:6, those who hunger for righteousness will be...

  1. Ignored
  2. Filled
  3. Left hungry
  4. Turned away

Week 5 · The Beatitudes as Leadership Traits (Matthew 5)

Wednesday – The Merciful Leader

"Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." – Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): Matthew 5:7 – "Blessed are the merciful, for they shall receive mercy."

Mercy means being kind to someone who does not deserve it, or forgiving someone who hurt you. It is easy to be kind to people who are kind to us. But a servant leader is merciful even when it is hard. Jesus says that when we show mercy, we will receive mercy too. Think of a time when you made a mistake and someone forgave you instead of punishing you. That gift changed how you felt, right? That is what mercy does. It heals hearts.

Archbishop Tutu lived through hard times in South Africa, where many people were treated badly because of their skin color. When things changed, he helped lead a way of forgiveness instead of revenge. He believed that small acts of goodness, added together, can overcome great evil. You can show mercy today. Forgive the friend who let you down. Be patient with the coworker who is slow to learn. Give food to someone who cannot pay you back. Every little bit of good counts. No act of kindness is too small. A merciful leader builds a team where people are not afraid to make mistakes, because they know they will be treated with grace. That kind of team grows stronger every day, and it changes the world one heart at a time.

Prayer:

Lord, thank You for Your mercy that never runs out. Today help me forgive the friend who broke a promise and be patient with the coworker who is slow to learn a new task. When I want to hold a grudge, remind me how many times You have forgiven me. Show me one small kindness I can do, like a meal for someone who cannot pay me back or a kind word for someone who is struggling. Let all our little bits of good join together to push back the darkness. Make me a leader who heals instead of harms. Amen.

Study questions

1What is mercy?

  1. Being kind only to nice people
  2. Being kind and forgiving even when it is hard
  3. Punishing every mistake
  4. Ignoring people in need

2What did Archbishop Tutu lead people toward?

  1. Revenge
  2. Forgiveness instead of revenge
  3. Running away
  4. Fighting back

3According to Matthew 5:7, what happens to the merciful?

  1. They are forgotten
  2. They will be shown mercy
  3. They lose everything
  4. They are punished

4What does a merciful leader build?

  1. A team afraid of mistakes
  2. A team where people are treated with grace
  3. A team that never works
  4. A team full of fear

5Why do small acts of good matter?

  1. They do not matter at all
  2. They only help rich people
  3. Added together, they can overcome great evil
  4. They make us look good

Week 5 · The Beatitudes as Leadership Traits (Matthew 5)

Thursday – The Peacemaker

"If you want to make peace with your enemy, you have to work with your enemy. Then he becomes your partner." – Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): Matthew 5:9 – "Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called sons of God."

Notice that Jesus does not say "blessed are the peace lovers" or "blessed are the peace wishers." He says peacemakers. Making peace is work. It means stepping into the middle of a fight and helping people understand each other. It is much easier to take sides or walk away. But a servant leader runs toward the hard conversations with love. Nelson Mandela spent 27 years in prison because of unfair laws. When he became free, he did not punish the people who hurt him. He invited them to work with him to build a new country. His enemies became his partners.

You can be a peacemaker too. When two friends are fighting, you can help them talk it out instead of picking a side. When your family argues at the dinner table, you can be the calm voice that says, "Let's listen to each other." At work, when people disagree, you can look for what they agree on and build from there. Peacemakers do not ignore problems. They face them with courage and kindness. Jesus calls them children of God, because that is exactly what God does. He made peace between Himself and us through love. When you make peace, you look like your Father in heaven.

Prayer:

Father, You are the God of peace, and my world needs You today. I pray for the family down the street that is always fighting, for coworkers who barely speak to each other, and for neighbors divided by anger. Start with me. Help me listen before I speak and love before I judge. When I am tempted to take sides, show me how to build a bridge instead. Turn my enemies into partners and my arguments into chances to show Your love. Use my mouth to bring calm into every room I enter today. Amen.

Study questions

1What is the difference between a peace lover and a peacemaker?

  1. There is no difference
  2. A peacemaker does the hard work of bringing people together
  3. A peace lover makes peace
  4. Peacemakers avoid all problems

2What did Nelson Mandela do after prison?

  1. Punished his enemies
  2. Invited them to work with him
  3. Left the country
  4. Stopped leading

3According to Matthew 5:9, peacemakers will be called...

  1. Children of God
  2. Weak people
  3. Troublemakers
  4. Losers

4How can you be a peacemaker at home?

  1. Pick a side in every fight
  2. Walk away from problems
  3. Help people talk and listen to each other
  4. Shout louder than everyone

5Why does making peace show we look like God?

  1. Because God avoids problems
  2. Because God made peace with us through love
  3. Because God takes sides
  4. Because God never forgives

Week 5 · The Beatitudes as Leadership Traits (Matthew 5)

Friday – Pure Hearts See Clearly

"There are three ways to ultimate success: The first way is to be kind. The second way is to be kind. The third way is to be kind." – Fred Rogers
Bible (ESV): Matthew 5:8 – "Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God."

A pure heart is a heart with no hidden tricks. It does not help people to get something back. It does not pretend to care while secretly being selfish. A pure heart loves simply and honestly. Jesus says people with pure hearts will see God. That means when your heart is clean and honest, you start to see God at work everywhere. You see Him in a child's laugh, in a stranger's kindness, and in your own life.

Fred Rogers spent his whole life speaking kindly to children on television. He never tried to be famous or rich. He just wanted every child to feel loved and special. That is what kindness from a pure heart looks like. It is not a plan to get ahead. It is love, plain and simple. A servant leader with a pure heart does not lead to get praise. They lead because they truly care about people. When your motives are clean, people trust you. They can feel the difference between someone who helps to look good and someone who helps because their heart is full of love. Ask God to clean your heart this week. Let go of jealousy, pride, and the need to be noticed. When your heart is pure, your leadership becomes a window that lets others see God.

Prayer:

Dear God, please clean my heart today. Take away the pride that wants people to notice my good deeds and the jealousy that stings when someone else gets praised. When I help at church or at work, let me do it simply because I care, not because I want thanks. I pray for everyone who feels unseen and unloved. Show them they matter to You. Open my eyes to see You working in ordinary people around me. Fill my heart so full of Your love that kindness flows out of me naturally. Amen.

Study questions

1What is a pure heart?

  1. A heart with hidden tricks
  2. A heart that loves simply and honestly
  3. A heart that never feels sad
  4. A heart that avoids people

2According to Matthew 5:8, the pure in heart will...

  1. See God
  2. Become famous
  3. Never have problems
  4. Be the richest

3Why did Fred Rogers speak kindly to children?

  1. To become famous
  2. To earn lots of money
  3. Because he wanted every child to feel loved
  4. Because he had no choice

4Why do people trust a leader with a pure heart?

  1. Because the leader is loud
  2. Because they can feel the love is real
  3. Because the leader has money
  4. Because the leader never makes mistakes

5What should we let go of to keep our hearts pure?

  1. Kindness and love
  2. Jealousy, pride, and the need to be noticed
  3. Friends and family
  4. Work and rest
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay question: Essay question: Describe a time when someone helped you with a pure heart, expecting nothing in return. How did it make you feel, and how did it change the way you treat others? Write 5-8 sentences.

Looking back

This week the Beatitudes became your leadership manual. Meekness is strength under control. Hunger for righteousness keeps a leader honest. Mercy, peacemaking, and a pure heart are not soft extras — they are the core of the job. Blessed are the servant leaders. Next week, Jesus makes it even plainer: the first shall be last.

Week 6 · The First Shall Be Last (Mark 9 & 10)

Monday – Greatness Is Service

Week opening

The disciples argued about who was the greatest. Jesus sat down, called them close, and said, ‘If anyone would be first, he must be last of all and servant of all.’ Then He took a child in His arms. This week you will sit in on that conversation. You will learn why greatness is service, why the last shall be first, and why childlike humility opens doors that pride keeps shut. And you will see the ransom — love that pays the highest price.

"Everybody can be great... because anybody can serve. You don't have to have a college degree to serve. You don't have to make your subject and your verb agree to serve. You only need a heart full of grace. A soul generated by love." – Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:43-45 – "But it shall not be so among you. But whoever would be great among you must be your servant, and whoever would be first among you must be slave of all. For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

One day, two of Jesus' disciples asked Him for the best seats in heaven. They wanted to be important. Jesus told them that in His kingdom, greatness works backwards. The way up is down. The way to be first is to be last. The way to be great is to serve. This is one of the most important lessons Jesus ever taught. He said that even He, the Son of God, came not to be served. He came to serve and to give His life for us.

Dr. King said the same thing in his own words. He told a church full of people that anybody can be great, because anybody can serve. You do not need money. You do not need a fancy degree. You do not need a big title. All you need is a heart full of grace and a soul full of love. Think about the people who have helped you most in life. They were probably not famous. They were probably people who served you quietly: a parent who worked hard, a teacher who stayed late, a friend who listened. That is greatness. A servant leader does not ask, "Who will serve me?" They ask, "Who can I serve today?" When you live this way, your life becomes great in God's eyes, even if the world never notices.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, thank You for showing us what true greatness looks like. You washed dirty feet, and You gave Your life for us. Today, when I am tempted to chase a title or a bigger name tag, remind me that greatness is found in serving. Show me the small, unseen jobs, like the dishes in the sink, the trash that needs taking out, or the neighbor who needs a ride, and help me do them with joy. I pray for everyone who feels small and unimportant today. Let them know how much they matter to You. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Jesus, how do you become great?

  1. By getting the best seats
  2. By being a servant
  3. By being the loudest
  4. By earning the most money

2What did Dr. King say you need in order to serve?

  1. A college degree
  2. Lots of money
  3. A heart full of grace and a soul generated by love
  4. A big title

3Why did Jesus come to earth, according to Mark 10:45?

  1. To be served by everyone
  2. To become a king on a throne
  3. To serve and give His life as a ransom
  4. To punish people

4Who are often the people who help us most?

  1. Famous celebrities
  2. People who serve us quietly
  3. People with the most money
  4. People who never talk

5What question should a servant leader ask each day?

  1. "Who will serve me?"
  2. "How can I look important?"
  3. "Who can I serve today?"
  4. "How can I get more?"

Week 6 · The First Shall Be Last (Mark 9 & 10)

Tuesday – The Last Shall Be First

"Kind words can be short and easy to speak, but their echoes are truly endless." – Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Mark 9:35 – "And he sat down and called the twelve. And he said to them, ‘If anyone would be first, he must be last of all and servant of all.’"

The disciples were arguing about which one of them was the greatest. They were walking with Jesus, the Son of God, and instead of learning from Him, they were fighting over first place. Jesus sat down, called them close, and said something shocking: "Anyone who wants to be first must be the very last, and the servant of all." He turned the whole idea of winning upside down. In God's kingdom, you do not climb over people to get to the top. You bend down to lift people up.

Mother Teresa understood this. She said kind words are short and easy to speak, but their echoes never end. A kind word is a small thing. It costs nothing. But it can change a person's whole day, or even their whole life. When a leader chooses to be last, they give away the spotlight so others can shine. They speak kind words to the person nobody notices. They thank the quiet worker. They encourage the struggling student. The world says, "Push yourself to the front." Jesus says, "Step to the back and serve." When you serve others first, God lifts you up in His own way and in His own time. The last become first, not by fighting for it, but by loving their way there.

Prayer:

Dear Father, I confess that I often want to be first. I want the praise, the best seat, and my name called out. Forgive me. Today, help me give away the spotlight. Let someone else go first in line, let a coworker take credit for a good idea, and speak a kind word to the person everyone overlooks. I pray for every person who feels forgotten today. Remind them that You see them. Teach me the way of Jesus, who chose the last place so He could serve us all. Make me a leader who lifts others up. Amen.

Study questions

1What were the disciples arguing about?

  1. What to eat for dinner
  2. Which one of them was the greatest
  3. Where to walk next
  4. Who was the tallest

2According to Mark 9:35, what must you do to be first?

  1. Push others down
  2. Be the very last and the servant of all
  3. Talk the loudest
  4. Never help anyone

3What did Mother Teresa say about kind words?

  1. They are a waste of time
  2. They are short and easy, but their echoes are endless
  3. They should only be said to rich people
  4. They cost a lot of money

4How does a servant leader treat the spotlight?

  1. They fight for it
  2. They give it away so others can shine
  3. They hide from everyone
  4. They sell it

5How do the last become first in God's kingdom?

  1. By fighting for first place
  2. By loving their way there through service
  3. By ignoring everyone
  4. By demanding respect

Week 6 · The First Shall Be Last (Mark 9 & 10)

Wednesday – The Ransom Paid in Love

"There is no pit so deep, that God's love is not deeper still." – Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:45 – "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

A ransom is the price paid to set someone free. Jesus said He came to give His life as a ransom for many. That means He paid the highest price possible — His own life — to set us free. He did not serve us halfway. He served us all the way to the cross. This is the deepest kind of servant leadership. It is love that costs something real.

Corrie ten Boom knew about deep pits. She and her family hid Jewish people from danger during World War II, and she was sent to a prison camp for it. She lost her sister there. But she discovered that even in the darkest place, God's love was deeper. She forgave the guards who hurt her, and she spent the rest of her life telling the world about God's love. A servant leader does not quit when serving gets hard. When helping people costs you time, comfort, or even safety, you keep going, because love is worth it. Most of us will never face what Corrie faced. But we all face small chances to pay a price for others: giving up our free time to help a friend, spending our money on someone in need, or forgiving someone who does not deserve it. Every time we choose to pay that price, we look a little more like Jesus, who paid it all.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, thank You for paying the ransom for us with Your own life. There is no pit so deep that Your love cannot reach. I pray for people who feel trapped today, in prison, in addiction, in deep sadness, or in fear. Let them feel Your love reaching down to them right where they are. Give me courage to keep serving even when it costs me something real, like my time, my comfort, or my money. Teach me to forgive when it is hard and to love when it hurts. Make my life a gift to others. Amen.

Study questions

1What is a ransom?

  1. A gift you get for free
  2. The price paid to set someone free
  3. A kind of song
  4. A place to hide

2How far did Jesus go in serving us?

  1. He stopped when it got hard
  2. He only helped rich people
  3. All the way to the cross
  4. He never really helped

3What did Corrie ten Boom discover in the prison camp?

  1. That God had forgotten her
  2. That God's love is deeper than any pit
  3. That serving is useless
  4. That she should give up

4What does a servant leader do when serving gets hard?

  1. Quits right away
  2. Keeps going, because love is worth it
  3. Blames other people
  4. Only serves when it is easy

5Which of these is a small "price" we can pay for others?

  1. Giving up free time to help a friend
  2. Keeping everything for ourselves
  3. Ignoring people in need
  4. Refusing to forgive

Week 6 · The First Shall Be Last (Mark 9 & 10)

Thursday – Childlike Humility

"The church is the church only when it exists for others... not dominating, but helping and serving." – Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:14-15 – "But when Jesus saw it, he was indignant and said to them, ‘Let the children come to me; do not hinder them, for to such belongs the kingdom of God. Truly, I say to you, whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child shall not enter it.’"

One day, people brought little children to Jesus, and the disciples tried to send them away. They thought Jesus was too important for children. But Jesus was upset with the disciples. He said the kingdom of God belongs to people like these little ones. Children do not pretend to be important. They do not care about titles or status. They come with open hands and trusting hearts. Jesus says that is how we must come to God, and that is how a leader must serve.

Dietrich Bonhoeffer said the church only matters when it exists for others, not for itself. He meant that a group of people is not great because of its big buildings or famous leaders. It is great when it helps and serves. The same is true for any leader. You are not great because of your position. You are great when you help others. Think like a child: do not worry about looking important. Just be honest, be kind, and be ready to share. A childlike leader asks simple questions, admits when they are wrong, and celebrates when others succeed. They do not try to control everyone. They serve everyone. When you lead with the humble heart of a child, people feel loved instead of used, and your leadership points them straight to Jesus.

Prayer:

Heavenly Father, make me like a little child in my heart. Take away my need to look important and my fear of looking foolish. I pray for all the children of the world, the hungry ones, the scared ones, the ones who feel alone. Send kind people into their lives and protect them. Teach me to welcome everyone the way Jesus welcomed the children, with open arms and no rushing. Help our churches and our teams exist for others, not for ourselves. Give me a humble heart that serves with joy. Amen.

Study questions

1Why did Jesus welcome the little children?

  1. Because they were rich
  2. Because to such belongs the kingdom of God
  3. Because they were quiet
  4. Because they were famous

2What is special about how children come to Jesus?

  1. They bring lots of money
  2. They come with open hands and trusting hearts
  3. They demand attention
  4. They try to look important

3According to Bonhoeffer, when does the church truly matter?

  1. When it has big buildings
  2. When its leaders are famous
  3. When it exists for others, helping and serving
  4. When it stays closed

4What does a childlike leader do?

  1. Tries to control everyone
  2. Admits when wrong and celebrates others' success
  3. Brags about their title
  4. Ignores people

5How do people feel when led with a humble heart?

  1. Used
  2. Loved
  3. Afraid
  4. Angry

Week 6 · The First Shall Be Last (Mark 9 & 10)

Friday – Strength to Forgive

"The weak can never forgive. Forgiveness is the attribute of the strong." – Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:27 – "Jesus looked at them and said, ‘With man it is impossible, but not with God. For all things are possible with God.’"

Forgiving someone who hurt you is one of the hardest things in the world. Many people think forgiving means you are weak, like you are letting the other person win. Gandhi said the opposite. He said only the strong can forgive. It takes real strength to let go of anger and choose peace instead. Jesus agreed. When His disciples wondered how anyone could be saved, He told them that with God, all things are possible — including forgiving the unforgivable.

A servant leader must be a forgiving leader. If you hold on to every hurt, your heart gets heavy, and you cannot lead with love. Think of a leader who refuses to forgive. Their team walks on eggshells, afraid of making mistakes. Now think of a leader who forgives. Their team feels safe to try, to learn, and to grow. That is the kind of leader Jesus was. On the cross, He forgave the very people who hurt Him. You can start small. Forgive the driver who cut you off. Forgive the friend who forgot your birthday. Forgive the family member who said something mean. Each time you forgive, you grow stronger, not weaker. And with God's help, even the hardest forgiveness becomes possible. A forgiving leader sets people free — including themselves.

Prayer:

God, I admit that forgiving is hard for me. I hold on to hurt because letting go feels like losing. But You tell me that with You, all things are possible. Today, help me release the anger I carry toward the person who wronged me, at work, in my family, or long ago. I pray for everyone carrying the heavy weight of unforgiveness. Set them free, and set me free too. Teach me that forgiveness is not weakness. It is the greatest strength of all. Make me strong enough to forgive like Jesus forgave. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Gandhi, who can forgive?

  1. Only the weak
  2. Only the strong
  3. Only rich people
  4. Nobody at all

2What did Jesus say in Mark 10:27?

  1. Some things are too hard even for God
  2. With God, all things are possible
  3. Forgiveness is impossible
  4. We should never try

3What happens to a leader who refuses to forgive?

  1. Their heart stays light
  2. Their heart gets heavy and they cannot lead with love
  3. Everyone trusts them more
  4. Nothing changes

4How does a forgiving leader make their team feel?

  1. Afraid to try
  2. Safe to learn and grow
  3. Angry all the time
  4. Unimportant

5What did Jesus do on the cross?

  1. He cursed His enemies
  2. He forgave the people who hurt Him
  3. He ran away
  4. He gave up on everyone
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay question: Essay question: Think of someone you need to forgive, or a time you were forgiven. Why is forgiveness an act of strength instead of weakness? Write 5-8 sentences.

Looking back

This week you heard it straight from Jesus: greatness is service, and the last shall be first. You learned that childlike humility is not thinking less of yourself but thinking of yourself less, that love pays the ransom price willingly, and that strength shows up as forgiveness. The world's ladder is upside down in God's kingdom. Next week, you will learn what it means to be a faithful steward of everything God puts in your hands.

Week 7 · The Parable of the Faithful Steward

Monday – Faithful With Little

Week opening

A steward does not own the house — he takes care of it for the master. Jesus told a story about a faithful manager, and this week you will live inside that story. You will learn why faithfulness with little leads to trust with much, why much given means much required, and why we work for God and not for people's applause. Your gifts were never meant to sit on a shelf. They were meant to be given.

"Life's most persistent and urgent question is, 'What are you doing for others?'" – Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Luke 12:42-43 – "And the Lord said, ‘Who then is the faithful and wise manager, whom his master will set over his household, to give them their portion of food at the proper time? Blessed is that servant whom his master will find so doing when he comes.’"

Jesus told a story about a master who put one servant in charge of the whole house while he was away. The faithful servant did his job well, feeding everyone on time and taking care of things. Jesus said this servant would be blessed when the master came home. A steward is someone who takes care of something that belongs to someone else. We are all stewards. Our time, our money, our talents, and even our families are gifts from God, and He trusts us to take care of them.

Dr. King said the most urgent question in life is, "What are you doing for others?" That is the steward's question. A faithful steward does not wait for a big job to start serving. They are faithful with little things first. Do you take care of the small tasks at work, even when no one is watching? Do you keep your promises to your family? Do you use your money wisely? Do you treat your own body and health with care? A servant leader is faithful in the small, hidden jobs. That is how God knows He can trust them with bigger ones. Today, look at the little things in your life. Do them well. Feed the people God put in your care. The Master is watching, and He will be pleased.

Prayer:

Lord, everything I have belongs to You, my time, my money, my gifts, my family. They are on loan from Your hand. Today, help me do the small jobs well, even when no one is watching: the report done right, the kitchen cleaned, the promise kept. I pray for everyone who is hungry, waiting for someone to feed them on time. Make me faithful, someone who shows up and does what I said I would do. Teach me to ask every day, "What am I doing for others?" May You find me doing Your work with joy. Amen.

Study questions

1What is a steward?

  1. The owner of everything
  2. Someone who takes care of something that belongs to someone else
  3. A person who never works
  4. A king on a throne

2What does the faithful servant do in the story?

  1. Sleeps all day
  2. Feeds everyone on time and takes care of things
  3. Runs away
  4. Steals the master's money

3What is Dr. King's most urgent question?

  1. "How much money do you have?"
  2. "What are you doing for others?"
  3. "Who is the greatest?"
  4. "Where are you going?"

4How does a servant leader handle small tasks?

  1. Ignores them
  2. Does them well, even when no one is watching
  3. Complains about them
  4. Gives them all away

5Why should we be faithful with little things?

  1. So people will praise us
  2. So God can trust us with bigger things
  3. So we can rest more
  4. So we look busy

Week 7 · The Parable of the Faithful Steward

Tuesday – Much Is Given, Much Required

"Pride gets no pleasure out of having something, only out of having more of it than the next man." – C.S. Lewis
Bible (ESV): Luke 12:48 – "But the one who did not know, and did what deserved a beating, will receive a light beating. Everyone to whom much was given, of him much will be required, and from him to whom they entrusted much, they will demand the more."

Jesus teaches a simple but serious rule: the more you have been given, the more is expected of you. If you have been given knowledge, you are expected to share it. If you have been given money, you are expected to be generous. If you have been given a position of leadership, you are expected to serve the people under your care. This is not a burden. It is an honor. God trusts you with much because He believes in you.

C.S. Lewis warned us about pride. He said pride is not about enjoying what you have. It is about enjoying having more than the next person. Pride turns blessings into weapons. A proud leader uses their gifts to feel better than others. A faithful steward uses their gifts to lift others up. Ask yourself: What has God given me? Maybe it is a good job, a strong body, a kind family, or a talent for music, cooking, or listening. Whatever it is, it is not just for you. It is a trust. Use it to feed people, the way the faithful servant fed the household. Do not compare yourself to others or brag about what you have. Instead, ask, "How can I use what God gave me to help someone today?" That is what a great steward does.

Prayer:

Father, thank You for every good gift You have given me, a home, work, health, people who love me. I know that much is expected from those who have received much. Today, show me how to share what I have: an extra meal for a hungry neighbor, money for someone who has very little, time for someone who is lonely. Take away my pride and my need to have more than others. Help me see my gifts as a trust from You, not trophies for myself. Show me how to lift others up today. Amen.

Study questions

1What does Jesus teach about being given much?

  1. You should keep it all for yourself
  2. Much will be required of you
  3. Nothing is expected of you
  4. You should hide it

2What did C.S. Lewis say about pride?

  1. Pride is enjoying what you have
  2. Pride is enjoying having more than the next person
  3. Pride is a good thing
  4. Pride does not exist

3How does a faithful steward use their gifts?

  1. To feel better than others
  2. To lift others up
  3. To brag on social media
  4. To hide them away

4Which of these is a gift God may have entrusted to you?

  1. A talent for listening
  2. Nothing at all
  3. Only money counts
  4. Gifts do not matter

5What question should a steward ask each day?

  1. "How can I get more?"
  2. "How can I use what God gave me to help someone today?"
  3. "Why do others have more?"
  4. "How can I look better?"

Week 7 · The Parable of the Faithful Steward

Wednesday – Working for God, Not People

"Often when you think you're at the end of something, you're at the beginning of something else." – Fred Rogers
Bible (ESV): Colossians 3:23-24 – "Whatever you do, work heartily, as for the Lord and not for men, knowing that from the Lord you will receive the inheritance as your reward. You are serving the Lord Christ."

Have you ever worked hard at something and nobody noticed? Maybe you cleaned the house and no one said thank you. Maybe you did your best at work and the boss gave the credit to someone else. It hurts. It is tempting to stop trying. But the Bible gives us a secret that changes everything: we are not really working for people. We are working for God. When you wash the dishes, you are serving Christ. When you do your job with care, you are serving Christ. When you help your neighbor, you are serving Christ.

Fred Rogers reminded us that endings are often beginnings in disguise. When your hard work goes unnoticed, that is not the end of the story. God sees it, and He is writing a bigger story with your faithfulness. A servant leader does not work for applause. They work for God. That means they do their best even on the boring days, even on the hard days, even when no one is watching. People will notice the difference. They will see someone who is steady, honest, and full of joy, and they will want to know why. Your quiet faithfulness is a sermon without words. Keep going. God is keeping score, and His reward is better than any praise from people.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, thank You for seeing the work that no one else sees. I pray for every tired worker today, the janitors mopping floors late at night, the cooks, the caregivers, the parents up with a crying baby. Strengthen their hands and their hearts, and remind them their work matters to You. Help me to work with all my heart, not for applause, but for You. When I feel unnoticed, fill me with Your joy. Let my quiet faithfulness be a light that points others to You. Amen.

Study questions

1Who are we really working for, according to Colossians 3:23?

  1. Our boss only
  2. The Lord, not human masters
  3. Ourselves alone
  4. Nobody

2What should we do when our hard work goes unnoticed?

  1. Stop trying
  2. Complain to everyone
  3. Keep going, because God sees it
  4. Only work when praised

3What did Fred Rogers say about endings?

  1. Endings are always bad
  2. They are often beginnings in disguise
  3. They mean you failed
  4. They never come

4How does a servant leader work?

  1. Only for applause
  2. Their best, even when no one is watching
  3. As little as possible
  4. Only on easy days

5What is "a sermon without words"?

  1. Staying silent forever
  2. Quiet faithfulness that points others to God
  3. Refusing to talk about faith
  4. Sleeping in church

Week 7 · The Parable of the Faithful Steward

Thursday – Ready When the Master Returns

"Silence in the face of evil is itself evil: God will not hold us guiltless. Not to speak is to speak. Not to act is to act." – Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): Luke 12:43 – "Blessed is that servant whom his master will find so doing when he comes."

In Jesus' story, the master goes away for a while, and the servant does not know exactly when he will return. The faithful servant keeps working anyway. He does not take a break from doing good. He does not say, "The master is gone, so I can do whatever I want." He stays ready. Jesus told this story to teach us how to live every day. We do not know the future, but we know our Master. So we keep serving, keep loving, and keep doing what is right.

Dietrich Bonhoeffer lived this out in a terrible time. When evil was rising in his country, many people stayed silent because speaking up was dangerous. Bonhoeffer refused to be silent. He said that silence in the face of evil is evil itself. He stood up for what was right, even though it cost him his life. A servant leader stays ready by staying faithful. They do not wait for the perfect moment to do good. They do good now. They speak up when someone is being hurt. They act when they see a need. They do not let fear or laziness make them silent. Imagine if the Master returned today. Would He find you serving? That is the question that keeps a steward's heart awake and ready.

Prayer:

God, my Master, I want to be ready for You. Do not let me stay silent when I see someone treated badly at work or a neighbor in need. Wake up my heart every morning with a desire to serve. Help me do good now, not later. Make the call, write the note, lend the hand today. I pray for people suffering in silence, waiting for someone to speak up for them. Give me courage like Bonhoeffer had. When You return, Lord, may You find me feeding Your people and loving my neighbors. Amen.

  4. Falls asleep

  3. He ran away from trouble
  4. He ignored the evil around him

4What keeps a steward's heart awake and ready?

  1. Waiting for the perfect moment
  2. Doing good now, not later
  3. Sleeping more
  4. Avoiding hard things

5According to Luke 12:43, who will be blessed?

  1. The servant the master finds sleeping
  2. The servant the master finds doing his work when he returns
  3. The servant who hides
  4. The servant who quits

Week 7 · The Parable of the Faithful Steward

Friday – Gifts Meant to Be Given

"Yesterday is gone. Tomorrow has not yet come. We have only today. Let us begin." – Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): 1 Peter 4:10 – "As each has received a gift, use it to serve one another, as good stewards of God’s varied grace."

God has given every person at least one gift. Maybe you are good at cooking. Maybe you are a good listener. Maybe you can fix things, or sing, or make people laugh, or organize a messy room. These gifts are not accidents. The Bible says they are God's grace in different forms, and we are stewards of them. A gift that is never used is like a lamp that is never lit. It cannot help anyone in the dark.

Mother Teresa said we only have today, so let us begin. Do not wait until you feel ready. Do not wait until you have more time or more money. Start now, with what you have, where you are. A servant leader looks at their gifts and asks, "Who needs this today?" The cook makes a meal for a sick neighbor. The listener sits with a lonely friend. The fixer helps an elderly person with a broken door. The singer brings joy to a nursing home. Every gift becomes greater when it is given away. That is the beautiful secret of stewardship: the more you give, the more you have. Your gifts were never meant to sit on a shelf. They were meant to be placed in the hands of people who need them. Begin today.

Prayer:

Generous God, thank You for the gifts You placed in each of us. Today, help me stop waiting for tomorrow and start serving today, with my cooking, my listening ear, my strong back, my cheerful words. I pray for everyone who feels like they have nothing to offer. Show them the beautiful gifts You hid inside them. Teach me to see my talents as Your grace to share, not my own to keep. Bless every person who uses their gifts to feed the hungry, comfort the lonely, and lift up the hurting. Amen.

Study questions

1What does 1 Peter 4:10 say we should do with our gifts?

  1. Hide them away
  2. Use them to serve others
  3. Sell them for money
  4. Keep them secret

2What is a gift that is never used like?

  1. A lamp that is never lit
  2. A treasure in a bank
  3. A perfect plan
  4. A loud song

3What did Mother Teresa say about today?

  1. Wait until tomorrow
  2. Yesterday is gone, tomorrow has not come, we have only today — let us begin
  3. Do nothing today
  4. Today does not matter

4What happens when gifts are given away?

  1. They disappear forever
  2. They become greater — the more you give, the more you have
  3. They lose their value
  4. Nothing happens

5What should a servant leader ask about their gifts?

  1. "How can I show off?"
  2. "Who needs this today?"
  3. "How much is this worth?"
  4. "Can I keep this forever?"
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay question: Essay question: What is one gift or talent God has given you? Write 5-8 sentences about how you could use it to serve someone this week, and what might stop you from starting today.

Looking back

This week you learned the steward's mindset: everything you have is on loan from God. Faithful with little, trusted with much. Work for God, not for people. Stay ready, because the Master is returning. And remember — your gifts were meant to be given, not kept. Next week, you will follow the Shepherd who leaves the ninety-nine to find the one.

Week 8 · Seeking the Lost vs. Seeking Status

Monday – The Shepherd Who Searches

Week opening

Religious leaders in Jesus' day chased status. Jesus chased lost sheep. This week you will walk the hills with the Shepherd who leaves the ninety-nine to find the one. You will learn why status will never save you, why ‘the least of these’ matter most to God, and why leaving what is comfortable to find one hurting person is the heart of servant leadership. If you cannot feed a hundred, feed one.

"What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." – Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): Luke 15:4 – "What man of you, having a hundred sheep, if he has lost one of them, does not leave the ninety-nine in the open country, and go after the one that is lost, until he finds it?"

Jesus told a story about a shepherd with one hundred sheep. When one sheep wandered off and got lost, the shepherd did something surprising. He left the ninety-nine safe sheep and went searching for the one that was lost. He did not say, "Oh well, I still have ninety-nine." He went after the one until he found it. That is how God loves us. No person is too small, too far gone, or too unimportant to be searched for.

Nelson Mandela said that what counts in life is not just that we lived, but what difference we made in the lives of others. A servant leader thinks like the shepherd. They do not chase after important people to make themselves look good. They go after the lost ones: the lonely kid at school, the coworker everyone avoids, the neighbor who stopped coming to church, the family member who feels forgotten. Seeking status means asking, "Who can help me look important?" Seeking the lost means asking, "Who needs me most?" The shepherd's joy was not in keeping his ninety-nine safe. His joy was in finding the one. Your life becomes significant when you spend it searching for the lost and bringing them home.

Prayer:

Lord, my Shepherd, thank You for searching for me when I was lost. Today, give me a shepherd's heart. Show me the one who needs finding, the lonely coworker eating lunch alone, the teenager drifting from family, the neighbor nobody visits. Help me leave my comfort and go to them with kindness, not judgment. I pray for every lost sheep in the world, the addicted, the runaway, the forgotten. Send someone to search for them. Let my life count by the difference I make, and bring the lost ones home. Amen.

Study questions

1What did the shepherd do when one sheep was lost?

  1. Said, "Oh well, I still have ninety-nine"
  2. Left the ninety-nine and went after the lost one until he found it
  3. Bought a new sheep
  4. Gave up shepherding

2What does seeking the lost mean?

  1. Chasing important people
  2. Asking, "Who needs me most?"
  3. Trying to look important
  4. Staying comfortable

3What did Mandela say counts in life?

  1. How long you live
  2. What difference you made in the lives of others
  3. How much money you earn
  4. How famous you become

4Who are the "lost ones" we can search for?

  1. Only rich people
  2. The lonely, the avoided, the forgotten
  3. Nobody needs us
  4. Only people like us

5Where did the shepherd find his joy?

  1. In keeping the ninety-nine safe
  2. In finding the one lost sheep
  3. In counting his money
  4. In resting all day

Week 8 · Seeking the Lost vs. Seeking Status

Tuesday – The Least of These

"I have decided to stick with love. Hate is too great a burden to bear." – Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Matthew 25:40 – "And the King will answer them, ‘Truly, I say to you, as you did it to one of the least of these my brothers, you did it to me.’"

Jesus said something amazing: when we help "the least of these," we are helping Him. Who are the least? They are the hungry, the thirsty, the stranger, the person without clothes, the sick, and the prisoner. They are the people the world overlooks. They are the ones with no status and no power. Jesus says that how we treat them is how we treat Him. That changes everything. Feeding a hungry stranger is not just a nice thing to do. It is serving Jesus Himself.

Dr. King decided to stick with love, even though hate would have been easier. Hate is a heavy burden. It poisons the person who carries it. Love sets us free to serve. A leader who seeks status spends time with important people who can do something for them. A servant leader spends time with "the least of these" who can do nothing for them in return. They visit the sick. They welcome the stranger. They feed the hungry. They do it with love, because they know they are serving Jesus. Look around your life this week. Who is the "least" near you? Who is hungry, lonely, or forgotten? Go to them with love. When you do, you are not just helping a person. You are meeting Jesus face to face.

Prayer:

King Jesus, You told us that when we serve the least, we serve You. Today, open my eyes to see You in the hungry man on the corner, the new stranger at church, the sick neighbor, the lonely widow. Take the heavy burden of hate out of my heart and fill it with love instead. I pray for every person who feels like "the least," unseen, unloved, forgotten. Let them know they are precious to You. Help me choose love every single day, and serve the least of these as if I were serving You. Amen.

Study questions

1Who are "the least of these"?

  1. The rich and famous
  2. The hungry, the stranger, the sick, the lonely
  3. Only kings and queens
  4. Nobody in particular

2According to Matthew 25:40, when we help the least, we are helping...

  1. Ourselves only
  2. Jesus Himself
  3. Nobody
  4. Only our friends

3Why did Dr. King stick with love?

  1. Because hate is easy
  2. Because hate is too great a burden to bear
  3. Because he was afraid
  4. Because love is weak

4Who does a status-seeking leader spend time with?

  1. People who can do something for them
  2. The lonely and forgotten
  3. The sick and hungry
  4. Strangers in need

5How can you meet Jesus face to face this week?

  1. By serving the least of these with love
  2. By ignoring people in need
  3. By only helping rich people
  4. By staying home alone

Week 8 · Seeking the Lost vs. Seeking Status

Wednesday – Status Will Not Save You

"Injustice anywhere is a threat to justice everywhere." – Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Luke 19:10 – "For the Son of Man came to seek and to save the lost."

In Jesus' day, the religious leaders loved status. They loved the best seats, the respectful greetings, and the praise of the crowds. But they looked down on the poor, the sick, and the sinners. They thought they were close to God because they looked important. Jesus said they were wrong. He came to seek and to save the lost, not to hand out prizes to the proud. Status cannot save you. Only love can change you.

Dr. King wrote that injustice anywhere is a threat to justice everywhere. He meant that we cannot ignore suffering just because it is not happening to us. When we chase status, we start to ignore the hurting people around us. We walk past the homeless person. We avoid the difficult neighbor. We stay silent when someone is treated unfairly. But a servant leader does the opposite. They use whatever position they have to lift up the people at the bottom. They speak for those who have no voice. They remember that Jesus left heaven's glory to come find the lost. If the Son of God was willing to leave His high position to serve, surely we can let go of our small pride to help someone in need. Do not build your life on being important. Build it on seeking the lost.

Prayer:

Father, forgive me for the times I chased status instead of serving people, wanting the title, the recognition, the important seat. Today, help me let go of pride and love of being important. I pray for everyone suffering injustice, treated unfairly because of where they live, what they look like, or how much they have. Give me eyes to see their pain and courage to stand beside them. Remind me that Jesus left heaven to seek and save the lost. Make me a leader who does the same with whatever I have. Amen.

Study questions

1What did the religious leaders in Jesus' day love?

  1. Serving the poor
  2. The best seats and the praise of crowds
  3. Helping sinners
  4. Washing feet

2According to Luke 19:10, why did Jesus come?

  1. To give prizes to the proud
  2. To seek and to save the lost
  3. To become famous
  4. To ignore the poor

3What did Dr. King mean by "injustice anywhere is a threat to justice everywhere"?

  1. We should ignore suffering
  2. We cannot ignore suffering just because it is not happening to us
  3. Only some people matter
  4. Justice is impossible

4What happens when we chase status?

  1. We help more people
  2. We start to ignore hurting people
  3. We become more loving
  4. We see Jesus clearly

5How should a servant leader use their position?

  1. To get more praise
  2. To lift up the people at the bottom
  3. To avoid difficult people
  4. To stay comfortable

Week 8 · Seeking the Lost vs. Seeking Status

Thursday – Leaving the Comfortable to Find the One

"Worry does not empty tomorrow of its sorrow, it empties today of its strength." – Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Luke 15:5-6 – "And when he has found it, he lays it on his shoulders, rejoicing. And when he comes home, he calls together his friends and his neighbors, saying to them, ‘Rejoice with me, for I have found my sheep that was lost.’"

Think about what the shepherd had to do. He left ninety-nine safe sheep in the open country. That was risky. He walked into the hills, maybe in the dark, maybe in danger, to find one lost sheep. He did not sit at home worrying about what might happen tomorrow. He used today's strength to go and search. And when he found the sheep, he did not scold it. He joyfully put it on his shoulders and carried it home. Then he threw a party.

Corrie ten Boom said that worry empties today of its strength. Many of us want to help lost people, but we worry too much. We worry about what to say. We worry about being rejected. We worry about leaving our comfort zone. So we do nothing, and today passes by unused. A servant leader does not let worry steal today's strength. They get up and go. They call the friend who stopped answering. They visit the neighbor who is struggling. They invite the lonely person to dinner. It might feel risky or awkward, but love is stronger than worry. And when that lost person comes home, there is joy in heaven and joy in your heart. Do not spend today worrying. Spend it searching.

Prayer:

Dear God, I confess that I worry too much and act too little. I stay comfortable on my couch while lost people wander alone. Forgive me. Today, take my worry and turn it into action. Help me pick up the phone, knock on the door, invite the lonely neighbor for coffee. I pray for everyone who is lost today, far from home and far from You. Give me courage to leave my comfort and go find them. And when the lost are found, let my heart celebrate with all of heaven. Amen.

Study questions

1What risk did the shepherd take?

  1. None at all
  2. He left the ninety-nine to search for the one
  3. He sold all his sheep
  4. He stayed home

2How did the shepherd treat the sheep when he found it?

  1. He scolded it
  2. He joyfully carried it home on his shoulders
  3. He left it there
  4. He ignored it

3What did Corrie ten Boom say worry does?

  1. It solves tomorrow's problems
  2. It empties today of its strength
  3. It makes us stronger
  4. It helps us rest

4What stops many of us from helping lost people?

  1. Having too much courage
  2. Worrying about what to say or being rejected
  3. Knowing exactly what to do
  4. Being too busy celebrating

5What should we do instead of worrying?

  1. Stay comfortable
  2. Spend today searching for the lost
  3. Wait for someone else to act
  4. Do nothing

Week 8 · Seeking the Lost vs. Seeking Status

Friday – If You Can't Feed a Hundred, Feed One

"If you can't feed a hundred people, then feed just one." – Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Matthew 25:40 – "And the King will answer them, ‘Truly, I say to you, as you did it to one of the least of these my brothers, you did it to me.’"

Sometimes the needs of the world feel too big. There are millions of hungry people, millions of lonely people, and millions of hurting people. It is easy to feel small and give up before you even start. Mother Teresa felt that too. She worked among the poorest people in the world, and the need never ended. But she said, "If you can't feed a hundred people, then feed just one." She knew that changing the whole world starts with loving one person.

Jesus agrees. He said that whatever we do for one of the least of these, we do for Him. Notice He said "one." Not a hundred. Not a thousand. One. A servant leader does not wait until they can fix everything. They start with the one person in front of them. You cannot feed a hundred people today. But you can buy lunch for one hungry person. You cannot visit every lonely person in your city. But you can call one lonely friend. You cannot end all suffering. But you can sit with one suffering person and listen. Do not let the size of the problem stop you from doing the small thing. God takes our small acts of love and multiplies them in ways we cannot see. Feed one. Love one. Serve one. That is how the world changes.

Prayer:

Lord, the needs of the world feel so big, and I feel so small. But You tell me to start with one. Today, show me the one person You want me to love this week, the hungry coworker, the lonely senior, the tired mom. Help me stop waiting until I can do something big, and do the small thing today. I pray for the millions of hungry, lonely, hurting people across the earth. Raise up servants to feed them, one by one. Bless my small acts of kindness and multiply them for Your glory. Amen.

Study questions

1What did Mother Teresa say about feeding people?

  1. Feed only rich people
  2. If you can't feed a hundred, feed just one
  3. Never feed anyone
  4. Wait until you can feed everyone

2Why do people sometimes give up before starting?

  1. The needs of the world feel too big
  2. They have too much time
  3. Nobody needs help
  4. Helping is too easy

3According to Matthew 25:40, Jesus notices when we help...

  1. Only large crowds
  2. One of the least of these
  3. Only famous people
  4. No one at all

4What should a servant leader do instead of waiting to fix everything?

  1. Do nothing
  2. Start with the one person in front of them
  3. Complain about the problem
  4. Move far away

5What does God do with our small acts of love?

  1. Ignores them
  2. Multiplies them in ways we cannot see
  3. Throws them away
  4. Laughs at them
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Looking back

This week you chose the Shepherd's path over the status game. You learned that God measures a life by the difference it makes, that the least of these are the greatest to Him, and that one person found is worth leaving the comfortable behind. Feed one. Find one. Love one. Next week, you will go where it hurts — to the untouchables Jesus touched.

Week 9 · Touching the Untouchable (Leper Cleansing)

Monday – Going Where It Hurts

Week opening

Nobody touched a leper. It was the rule. Then Jesus, moved with pity, stretched out His hand and touched him. This week you will learn what compassion really costs: it asks us to go where it hurts and enter into places of pain. You will put down your stones of judgment, roll up your sleeves, and discover that thankful hearts grow in healed lives. We can love because we were first loved.

"Compassion asks us to go where it hurts, to enter into the places of pain, to share in brokenness, fear, confusion, and anguish." — Henri Nouwen
Bible (ESV): Mark 1:41 — "Moved with pity, he stretched out his hand and touched him and said to him, 'I will; be clean.'"

In Jesus' day, a man with leprosy was not just sick. He was cast out. He could not live with his family. He could not worship with his friends. He had to shout "unclean" so people would stay away from him. He was the one everyone avoided. Then Jesus did what no one else would do. Jesus stretched out his hand and touched him. That touch is the heart of this whole story. Jesus did not shout a command from a safe distance. He did not send a helper in his place. He touched a man that the whole town would not touch. Think about the people in your own life that others step around. Maybe it is the neighbor whose yard is a mess because he is too old to care for it. Maybe it is the coworker who smells different, or the stranger on the bus who talks to himself, or the church volunteer who cries during every service and makes people uncomfortable. Compassion does not watch pain from the sidewalk. It walks across the street. It sits down next to the hurt. Going where it hurts does not mean you have to fix everything. You may not be able to heal anyone the way Jesus did. But you can do what Jesus did first. You can get close. You can offer your time, your listening ear, your kind words, your simple presence. That is what true servant leadership looks like. It leaves the comfortable spot and moves toward the hurting person. When we do that, we show people that their pain matters to us, and we show the world a picture of Jesus himself.

Prayer:

Lord, thank You for not staying far away when we were hurting. You came close and touched us with Your love. Today, give me the courage to go where it hurts, to sit beside the grieving widow, to visit the sick neighbor in the hospital, to listen to the coworker going through a divorce. Help me enter into the pain of others instead of running from it, even when it is messy. Use my hands and my time to bring Your comfort. Teach me to love the outcast, the lonely, and the sick the way You do. Amen.

Study questions

1In Jesus' time, what did a man with leprosy have to do when he walked near others?

  2. Shout "unclean" so people would stay away
  3. Wear a mask and gloves
  4. Pay a fine to the town leaders

2What did Jesus do first when the man with leprosy came to him?

  1. He asked the man's name
  2. He shouted for the man to go away
  3. He stretched out his hand and touched him
  4. He told the crowd to pray for the man

3In this lesson, what does "going where it hurts" mean for us today?

  1. Visiting only places that feel safe and comfortable
  2. Watching other people suffer without getting involved
  3. Getting close to hurting people instead of avoiding them
  4. Telling hurting people how to fix their own problems

4Which everyday person is an example of someone others might avoid?

  1. A stranger on the bus who talks to himself
  2. A friendly store clerk who greets everyone
  3. A neighbor who brings you fresh cookies
  4. A coworker who always helps on projects

5What is the main way this lesson says we can help hurting people?

  1. By giving them medical treatment like a doctor
  2. By offering our time, our listening ear, and our presence
  3. By telling them to be stronger
  4. By sending them to someone else

Week 9 · Touching the Untouchable (Leper Cleansing)

Tuesday – Putting Down Our Stones

"If you judge people, you have no time to love them." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Luke 6:37 — "Judge not, and you will not be judged; condemn not, and you will not be condemned; forgive, and you will be forgiven;"

Long before the leper met Jesus, the crowd had already judged him. They had decided he was unclean, dangerous, and not worth their time. Nobody asked how he felt. Nobody asked his story. The label said it all. We do the same thing today, maybe without even thinking about it. We see a man sleeping on a park bench and decide he is lazy. We see a young mom with three kids in the checkout line using food assistance and decide she is making bad choices. We see a coworker who never comes to the church potluck and decide he does not care about God. Every one of those quick judgments builds a wall. It keeps us from loving the real person behind the label. Jesus never picked up a stone. When the man with leprosy came near, Jesus did not ask what he had done wrong or whether he deserved healing. He saw a person in pain and he acted with love. Putting down our stones means we stop writing people off. It means we choose curiosity over judgment. Instead of asking "What is wrong with that person?", we can ask "I wonder what that person has been through." A servant leader does not have to agree with every choice someone has made. But a servant leader refuses to let a label be the last word. When we forgive quickly and judge slowly, we make room for love to do its work. And love is what changes hearts, not judgment.

Prayer:

Heavenly Father, I am sorry for the times I judged people before I ever knew them, the labels I placed, the gossip I repeated, the walls I built with my thoughts. Forgive me for picking up stones instead of lending a hand. Today, when I meet someone with a rough past or a different lifestyle, help me see them as loved and valuable, the way You see them. Teach me to be slow to judge and quick to forgive. Fill my heart with Your love so no room is left for condemning others. Amen.

Study questions

1Before the leper met Jesus, what had the crowd already decided about him?

  1. That he was a great teacher
  2. That he was unclean and not worth their time
  3. That he was the new town leader
  4. That he was hiding money

2What does the lesson say we often do without thinking?

  1. We judge people by what we see on the outside
  2. We invite strangers to dinner
  3. We pray for everyone we meet
  4. We volunteer at shelters every week

3When the man with leprosy came near, what did Jesus do?

  1. He asked what the man had done wrong
  2. He checked if the man deserved healing
  3. He saw a person in pain and acted with love
  4. He told the man to go clean himself up first

4What question does the lesson suggest we ask instead of "What is wrong with that person?"

  1. "How fast can I get away from this person?"
  2. "I wonder what that person has been through."
  3. "Why does this person keep bothering me?"
  4. "Who should I tell about this person?"

5According to the lesson, what changes hearts?

  1. Strict judgment
  2. Harsh rules
  3. Love
  4. Punishment

Week 9 · Touching the Untouchable (Leper Cleansing)

Wednesday – Thankful Hearts, Healed Lives

"We shall never know all the good that a simple smile can do." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Matthew 8:3 — "And Jesus stretched out his hand and touched him, saying, 'I will; be clean.' And immediately his leprosy was cleansed."

The moment Jesus touched the man, everything changed. The sickness that had stolen his home, his family, and his place in the community was gone. But notice something beautiful. The healing did not only clean his skin. It gave him his life back. He could hug his children again. He could walk into town without shouting "unclean." He could worship with his friends again. Kindness does not just fix a problem. It gives a person their dignity back. A simple smile can do the same kind of work in smaller ways. Think about a tired cashier who has heard complaints all day long. A warm smile and a thank you can remind her that she matters. Think about the new volunteer at church who does not know anyone yet. A smile and a kind hello can turn a scary first day into a happy one. Think about the neighbor who just lost his wife. Your smile across the fence can tell him he is not forgotten. We may never know all the good a smile can do, because we rarely see the whole story. The person we smiled at goes home feeling a little lighter, and they are kinder to the next person they meet. That is how healing spreads. Jesus touched the untouchable man, and his life was made whole. When we share small, kind moments with the people others overlook, we become part of that same healing. A thankful heart notices the good in others. A healed life passes the healing on. That is the chain we get to be part of every single day.

Prayer:

Dear God, thank You for the way Jesus touched the man no one else would touch and made his life whole. Thank You for the small gifts of kindness You put in my hands each day, a smile for the tired cashier, a kind word for the lonely neighbor, a helping hand for the overwhelmed mom. Give me a thankful heart that notices the good in people. Remind me I never know how far a small act of love can travel. Let my words and my smile bring a little of Your healing to the world today. Amen.

Study questions

1What did the man's healing give him back besides clean skin?

  1. Money and land
  2. His life, his family, and his place in the community
  3. A new house
  4. A job as a priest

2After being healed, what could the man do that he could not do before?

  1. Hug his children and walk into town without shouting "unclean"
  2. Fly to another country
  3. Become the king
  4. Build a temple

3According to the lesson, what can a simple smile do for a tired cashier?

  1. Make her work faster
  2. Remind her that she matters
  3. Give her a raise
  4. Make her forget her job

4Why do we never know all the good a smile can do?

  1. Because smiles do not really matter
  2. Because we rarely see the whole story of how kindness spreads
  3. Because people forget about smiles right away
  4. Because only doctors can help people

5In this lesson, what does a healed life do?

  1. Keeps the healing to itself
  2. Passes the healing on to others
  3. Moves to a new town
  4. Stops helping anyone else

Week 9 · Touching the Untouchable (Leper Cleansing)

Thursday – Rolling Up Our Sleeves

"Life's most persistent and urgent question is, 'What are you doing for others?'" — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): James 1:27 — "Religion that is pure and undefiled before God the Father is this: to visit orphans and widows in their affliction, and to keep oneself unstained from the world."

It is easy to say we care about people. It is harder to roll up our sleeves and show it. The Bible tells us that real faith is not just what we believe in our heads or what we say with our mouths. Real faith is what we do with our hands. Visiting orphans and widows in their affliction means moving toward the people who have the least help and giving them our time and our strength. That is what Jesus did for the man with leprosy. He did not just feel sorry for him from far away. He reached out and touched him. His love had hands and feet. We can do the same in our everyday lives. Rolling up our sleeves might look like bringing a warm meal to the widow who lives alone on your street. It might look like helping the single dad next door fix his broken fence on a Saturday morning. It might look like sitting with a lonely elderly man at church instead of rushing home after service. It might look like giving a ride to the coworker whose car broke down, even when it makes your day longer. These are not big, famous acts. They are small, faithful ones. But they answer the most urgent question of all. What are you doing for others? A servant leader does not wait to be asked. He looks for the need and steps in. When we serve the forgotten and the hurting, we are practicing the purest kind of faith there is, the kind God smiles on. Our sleeves may get dirty. Our plans may get interrupted. But our hearts will grow, and the people we serve will know they are loved.

Prayer:

Father God, thank You for showing me that real faith is not just words, but actions. Forgive me for the times I said I cared but did nothing. Today, open my eyes to the needs around me, the lonely widow, the struggling single parent, the forgotten neighbor, and give me willing hands and an open schedule to serve them with joy. Help me answer the question "What are you doing for others?" with my life, not just my words. Make me a servant leader who sees the need and steps in with love. Amen.

1According to the lesson, what is real faith?

  1. Only what we believe in our heads
  2. Only what we say with our mouths
  3. What we do with our hands
  4. What we wear on Sundays

  1. Kings and rulers

  3. Soldiers and sailors
  4. Teachers and doctors

  1. Bringing a warm meal to the widow who lives alone on your street
  2. Waiting to be asked before ever helping anyone
  3. Rushing home quickly after church service
  4. Feeling sorry for people from far away

4What did Jesus do for the man with leprosy, according to this lesson?

  1. He felt sorry for him from far away
  2. He reached out and touched him, showing love with hands and feet
  3. He told someone else to help the man
  4. He asked the man to pay him first

5What does the lesson say a servant leader does when he sees a need?

  1. He waits until someone asks him for help
  2. He looks for the need and steps in
  3. He tells the need to go away
  4. He ignores it and keeps walking

Week 9 · Touching the Untouchable (Leper Cleansing)

Friday – Loved So We Can Love

"The greatest thing that we can do is to help somebody know that they're loved and capable of loving." — Fred Rogers
Bible (ESV): John 13:34-35 — "'A new commandment I give to you, that you love one another: just as I have loved you, you also are to love one another. By this all people will know that you are my disciples, if you have love for one another.'"

All week we have been learning about Jesus and the man with leprosy. Now we come to the reason behind it all. Jesus did not touch the man because the man earned it. He touched him because love was in his heart, and love has to move. Then Jesus gave us a new command. Love one another the way I have loved you. Notice the order. First we are loved. Then we love. We do not love people to earn God's favor. We love people because God's love is already filling us up, and a full heart has to pour out somewhere. Think of it like a cup under a running faucet. When the cup is full, the water spills over the sides. When God's love fills us, it spills over onto everyone around us. That is why the greatest thing we can do is help someone know they are loved. Maybe you know a neighbor who believes nobody cares about her. Your kindness can be the first drop of proof that love is real. Maybe there is a teenager at church who acts tough because he is afraid no one sees him. Your attention can show him he matters. Maybe there is a stranger on the bus who looks tired and worn down. Your smile can tell him he is not invisible. When people feel loved, something wakes up inside them. They begin to believe they are capable of loving too. That is the miracle. Love does not run out when we give it away. It multiplies. And Jesus said the world would know we belong to him by our love. Not by our buildings. Not by our arguments. By our love. That is our mark. That is our mission.

Prayer:

Loving God, thank You for loving me first, long before I earned it or deserved it. Fill my heart so full of Your love that it spills over onto everyone I meet today. Help me be the proof of Your love to the neighbor who feels forgotten, the teenager who feels unseen, the new kid at school who feels invisible. Make me gentle, patient, and kind in all I do, at home, at work, in the checkout line. Let the world see that I belong to You by the way I love. Amen.

  2. Because love was in his heart, and love has to move
  3. Because the crowd forced him to
  4. Because he wanted to become famous

2What is the order Jesus gives us for loving others?

  1. We love first, then God loves us
  2. We earn love, then we share it
  3. First we are loved, then we love
  4. We love only people who love us back

3The lesson compares God's love filling us to what?

  1. A cup under a running faucet that spills over when full
  2. A bank account that keeps growing
  3. A fire that burns out quickly
  4. A locked box that stays shut

4According to Jesus in John 13:35, how will everyone know we are his disciples?

  1. By our church buildings
  2. By our arguments
  3. If we love one another
  4. By our clothes

5When people feel loved, what does the lesson say happens inside them?

  1. They become proud and selfish
  2. They begin to believe they are capable of loving too
  3. They stop needing anyone
  4. They forget about God

Looking back

This week you watched Jesus touch the untouchable — and you learned to do the same. Go where it hurts. Put down your stones; if you judge people, you have no time to love them. Roll up your sleeves and serve. Thankful hearts grow where healing happens. Loved, so we can love.
