Week 14 · Prayer and Solitude as Fuel for Service

Tuesday – Small Worries Belong in Big Prayers

"Any concern too small to be turned into a prayer is too small to be made into a burden." — Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Philippians 4:6 — "do not be anxious about anything, but in everything by prayer and supplication with thanksgiving let your requests be made known to God."

Do you ever carry little worries around all day? The parking ticket you forgot to pay. The kind word you forgot to say. The friend you have not called back. They are small, but they pile up on your heart until they feel heavy. Corrie ten Boom learned this truth in the hardest place imaginable. She and her family hid Jewish people from the Nazis during World War II, and she was sent to a prison camp for it. She learned there that no worry is too small to hand to God, and no worry is too small to weigh you down if you keep it to yourself.

Servant leaders carry a lot. You worry about the people you serve, the team you lead, and whether you are doing enough. If you do not pray about those worries, they turn into stress, and stress turns into treating people badly. You snap at a volunteer. You stop listening to a hurting person. Your heart gets tired.

But here is the beautiful part: God wants every one of those small concerns. Nothing is too little for Him. When you pray about the small stuff, you are not bothering God. You are trusting Him. And a leader who trusts God with small worries becomes a calm, kind leader that people want to follow.

This calls us to be better people because prayer makes us lighter. When your heart is light, you smile more, you forgive faster, and you have room to care about other people's burdens instead of drowning in your own. Give God the small things, and watch how big your heart becomes.

Prayer:

Lord, I bring You my small worries today, the bill I am not sure I can pay, the doctor's appointment I am nervous about, my child's bad day at school. No concern is too small for prayer, and none is too big for You. Instead of carrying these alone until my shoulders ache, I lay them at Your feet. Replace my anxious thoughts with Your peace. I pray for every worried heart tonight. Teach us all to trade our burdens for prayer, one small worry at a time. Amen.

Study questions

1What did Corrie ten Boom learn about small concerns?

  1. They should be ignored
  2. Any concern too small to be prayed about is too small to become a burden
  3. They always disappear on their own
  4. Only big worries matter to God

2What does Philippians 4:6 tell us to do in every situation?

  1. Worry quietly
  2. Present our requests to God by prayer and petition
  3. Handle everything alone
  4. Wait until the worry gets bigger

3When servant leaders do not pray about their worries, the lesson says those worries turn into:

  1. Greater wisdom
  2. Stress that makes them treat people badly
  3. More free time
  4. Better planning skills

4Why is a praying leader someone people want to follow?

  1. They never have any problems
  2. They are calm and kind because they trust God
  3. They always have the right answers
  4. They work longer hours than everyone

5Giving God the small things makes our hearts:

  1. Smaller and harder
  2. Bigger, so we can care for others' burdens
  3. Tired and worn out
  4. Full of fear

Week 14 · Prayer and Solitude as Fuel for Service

Wednesday – The Longing of the Soul

"Prayer is not asking. It is a longing of the soul." — Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): Luke 5:16 — "But he would withdraw to desolate places and pray."

Many people think prayer is just a wish list we hand to God. Please fix this. Please give me that. Please help me now. But Gandhi saw something deeper. He said prayer is not really about asking. It is a longing, a deep hunger inside us to be close to something greater than ourselves.

Look at Jesus. The Bible says He "often" went to lonely places to pray. Not once in a while. Often. He was not just asking for things. He was longing to be with His Father. That longing kept His heart soft and His purpose clear. Every time He stepped away from the crowds, He came back with more love for them, not less.

A servant leader who only prays when they need something will burn out fast. But a servant leader who prays because their soul longs for God will stay strong for the long road. That kind of prayer changes you from the inside out. You stop serving to feel good about yourself, and you start serving because love is overflowing from you.

This calls us to be better people because when prayer is a longing and not just asking, we become givers instead of takers. We stop thinking, "What can people do for me?" and start thinking, "How can I pour out what God has poured into me?" The people around you can feel the difference. They can tell when your kindness comes from a full heart or an empty one. Let your soul long for God, and your life will become a gift to everyone near you.

Prayer:

God, my soul is longing for You, not just for answers, but for You. In the middle of my noisy house and my crowded calendar, I feel the pull to withdraw like Jesus did, to find a quiet place and simply be with You. Forgive me for filling every silence with noise. Today, carve out a few still minutes where I can breathe and listen. Satisfy this holy hunger with Your presence. I pray for every restless heart chasing noise. Lead us all to the quiet place where You are waiting. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Gandhi, prayer is mainly:

  1. A wish list for God
  2. A longing of the soul
  3. A way to look religious
  4. Something only priests should do

2How often did Jesus withdraw to lonely places to pray, according to Luke 5:16?

  1. Once a year
  2. Only when He was in trouble
  3. Often
  4. Never alone

3What happened each time Jesus came back from praying alone?

  1. He was more tired
  2. He came back with more love for the crowds
  3. He avoided people
  4. He forgot His purpose

4A servant leader who prays only when they need something will:

  1. Grow stronger every day
  2. Burn out fast
  3. Become famous
  4. Never need rest

5When prayer is a longing instead of just asking, we become:

  1. Takers who need more from people
  2. Givers who pour out what God poured in
  3. Quieter and less helpful
  4. Focused only on ourselves

Week 14 · Prayer and Solitude as Fuel for Service

Thursday – One Step of Faith

"Faith is taking the first step even when you don't see the whole staircase." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Matthew 6:6 — "But when you pray, go into your room and shut the door and pray to your Father who is in secret. And your Father who sees in secret will reward you."

Dr. King led millions of people through some of the darkest days in American history, and he did not have all the answers. He could not see the whole staircase. He could only see the next step. That is what faith looks like, and it is also what prayer looks like.

When you go into your room, shut the door, and pray, you cannot see God with your eyes. You cannot see how He will answer. You are taking a step in the dark, trusting that your Father is there in the secret place, listening. That takes faith. But Jesus promises that the Father who sees in secret will reward you. He does not say how or when. He just says trust Me and take the step.

Servant leaders face this every day. You step up to help someone and you do not know if it will work. You start a new ministry and you cannot see the ending. You forgive someone and you do not know if they will change. Prayer in the secret place is where you get the courage to take those steps anyway.

This calls us to be better people because faith-filled people are brave for others. When you trust God in the quiet, you become bold in the open. You visit the sick even when it is uncomfortable. You stand up for someone being treated badly even when it costs you. You give even when your own wallet feels thin. Your secret faith becomes public love, and the people around you learn to trust God too, just by watching you take that first step.

Prayer:

Father, today I face something that scares me, a hard conversation, a big decision, a step I cannot see the end of. Like Jesus taught, I come to You in the quiet of my room first. Give me the faith to take just one step, even when I cannot see the whole staircase. When my knees shake and my voice trembles, hold me steady. I pray for everyone standing at the edge of a frightening step today. Walk with us. Remind us that we never step alone when we step with You. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Dr. King, faith is:

  1. Seeing the whole staircase before you move
  2. Taking the first step even when you don't see the whole staircase
  3. Never feeling afraid
  4. Having all the answers

2What does Matthew 6:6 tell us to do when we pray?

  1. Pray loudly on the street corner
  2. Go into our room, close the door, and pray to our Father
  3. Only pray in church
  4. Pray so others will notice

3What does Jesus promise about the Father who sees in secret?

  1. He will ignore secret prayers
  2. He will reward you
  3. He only rewards public prayer
  4. He will tell everyone what you prayed

4How does secret prayer make a servant leader brave?

  1. It removes all their problems
  2. Trusting God in the quiet makes them bold in the open
  3. It makes them popular
  4. It guarantees success

5The lesson says our secret faith becomes:

  1. A private hobby
  2. Public love that others can see
  3. A reason to avoid people
  4. Something we keep to ourselves forever

Week 14 · Prayer and Solitude as Fuel for Service

Friday – Listening Is the First Service

"The first service that one owes to others in the fellowship consists in listening to them." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): Psalm 46:10 — "Be still, and know that I am God. I will be exalted among the nations, I will be exalted in the earth!"

Bonhoeffer was a pastor in Germany who stood up against great evil, and he learned that the very first way to serve someone is not to talk, not to fix, and not to advise. It is to listen. Before you can help a person, you have to hear them. That sounds simple, but most of us are terrible at it. We listen just long enough to think of what we want to say next.

Listening starts in the quiet place with God. When you are still before God, you practice the skill of listening. You stop talking. You stop rushing. You learn to be fully present. Then, when you walk out into your day, you carry that stillness with you. You listen to the tired mother without checking your phone. You listen to the lonely neighbor without looking at your watch. You listen to the hurting friend without jumping in to fix them.

This is one of the greatest gifts a servant leader can give, because most hurting people do not need your answers. They need your ears. When someone feels truly heard, they feel truly loved. And when you listen well, you often learn what a person really needs, which makes your help ten times better.

This calls us to be better people because listening is love in action. Anyone can talk. It takes a servant's heart to be quiet and pay attention. This week, practice being still with God first, then be still with people. Put the phone down. Look them in the eyes. Let them finish. You will be amazed at how your listening heals hearts, including your own.

Prayer:

Lord, teach me that listening is service. Today, when my friend pours out her troubles, when my child tells me about his day, when my coworker just needs to vent, help me be still and truly listen. Quiet my urge to interrupt with advice or rush to the next thing. "Be still, and know that I am God." Let me live that with people too. I pray for everyone who feels unheard today. Make me the one who stops, sits down, and listens with love. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Bonhoeffer, the first service we owe others is:

  1. Giving them advice
  2. Listening to them
  3. Fixing their problems
  4. Teaching them lessons

2What does Psalm 46:10 command us to do?

  1. Be still, and know that I am God
  2. Work harder every day
  3. Never rest
  4. Speak before listening

3Where does good listening begin, according to this lesson?

  1. In a public speaking class
  2. In the quiet place with God
  3. On social media
  4. By talking more

4Why is listening better than giving quick answers to a hurting person?

  1. Answers are always wrong
  2. Most hurting people need your ears more than your answers
  3. Listening is faster
  4. Answers take too much effort

5What practical step does the lesson suggest for better listening?

  1. Check your phone while they talk
  2. Look them in the eyes and let them finish
  3. Interrupt with your own story
  4. Watch the clock
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Short essay: Think of someone in your life who truly listens to you. Describe how their listening makes you feel, and write about one specific way you will practice better listening this week, both with God in prayer and with the people around you.

Looking back

This week you learned the servant's secret fuel: prayer and solitude. Quiet first, service second. Small worries belong in big prayers. Take one step of faith, then another. And remember — listening is the first service you can offer anyone. A leader who never stops to pray will eventually run on empty. Next week, you will learn how to stand steady when criticism comes and popularity fades.

Week 15 · Handling Criticism and Popularity

Monday – The Time Is Always Right

Week opening

Jesus was cheered one day and jeered the next. Crowds loved Him, leaders plotted against Him — and He never wavered. This week you will learn how to handle both criticism and popularity without losing yourself. You will stop seeking people's approval and start seeking God's, let go of worry that steals today's strength, and lose yourself in service. The time is always right to do what is right — no matter who is watching.

"The time is always right to do what is right." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Galatians 1:10 — "For am I now seeking the approval of man, or of God? Or am I trying to please man? If I were still trying to please man, I would not be a servant of Christ."

Dr. King said these words while facing some of the harshest criticism any leader has ever faced. People called him names. People threatened his life. Powerful people told him to wait, to slow down, to stop. But he kept doing what was right, because he answered to God first, not to the crowd.

Every servant leader will face this same test. When you stand up for what is right, some people will cheer and some people will criticize. If you make your choices based on who is clapping, you will be tossed around like a leaf in the wind. One day you do this to please one group, the next day you change to please another. That is no way to lead.

Paul gives us the cure in Galatians. He asks a straight question: am I trying to please people, or God? A servant of Christ answers to God. That does not mean we ignore people or stop caring about their feelings. It means God's approval is the anchor that holds us steady when the crowd's opinion shifts.

This calls us to be better people because doing right even when it is unpopular is one of the bravest forms of love. It means telling the truth kindly when a lie would be easier. It means including the left-out person when the popular thing is to ignore them. It means staying honest when cheating would win applause. When you do what is right no matter who is watching, the people around you learn that integrity is real, and they start to live it too.

Prayer:

God, give me the courage to do what is right even when it is unpopular. Today at work or at school, when everyone else is cutting corners or laughing at someone's expense, help me stand for what pleases You, not people. Free me from the need for applause. The time is always right to do right, in the morning meeting, at the lunch table, in the group chat. I pray for everyone pressured to go along with wrong today. Strengthen them. Let my life seek Your approval above all. Amen.

Study questions

1Why did Dr. King keep doing what was right despite harsh criticism?

  1. He enjoyed arguing with people
  2. He answered to God first, not to the crowd
  3. He wanted to become famous
  4. He never faced any real danger

2According to Galatians 1:10, a servant of Christ tries to please:

  1. The largest crowd
  2. Their closest friends
  3. God, not people
  4. Whoever is loudest

3What happens when a leader makes choices based on who is clapping?

  1. They become wise
  2. They get tossed around like a leaf in the wind
  3. Everyone always agrees with them
  4. They never face criticism

4Doing right when it is unpopular is described as:

  1. A foolish waste of time
  2. One of the bravest forms of love
  3. A way to win arguments
  4. Only for famous leaders

5When you do what is right no matter who is watching, the people around you:

  1. Will always criticize you
  2. Learn that integrity is real and start to live it too
  3. Stop trusting you
  4. Forget about you

Week 15 · Handling Criticism and Popularity

Tuesday – Worry Steals Today's Strength

"Worry does not empty tomorrow of its sorrow. It empties today of its strength." — Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Matthew 5:11-12 — "Blessed are you when others revile you and persecute you and utter all kinds of evil against you falsely on my account. Rejoice and be glad, for your reward is great in heaven, for so they persecuted the prophets who were before you."

Corrie ten Boom survived a Nazi prison camp, so she knew what real trouble looked like. She also knew that worry is a thief. It does not fix tomorrow. It only steals the strength you need for today. When leaders are criticized, worry whispers lies: What will they say next? What if everyone turns against me? What if I fail? Those thoughts drain your energy until you have nothing left to give the people who need you.

Jesus gives us a shocking answer to criticism in Matthew 5. He does not say, "Feel sorry for yourself when people insult you." He says, "Rejoice and be glad." That sounds crazy until you understand why. When you are criticized for doing what Jesus would do, you are in good company. The prophets faced the same thing. You are walking the same road as the heroes of faith.

This does not mean criticism never hurts. It hurts. But worry about criticism is optional, and it is poison. A servant leader who spends all day replaying mean words in their head has no strength left to serve. But a leader who hands the criticism to God and chooses joy stays strong enough to keep loving people, even the critics.

This calls us to be better people because a worry-free leader is a generous leader. When you are not drained by fear of what people think, you have energy to encourage your team, patience to listen to complaints without getting defensive, and kindness to spare for the very people who criticized you. Refuse to let worry steal today. Use today's strength to love today's people.

Prayer:

Lord, my mind keeps racing to tomorrow, what people will say, what might go wrong, whether I will be criticized. But worry only empties today of its strength. When others misunderstand me or speak against me for doing right, remind me that You called me blessed. Fill this day with Your strength instead of my fear. I pray for everyone lying awake with anxious thoughts tonight. Hold them close. Teach us to trust You with tomorrow so we can live fully today. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Corrie ten Boom, what does worry do?

  1. Fixes tomorrow's problems
  2. Empties today of its strength
  3. Makes leaders stronger
  4. Helps us plan better

2What does Jesus say to do when people insult you because of Him?

  1. Feel sorry for yourself
  2. Rejoice and be glad
  3. Insult them back
  4. Quit serving

3Why can we rejoice when criticized for doing right?

  1. Because criticism means we are popular
  2. Because we are in good company with the prophets
  3. Because it never hurts
  4. Because critics are always wrong about everything

4What happens to a leader who replays mean words all day?

  1. They become wiser
  2. They have no strength left to serve
  3. They earn more respect
  4. They sleep better

5A worry-free leader is described as:

  1. A generous leader with energy to encourage and patience to listen
  2. A leader who never faces problems
  3. A leader who ignores everyone
  4. A leader who avoids hard work

Week 15 · Handling Criticism and Popularity

Wednesday – Impossible Until It's Done

"It always seems impossible until it's done." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): John 15:18 — "If the world hates you, know that it has hated me before it hated you."

Nelson Mandela spent 27 years in prison for standing against apartheid, the cruel system that separated people by the color of their skin in South Africa. The world told him change was impossible. Critics mocked him. Even some of his own people lost hope. But he kept going, and one day the impossible was done. He walked out of prison and later became the president of a free South Africa.

Servant leaders often feel the same way. You try to bring people together and someone criticizes you. You try to help the poor and someone says it will never work. You try to forgive and someone calls you weak. The voices of criticism can make the mission feel impossible. But Jesus warned us about this ahead of time. He said, "If the world hates you, keep in mind that it hated me first." Criticism is not a sign that you are failing. Sometimes it is a sign that you are following Jesus closely.

The key is to keep serving while the critics talk. Mandela did not win by arguing with every critic. He won by staying faithful to the mission for 27 years. Your mission may not take 27 years, but it will take staying power. Keep showing up. Keep loving people. Keep doing the next right thing.

This calls us to be better people because steady servants change the world around them. When you refuse to quit, the discouraged people watching you find hope. The young people watching you learn courage. The critics watching you sometimes even change their minds. Do not let "impossible" stop you. One faithful day at a time, the impossible gets done.

Prayer:

God, I am facing something that feels impossible, a broken relationship, a mountain of debt, a dream everyone says will never happen. When the world pushes back and people say it cannot be done, remind me that it always seems impossible until it is done. Give me stubborn faith and steady hands. I pray for everyone told their cause is hopeless today. Breathe courage into them. With You, no wall is too high, no night too long, and no battle too hard. Amen.

  2. 17 years

  4. 37 years

  1. It means we are doing something wrong
  2. Keep in mind that it hated me first
  3. We should hate the world back
  4. It will never happen to good people

3According to the lesson, criticism is sometimes a sign that:

  1. You are failing
  2. You are following Jesus closely
  3. You should quit
  4. You need to argue more

4How did Mandela win, according to the lesson?

  1. By arguing with every critic
  2. By staying faithful to the mission for 27 years
  3. By giving up early
  4. By seeking popularity

5When you refuse to quit, the people watching you:

  1. Always criticize you more
  2. Find hope and learn courage
  3. Lose interest
  4. Become your enemies

Week 15 · Handling Criticism and Popularity

Thursday – Feed Just One

"If you can't feed a hundred people, then feed just one." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Proverbs 29:25 — "The fear of man lays a snare, but whoever trusts in the Lord is safe."

Mother Teresa worked in the slums of Calcutta, India, where the need was so big it could crush your heart. Millions of poor people. Sickness everywhere. Critics said her work was too small to matter. Why bother? You cannot fix all of this. But she answered with this simple truth: if you cannot feed a hundred, feed just one. She refused to let the size of the problem, or the voices of the critics, stop her from helping the one person right in front of her.

This is a powerful answer to both criticism and popularity. Some leaders only act when the crowd is big enough to notice. They want the hundred, the thousand, the viral moment. And when critics say the work is too small, they quit. But Mother Teresa teaches us a better way. Serve the one. Love the one. The size of the audience does not decide the size of the love.

Proverbs warns us that fear of man is a snare, a trap. When we fear what people think, we get trapped. We only serve when it looks impressive. We only speak when it is safe. But when we trust in the Lord, we are kept safe. Safe to do small, faithful work that no one sees. Safe to keep going when the critics laugh at how little we are doing.

This calls us to be better people because the world is changed one person at a time. The tired coworker you encourage. The lonely neighbor you visit. The child you listen to. These small acts may never make the news, but they make heaven rejoice. Do not wait until you can feed a hundred. Feed just one, today, with all your heart.

Prayer:

Lord, I am afraid of what people think, afraid to look foolish, afraid to fail in front of others. But the fear of people is a trap, and I want to be free. Today, help me trust You more than I fear their opinions. Show me just one person to feed, help, or encourage, one small act, done bravely. I pray for everyone paralyzed by people's judgment today. Set us free. Let our quiet faithfulness matter more than anyone's applause. Amen.

  2. In the slums of Calcutta, India

  4. In the deserts of Egypt

  1. She quit and went home
  2. If you can't feed a hundred people, then feed just one
  3. She argued with them daily
  4. She only helped large crowds after that

3According to Proverbs 29:25, fear of man is:

  1. A wise strategy
  2. A snare, a trap
  3. The key to popularity
  4. A gift from God

  1. Guaranteed to be famous
  2. Kept safe
  3. Never criticized
  4. Always wealthy

  3. By waiting for big opportunities

Week 15 · Handling Criticism and Popularity

Friday – Lose Yourself in Service

"The best way to find yourself is to lose yourself in the service of others." — Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:45 — "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

Gandhi noticed something strange about human hearts. The more we chase after ourselves, our image, our fame, our comfort, the more lost we feel. But the moment we forget about ourselves and start serving others, we find out who we really are. It is a beautiful paradox: you find yourself by losing yourself.

Jesus lived this perfectly. He said He came not to be served but to serve, and to give His life as a ransom for many. He was the King of everything, yet He washed dirty feet. He deserved all the praise in the world, yet He took all the criticism without fighting back. He lost Himself completely in serving us, and in doing so He showed us exactly who He is: love itself.

This is the final answer to both criticism and popularity. The leader who is chasing popularity will always be shaken by criticism, because their identity is built on what people think. But the leader who has lost themselves in service is unshakable, because their identity is built on love. Critics can take away your reputation, but they cannot take away your love. Crowds can stop clapping, but they cannot stop you from serving.

This calls us to be better people because self-forgetful servants are the happiest people on earth. They are not keeping score. They are not checking who noticed. They are simply giving, and giving fills the heart in a way that getting never can. This week, try losing yourself. Serve someone who cannot pay you back. Love someone who may never thank you. You will find yourself, and you will find joy.

Prayer:

God, I am tired of thinking about myself, my wants, my image, my plans. Today, help me lose myself in serving others. Show me the tired mom who needs a break, the elderly neighbor who needs groceries, the coworker drowning in work. Let me pour my energy into them and forget to keep score. Like Jesus, who came not to be served but to serve, teach me that the best way to find my life is to give it away. Fill my self-forgetfulness with Your joy. Amen.

  4. He came to build His reputation

  2. Because their identity is built on what people think
  3. Because popularity is a sin

  4. Their applause

Looking back

Lose yourself in service and you will find yourself. Criticism cannot sink a leader anchored in God. Next week, you will learn to hold truth and grace together the way Jesus did — full of both, never one without the other.

Week 16 · Inhabiting Truth and Grace

Monday – Look at What They Suffer

Week opening

‘And the Word became flesh and dwelt among us... full of grace and truth.’ Jesus never chose between honesty and kindness — He carried both, fully. This week you will learn to do the same. You will look at what people suffer before you judge them, trade judgment for love, and do your little bit of good where you are. Truth without grace crushes. Grace without truth drifts. A servant leader holds both.

"We must learn to regard people less in the light of what they do or omit to do, and more in the light of what they suffer." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): John 1:14 — "And the Word became flesh and dwelt among us, and we have seen his glory, glory as of the only Son from the Father, full of grace and truth."

Bonhoeffer wrote these words from a Nazi prison cell, where he was locked up for standing against Hitler. He had every reason to judge the people around him. Instead, he learned to look deeper. He stopped asking only, "What did this person do wrong?" and started asking, "What has this person suffered?" That one change turned judgment into compassion.

This is what it means to be full of grace and truth, like Jesus in John 1:14. Truth means we do not pretend wrong is right. Grace means we do not crush people for their wrong. Jesus lived both perfectly. He told the truth about sin, but He always saw the suffering behind the sin. He saw the lonely woman at the well, not just her broken past. He saw the hated tax collector up the tree, not just his greed.

Servant leaders need this double vision. When a volunteer lets you down, truth says the job still matters. Grace says maybe they are carrying a burden you cannot see. When a team member is difficult, truth says the behavior needs to change. Grace says lead with kindness first and find out what hurts underneath.

This calls us to be better people because everyone around you is fighting a battle you know nothing about. The rude cashier may have just lost someone she loves. The grumpy coworker may be scared about money. When you look at what people suffer instead of only what they do, your heart softens. You correct gently. You forgive quickly. You become the kind of person others feel safe around, and that is what a servant leader should be.

Prayer:

Father, the rude cashier, the angry driver, the critical relative, it is so easy to judge what they do. Today, teach me to look deeper, at what they suffer. Maybe she is grieving, maybe he is scared, maybe they are carrying a weight I cannot see. Give me eyes of compassion instead of judgment. When someone's sharp words sting me, help me wonder about their hidden pain. I pray for every hurting person acting out of their wounds today. Heal them, Lord, and soften my heart toward them. Amen.

  1. On a vacation
  2. In a Nazi prison cell
  3. At a university
  4. In a church meeting

2According to John 1:14, Jesus came from the Father full of:

  1. Power and anger
  2. Grace and truth
  3. Money and fame
  4. Rules and punishment

3What does "grace" mean in this lesson?

  1. Pretending wrong is right
  2. Not crushing people for their wrong while seeing their suffering
  3. Ignoring all problems
  4. Always agreeing with everyone

4When a volunteer lets you down, the lesson says a servant leader should:

  1. Fire them immediately
  2. Remember truth says the job matters, and grace says they may carry an unseen burden
  3. Pretend nothing happened
  4. Tell everyone about their failure

5Looking at what people suffer instead of only what they do makes us:

  1. Weaker leaders
  2. People others feel safe around
  3. Blind to the truth
  4. Afraid to correct anyone

Week 16 · Inhabiting Truth and Grace

Tuesday – No Time to Judge

"If you judge people, you have no time to love them." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): John 8:32 — "and you will know the truth, and the truth will set you free."

Mother Teresa spent her days picking up dying people from the streets of Calcutta. She did not ask how they ended up there. She did not sort them into good and bad. She just loved them. She knew a secret: judging and loving cannot live in the same heart at the same time. Every minute you spend judging someone is a minute you are not loving them.

Jesus said the truth will set you free, and one of the greatest truths is this: judging others puts you in prison, not them. When you judge, your heart gets hard. You start keeping a list of everyone's faults. You feel better than them for a moment, but then you feel lonely, because judges do not have friends, they have courtrooms. But when you let the truth set you free from judging, your hands are suddenly free to help and your heart is free to love.

Servant leaders are tempted to judge all the time. The team member who is always late. The neighbor who never says thank you. The family member who made the same mistake again. Truth matters, and we should not pretend problems do not exist. But there is a difference between seeing a problem clearly and judging a person's worth. You can address the lateness with kindness. You can set a boundary with love.

This calls us to be better people because the world has enough judges and not enough lovers. When you put down the gavel and pick up a towel, like Jesus washing feet, people feel it. They open up. They try harder. They change, not because you shamed them, but because you loved them. Trade your judging time for loving time today.

Prayer:

Lord, forgive me for the minutes I waste judging people, sizing up the neighbor's yard, whispering about a coworker's choices, deciding who is worthy. That time could be spent loving them instead. Today, when judgment rises in me, replace it with love. Help me know the truth that sets free: that every person is precious to You. I pray for everyone crushed under others' judgment today. Lift their heads. Make me someone whose presence feels like grace, not a courtroom. Amen.

Study questions

1What did Mother Teresa do instead of judging the dying people she picked up?

  1. She sorted them into good and bad
  2. She just loved them
  3. She asked how they ended up on the streets
  4. She sent them away

2According to the lesson, judging and loving:

  1. Work perfectly together
  2. Cannot live in the same heart at the same time
  3. Are the same thing
  4. Should both be avoided

3What does John 8:32 say the truth will do?

  1. Make us rich
  2. Set us free
  3. Make us popular
  4. Remove all problems

4What is the difference between seeing a problem and judging a person's worth?

  1. There is no difference
  2. You can address problems like lateness with kindness without condemning the person
  3. Problems should always be ignored
  4. Kindness means never speaking up

5When you trade judging for loving, people:

  1. Take advantage of you
  2. Open up and change because they feel loved, not shamed
  3. Stop respecting you
  4. Never improve

Week 16 · Inhabiting Truth and Grace

Wednesday – Your Little Bit of Good

"Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): Micah 6:8 — "He has told you, O man, what is good; and what does the Lord require of you but to do justice, and to love kindness, and to walk humbly with your God?"

Desmond Tutu helped lead South Africa out of apartheid, one of the greatest evils of the last hundred years. He did not do it alone, and he did not do it with one giant act. He did it with millions of little bits of good, done by ordinary people, put together until they overwhelmed the darkness. Your little bit matters more than you think.

Micah 6:8 gives us the simple recipe for those little bits: act justly, love mercy, and walk humbly with God. Notice how small and daily those words are. Act justly means do the fair thing in today's decisions. Love mercy means be kind to someone who does not deserve it today. Walk humbly means remember you are not the hero of the story, God is. None of these require a stage or a spotlight. They just require you, where you are, today.

Servant leaders sometimes dream so big they miss the small. They want to change the whole city while ignoring the lonely person at their own table. But Tutu's wisdom flips that around. Change the table first. Be fair in the small meeting. Show mercy to the difficult volunteer. Walk humbly when you are praised. Those little bits add up, and when enough servants do their little bit, the world gets overwhelmed with good.

This calls us to be better people because you do not have to wait until you are powerful to be good. You can start right where you are, with what you have, today. Smile at the tired cashier. Share your lunch. Tell the truth when a lie would be easier. Your little bit of good, joined with everyone else's, is how God overwhelms the darkness.

Prayer:

God, thank You that my little bit of good matters. Today, I will do it where I am: hold the door for the stranger, write the thank-you note, pick up the litter on my street, smile at the lonely kid. These small things feel tiny, but You say they overwhelm the world when we all do them. Forgive me for waiting to do something big. I pray for every quiet doer of good today. Bless their small acts. Let my little bit join theirs, and watch what You do with it. Amen.

Study questions

1How did Desmond Tutu say the world gets overwhelmed with good?

  1. By one giant act from a hero
  2. By little bits of good put together
  3. By waiting for governments to act
  4. By ignoring small problems

2According to Micah 6:8, what does the Lord require?

  1. To become famous and powerful
  2. To act justly, love mercy, and walk humbly with God
  3. To avoid all difficult people
  4. To work only on big projects

3"Walk humbly with God" means:

  1. You are the hero of the story
  2. Remembering God is the hero, not you
  3. Never speaking up
  4. Walking slowly

4What mistake do servant leaders sometimes make, according to the lesson?

  1. Dreaming too small
  2. Dreaming so big they miss the small acts right in front of them
  3. Helping too many people
  4. Praying too much

5You can start doing good:

  1. Only after you become powerful
  2. Right where you are, with what you have, today
  3. Only on special occasions
  4. Only when others join you first

Week 16 · Inhabiting Truth and Grace

Thursday – A Threat to Justice Everywhere

"Injustice anywhere is a threat to justice everywhere." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Ephesians 4:15 — "Rather, speaking the truth in love, we are to grow up in every way into him who is the head, into Christ,"

Dr. King wrote these words in a letter from a jail cell in Birmingham, Alabama. People told him to stay in his own city and mind his own business. Why care about injustice somewhere else? His answer changed history: injustice anywhere is a threat to justice everywhere. If it is wrong in one place, it is wrong in every place. We are all connected.

This is truth and grace working together. Truth says: name the wrong clearly. Do not soften it, do not look away. Grace says: speak it in love, the way Paul teaches in Ephesians 4:15. Speaking the truth in love means your goal is never to destroy a person. Your goal is to heal a situation and help everyone grow, including the one doing wrong.

Servant leaders live this out in small, everyday ways. When you hear a cruel joke about someone at work, truth says that joke is wrong. Grace says you can speak up kindly instead of attacking the joker. When you see a volunteer being treated unfairly, truth says it needs to stop. Grace says you can defend them without hating the offender. You become a protector, not a punisher.

This calls us to be better people because silence in the face of wrong is not kindness, it is fear wearing a mask. Real love speaks up. But real love also speaks gently. When you learn to speak the truth in love, you become the kind of person people trust with hard things. They know you will tell them the truth, and they know you will do it with a caring heart. That is a servant leader worth following.

Prayer:

Lord, give me the courage to speak the truth in love. Today, when I see something wrong at work, in my family, or in my community, do not let me stay silent just to keep the peace. Teach me to speak gently but clearly, correcting without crushing, honest without harsh. Injustice anywhere threatens justice everywhere, even in my own small corner. I pray for everyone afraid to speak up today. Loosen our tongues with love. Make my words a bridge to what is right. Amen.

Study questions

1Where was Dr. King when he wrote "Injustice anywhere is a threat to justice everywhere"?

  1. At a celebration dinner
  2. In a jail cell in Birmingham, Alabama
  3. On a vacation
  4. At a university lecture

2What does the quote mean?

  1. Only worry about your own city
  2. If injustice is wrong in one place, it threatens justice everywhere because we are all connected
  3. Justice is impossible
  4. Mind your own business

3According to Ephesians 4:15, we should speak:

  1. The truth in love
  2. Only when angry
  3. Whatever people want to hear
  4. Never about hard things

4Speaking the truth in love means your goal is:

  1. To destroy the person doing wrong
  2. To heal the situation and help everyone grow
  3. To win every argument
  4. To stay silent

5The lesson says silence in the face of wrong is:

  1. True kindness
  2. Fear wearing a mask
  3. The wisest choice
  4. What leaders should always do

Week 16 · Inhabiting Truth and Grace

Friday – Love Until It Hurts

"I have found the paradox, that if you love until it hurts, there can be no more hurt, only more love." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Romans 5:8 — "but God shows his love for us in that while we were still sinners, Christ died for us."

Mother Teresa discovered a strange secret about love. Most people love until it starts to cost them something, and then they stop. They love until someone is ungrateful, then they quit. They love until it is inconvenient, then they walk away. But she kept loving past the point of pain, and she found that on the other side of the hurt there was not emptiness. There was more love.

God showed us this first. Romans 5:8 says that while we were still sinners, while we were still running from Him, ignoring Him, and breaking His heart, Christ died for us. He did not wait until we were good enough. He did not wait until we said thank you. He loved until it hurt, all the way to the cross, and that love changed the world forever.

Servant leaders are called to this same costly love. You will pour into people who never thank you. You will forgive people who hurt you again. You will serve people who do not even like you. It will hurt sometimes. But Mother Teresa promises that if you keep going, the hurt turns into more love, not less. Your heart stretches. Your compassion deepens. You start to love like God loves.

This calls us to be better people because comfortable love changes nothing, but costly love changes everything. The people around you do not need a leader who loves them only when it is easy. They need a leader who stays when it is hard. Love until it hurts this week, and watch God fill the hurt places with even more love to give away.

Prayer:

God, loving is easy when people are kind, but You call me to love until it hurts. Today, help me love the difficult person: the relative who criticizes, the coworker who takes credit, the neighbor who complains. When my love runs out, fill me with Yours, the love that died for sinners, including me. Take away my limits and my scorekeeping. I pray for everyone hard to love today; surround them with patient hearts. Let there be no more hurt in me, only more love. Amen.

Study questions

1What paradox did Mother Teresa discover about love?

  1. Loving less brings more joy
  2. If you love until it hurts, there can be no more hurt, only more love
  3. Love always avoids pain
  4. Only easy love is real love

2According to Romans 5:8, when did Christ die for us?

  1. After we became good enough
  2. While we were still sinners
  3. Only after we said thank you
  4. When we earned it

3What does "costly love" mean in this lesson?

  1. Buying expensive gifts
  2. Loving people even when it hurts, when they are ungrateful, or when it is inconvenient
  3. Only loving wealthy people
  4. Avoiding difficult relationships

4When you keep loving past the point of pain, the lesson says:

  1. Your heart becomes empty
  2. Your heart stretches and your compassion deepens
  3. People take advantage forever
  4. You should stop immediately

5The lesson says comfortable love changes nothing, but:

  1. Giving up changes everything
  2. Costly love changes everything
  3. Avoiding people changes everything
  4. Waiting changes everything
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Short essay: Write about someone who loved you even when it was hard for them, maybe a parent, a teacher, or a friend. How did their costly love affect you? Now describe one relationship in your life where God might be asking you to “love until it hurts.”

Looking back

This week you learned to be full of grace and truth, like Jesus. Look at what people suffer. Put down judgment — if you judge people, you have no time to love them. Do your little bit of good where you are; those little bits put together overwhelm the world. Love until it hurts. Next week, you will pick up the hardest tool in a leader's hands: forgiveness.

Week 17 · Forgiveness as a Leadership Tool

Monday – The Key That Unlocks

Week opening

Every leader gets hurt. People will fail you, betray you, and let you down. What you do next decides what kind of leader you are. This week you will learn that forgiveness is not weakness — it is the key that unlocks the door of resentment and the handcuffs of hatred. You will stick with love, stay faithful in small things, and let your light shine despite the darkness. Forgiven people forgive.

"Forgiveness is the key that unlocks the door of resentment and the handcuffs of hatred." — Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Ephesians 4:32 — "Be kind to one another, tenderhearted, forgiving one another, as God in Christ forgave you."

Corrie ten Boom knew about hatred. She watched the Nazis murder her sister in a prison camp. After the war, she traveled and spoke about forgiveness, and one night a former prison guard, one of the cruel men from her camp, walked up to her and asked for her forgiveness. Her hand froze. Everything in her wanted to refuse. But she prayed, she reached out her hand, and she forgave him. In that moment, she said she felt God's love flood through her like electricity. The key turned. The handcuffs fell off.

Resentment is like locking yourself in a prison and throwing away the key. You think you are punishing the person who hurt you, but you are the one stuck inside. Hatred is like handcuffs on your own wrists. Forgiveness is the key, and here is the surprise: the person it sets free first is you.

Ephesians 4:32 tells us to forgive each other just as God forgave us. Think about how much God has forgiven you. Every wrong, every selfish choice, every unkind word. He forgave it all through Christ. When that truth sinks in, forgiving others stops feeling impossible and starts feeling like the only natural response.

This calls us to be better people because a forgiving leader sets the whole team free. When you forgive quickly, your team learns it is safe to make mistakes and try again. Grudges poison a group from the inside, but forgiveness keeps the air clean. This week, look for a locked door in your heart. Turn the key. You will be amazed at how light you feel on the other side.

Prayer:

Father, I am holding a grudge, and it feels like handcuffs, heavy, cold, keeping me chained to the past. Today, help me use the key of forgiveness. I choose to release the family member who hurt me, the friend who betrayed me, the boss who wronged me. Unlock the door of my resentment and let Your peace walk in. I pray for everyone locked in hatred tonight. Turn their keys too. Show us that forgiving does not excuse the wrong. It frees the heart that was wronged. Amen.

Study questions

1What happened when the former prison guard asked Corrie ten Boom for forgiveness?

  1. She refused and walked away
  2. She prayed, reached out her hand, and forgave him
  3. She called the police
  4. She pretended not to know him

2According to the lesson, resentment is like:

  1. A fun game
  2. Locking yourself in a prison and throwing away the key
  3. A sign of strength
  4. Something that only hurts the other person

3What does Ephesians 4:32 tell us to do?

  1. Forgive each other, just as in Christ God forgave you
  2. Never forgive serious wrongs
  3. Wait for an apology before forgiving
  4. Forgive only once

4Who does forgiveness set free first?

  1. The person who was forgiven
  2. You, the one who forgives
  3. Nobody
  4. Only the witnesses

5A forgiving leader helps the team because:

  1. The team learns it is safe to make mistakes and try again
  2. The team never has to work hard
  3. Mistakes are never addressed
  4. The leader looks weak

Week 17 · Forgiveness as a Leadership Tool

Tuesday – Stick With Love

"I have decided to stick with love. Hate is too great a burden to bear." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Matthew 6:14-15 — "For if you forgive others their trespasses, your heavenly Father will also forgive you, but if you do not forgive others their trespasses, neither will your Father forgive your trespasses."

Dr. King had every reason to hate. He was beaten, jailed, threatened, and finally murdered for his work. People spit on him and called him terrible names. But he made a decision, not a feeling, but a decision: "I have decided to stick with love." He understood that hate is not just wrong. It is heavy. It is a burden too great to bear. Hate wears you down from the inside until you cannot lead, cannot love, and cannot live in peace.

Forgiveness is how we stick with love. It is the daily decision to put down the heavy bag of hate and pick up the lighter load of love. Jesus makes this very serious in Matthew 6. He says if we forgive others, our Father will forgive us. But if we refuse to forgive, we block our own forgiveness. That is not God being mean. It is God showing us how forgiveness works: it has to flow through us, not just to us. A clogged pipe cannot carry water.

Servant leaders cannot afford to carry hate. You are trying to lift other people up, and you cannot lift anyone while you are weighed down yourself. The volunteer who betrayed your trust. The friend who talked behind your back. The family member who never apologized. Put down those bags, one by one, through forgiveness.

This calls us to be better people because people who stick with love change the temperature of every room they enter. They bring peace instead of tension. They bring hope instead of bitterness. Decide today, like Dr. King did: I will stick with love, no matter what. It is lighter, it is freer, and it is the way of Jesus.

Prayer:

Lord, I have decided to stick with love, but some days hate feels easier. When the insult replays in my mind and revenge sounds sweet, remind me that hate is too heavy a burden to carry. Today, help me lay it down. If I forgive others, You forgive me. Let that promise steady my shaking heart. I pray for everyone choosing between love and hate today. Tip the scales toward love. Keep my feet on love's path, even when it climbs uphill. Amen.

Study questions

1Why did Dr. King decide to stick with love?

  1. He never faced any real hatred
  2. Hate is too great a burden to bear
  3. Love was the popular choice
  4. He was afraid of his enemies

2According to Matthew 6:14-15, if we forgive others:

  1. Nothing happens
  2. Our heavenly Father will also forgive us
  3. People will always apologize
  4. We will never be hurt again

3What happens if we refuse to forgive, according to the verse?

  1. We get stronger
  2. Our Father will not forgive our sins
  3. Everyone respects us more
  4. Life gets easier

4The lesson compares unforgiveness to:

  1. A clogged pipe that cannot carry water
  2. A fast-flowing river
  3. A beautiful garden
  4. A warm blanket

5People who stick with love change every room they enter because they bring:

  1. Tension and fear
  2. Peace instead of tension, hope instead of bitterness
  3. Loud arguments
  4. Silence and distance

Week 17 · Forgiveness as a Leadership Tool

Wednesday – Taught to Love

"No one is born hating another person because of the color of his skin, or his background, or his religion. People must learn to hate, and if they can learn to hate, they can be taught to love, for love comes more naturally to the human heart than its opposite." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): Colossians 3:13 — "bearing with one another and, if one has a complaint against another, forgiving each other; as the Lord has forgiven you, so you also must forgive."

Nelson Mandela spent nearly three decades locked in prison by people who hated him because of his skin color. When he walked free, the whole world held its breath. Would he seek revenge? Would he call his people to hate back? Instead, he called them to forgive. He believed something beautiful: no baby is born hating. Hate has to be taught. And if hate can be taught, then love can be taught too, because love comes more naturally to our hearts.

This is one of the most powerful leadership tools in the world. A leader who forgives teaches everyone watching how to forgive. When Mandela forgave the people who stole 27 years of his life, he taught a whole nation to choose love over revenge. Your forgiveness works the same way on a smaller stage. When you forgive the coworker who wronged you, your team learns that revenge is not the way. When you forgive a family member, your children learn that love is stronger than hate.

Paul tells us in Colossians to forgive as the Lord forgave us. That is the standard. Not forgive when it is easy. Not forgive when they deserve it. Forgive the way Jesus forgave you: fully, freely, and first. He forgave you before you even asked.

This calls us to be better people because every act of forgiveness is a lesson in love for someone watching. You may never know who is learning from you. A young person might be watching how you handle betrayal. A hurting friend might be watching how you handle pain. Teach them well. Show them that love comes naturally to the human heart, and forgiveness is how we set it free.

Prayer:

God, nobody is born hating. Hate is learned, which means love can be learned too. Today, teach me. When irritation rises toward someone different from me, replace it with curiosity, patience, and kindness. Help me bear with the annoying coworker, the difficult in-law, the stranger I do not understand. Forgive my complaints and my cold shoulder. I pray for every heart trained in hatred. Retrain us all in love. Make my life living proof that people really can change. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Mandela, why can people be taught to love?

  1. Because love is forced on everyone
  2. Because hate must be learned, and love comes more naturally to the human heart
  3. Because babies are born loving everyone perfectly
  4. Because hate does not exist

2How many years did Mandela spend in prison before choosing forgiveness over revenge?

  1. 7 years
  2. 17 years
  3. Nearly three decades, 27 years
  4. 37 years

3What does Colossians 3:13 tell us to do?

  1. Forgive only when it is easy
  2. Forgive as the Lord forgave you
  3. Wait for others to forgive first
  4. Keep a record of wrongs

4When a leader forgives, the people watching:

  1. Learn nothing
  2. Learn how to forgive and that revenge is not the way
  3. Think the leader is weak
  4. Stop trusting the leader

5Jesus forgave us:

  1. Only after we earned it
  2. Fully, freely, and before we even asked
  3. Only for small mistakes
  4. After we proved ourselves

Week 17 · Forgiveness as a Leadership Tool

Thursday – Faithful in Small Things

"Be faithful in small things because it is in them that your strength lies." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Matthew 18:21-22 — "Then Peter came up and said to him, ‘Lord, how often will my brother sin against me, and I forgive him? As many as seven times?’ Jesus said to him, ‘I do not say to you seven times, but seventy-seven times.’"

Peter thought he was being generous. The religious teachers of his day said you only had to forgive three times. Peter doubled it and added one more: seven times! He probably expected Jesus to be impressed. Instead, Jesus said seventy-seven times. He was not giving Peter a math problem. He was saying forgiveness is not something you count. It is something you live, over and over, in the small moments of every day.

Mother Teresa understood this. She said our strength lies in being faithful in small things. Forgiveness is mostly a small thing. It is not usually about forgiving a prison guard like Corrie ten Boom did. It is about forgiving the small, daily hurts: the rude comment, the forgotten promise, the interrupted sentence, the credit someone else took for your idea. These little unforgiven moments pile up like small stones in your shoe. Each one is tiny, but together they make every step painful.

A servant leader who practices small forgiveness stays strong. When you let go of the little hurts quickly, they never grow into big bitterness. Your heart stays light. Your team stays healthy. People around you feel the difference because you are not carrying yesterday's stones into today's work.

This calls us to be better people because strength is built in the small, unseen choices. Nobody will applaud you for forgiving a rude comment. Nobody will notice when you let go of a small hurt. But God notices, and your heart knows. Be faithful in the small forgivenesses today, and you will find you have strength for the big ones when they come.

Prayer:

Lord, forgiving once is hard, and forgiving seventy times seven feels impossible. Yet in my marriage, my family, my friendships, the small hurts keep coming. Today, give me strength for the small things: the forgotten promise, the sharp tone, the thoughtless word. Help me forgive quickly and completely, before bitterness takes root. I pray for everyone keeping count of wrongs today. Teach us to lose count and keep loving. Show me that faithfulness in small forgiving is where real strength lives. Amen.

  2. Seven times
  3. Seventy-seven times
  4. Once

2What did Jesus answer Peter?

  1. Three times is enough
  2. Not seven times, but seventy-seven times
  3. Never forgive
  4. Only forgive family

3According to Mother Teresa, where does our strength lie?

  1. In big, impressive acts
  2. In being faithful in small things
  3. In avoiding all problems
  4. In public recognition

4The lesson compares small unforgiven hurts to:

  1. Small stones in your shoe that make every step painful
  2. Beautiful flowers
  3. Pleasant memories
  4. Nothing important

5A leader who practices small forgiveness:

  1. Becomes weaker over time
  2. Stays strong, keeps a light heart, and keeps the team healthy
  3. Gets taken advantage of constantly
  4. Never has to forgive big hurts

Week 17 · Forgiveness as a Leadership Tool

Friday – Light Despite the Darkness

"Hope is being able to see that there is light despite all of the darkness." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): Luke 23:34 — "And Jesus said, ‘Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do.’ And they cast lots to divide his garments."

Desmond Tutu lived through apartheid, one of the darkest chapters in his country's history. He saw cruelty, injustice, and hatred up close. Yet he never lost hope, because hope is seeing light even when darkness is all around you. Forgiveness is one of the brightest lights there is.

Look at Jesus on the cross. It does not get darker than that. He was innocent, yet He was beaten, mocked, and nailed to wood. His friends had run away. The crowd was laughing at Him. And in the middle of that darkness, He prayed the most powerful prayer of forgiveness ever spoken: "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do." He did not wait until the pain stopped. He forgave right in the middle of it.

That is what forgiveness does as a leadership tool. It brings light into the darkest situations. When a team is torn apart by conflict, one person's forgiveness can be the light that starts healing. When a family is broken by old wounds, one person's "I forgive you" can be the dawn after a long night. You do not have to wait until you feel like it. You do not have to wait until they deserve it. Like Jesus, you can forgive in the middle of the pain.

This calls us to be better people because the world is full of darkness, and it is waiting for people who carry light. Every time you forgive, you prove that darkness does not win. You show the people around you that hope is real. Be the light this week. Forgive the unforgivable. Love the unlovable. And watch the darkness run from the light of a forgiving heart.

Prayer:

Father, when Jesus hung on the cross, He prayed for the very people who put Him there. Today, when darkness feels thick, when I have been wronged and no apology is coming, help me see the light anyway. Give me hope that forgiveness is possible even here, even now. I pray for everyone sitting in the dark of bitterness tonight. Shine Your light into their prison. Teach me to pray "forgive them" with my whole heart, and watch the darkness lift. Amen.

  2. Being able to see that there is light despite all of the darkness
  3. Waiting for others to fix things
  4. Avoiding hard situations

  1. "Father, punish them for what they are doing"
  2. "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do"
  3. "Father, save me from this pain"
  4. "Father, I give up"

3When did Jesus forgive the people crucifying Him?

  1. After the pain stopped
  2. Right in the middle of the pain
  3. Years later
  4. He never forgave them

4How does forgiveness work as a leadership tool, according to the lesson?

  1. It punishes the guilty
  2. It brings light into the darkest situations and starts healing
  3. It ignores all problems
  4. It makes the leader look powerful

5The lesson says we do not have to wait to forgive until:

  1. We feel like it or they deserve it
  2. The sun comes up
  3. Someone tells us to

Looking back

This week you learned that forgiveness is a leadership tool, not a feeling. It is the key that unlocks resentment. Stick with love — hate is too great a burden to bear. Be faithful in small things, and let your light shine even in dark places. As God forgave you, forgive others. Next week, you will kneel with Jesus in the Upper Room and watch greatness wear a towel.

Week 18 · The Upper Room and the Basin (John 13)

Monday – Greatness Wears a Towel

Week opening

It was the night before the cross. Jesus knew everything was in His hands — and He got up, wrapped a towel around His waist, and washed His friends' feet. This week you will kneel beside that basin. You will learn that greatness wears a towel, that small acts carry great love, and that we were built for others. ‘If I then, your Lord and Teacher, have washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another's feet.’

"Everybody can be great because everybody can serve." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): John 13:14-15 — "If I then, your Lord and Teacher, have washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another's feet. For I have given you an example, that you also should do just as I have done to you."

Picture the scene. It is the night before Jesus will die. He knows everything that is coming. And what does He do? He gets up from the table, wraps a towel around His waist, pours water into a basin, and starts washing His friends' dirty feet. In that culture, foot-washing was the job of the lowest servant in the house. Nobody with any status would do it. Yet Jesus, the Lord and Teacher, chose the lowest job on purpose.

This is what servant leadership looks like. It is not a title or a position. It is a towel. It is choosing the work nobody else wants to do and doing it with love. In real life, that might look like staying late to clean up after an event when everyone else has gone home. It might look like doing the dishes without being asked, or helping a coworker finish a hard task even though it is not your job. It might look like listening to someone who is lonely when you are tired and want to go to bed.

Here is the part that calls us to be better: Jesus did not wash feet to look good. Nobody was watching except His friends, and one of them would betray Him that very night. He served anyway. That means we serve even when nobody thanks us. We serve even the people who are hard to love. When we pick up the towel, we are following Jesus in the most direct way possible. Greatness is not about being noticed. It is about noticing who needs help and quietly helping them.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, You wrapped a towel around Your waist and washed dusty feet, the job of the lowest servant. Today, give me the same willingness. When the dishes pile up, when the bathroom needs cleaning, when the unglamorous job falls to me, help me do it with joy instead of grumbling. Forgive my hunger for important titles. I pray for everyone doing hidden, lowly work today. Honor them. Teach me that greatness wears a towel, and let me wear mine gladly. Amen.

Week 18 · The Upper Room and the Basin (John 13)

Tuesday – Small Acts, Great Love

"Not all of us can do great things. But we can do small things with great love." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Galatians 5:13 — "For you were called to freedom, brothers. Only do not use your freedom as an opportunity for the flesh, but through love serve one another."

Mother Teresa spent her life caring for the poorest people in Calcutta, India. She did not build hospitals with her own hands or change governments. She held the hands of dying people. She fed hungry children one bowl at a time. She said the world does not need big heroes as much as it needs ordinary people doing small things with great love.

That is exactly what Jesus taught in the upper room. Washing feet was a small thing. It took a few minutes. But it was done with great love, and two thousand years later we are still talking about it. Small acts done with love have a way of growing bigger than we ever imagined.

Think about your own life. You may never preach to thousands or lead a big organization. But you can do small things today. You can text a friend who is going through a hard time and tell them you are thinking of them. You can make an extra plate of food for a neighbor. You can smile at the cashier and ask how their day is going. You can hold the door, give up your seat, or say thank you to someone who rarely hears it.

These things look tiny. But to the person receiving them, they can feel huge. A kind word can change the whole direction of someone's bad day. A small act of service tells a person, "You matter. You are seen. You are loved." That is what it means to serve one another through love. Do not wait until you can do something big. Start small, start today, and do it with all your heart.

Prayer:

God, You set me free, not so I can serve myself, but so I can serve others in love. Today, use my freedom for small acts with great love: the ride I offer, the meal I cook, the hour I give to help a neighbor move. When I am tempted to spend all my free time on me, redirect my feet. I pray for everyone who feels they have nothing to give. Show them their small acts matter. Let my freedom look like love in action, all day long. Amen.

  1. Only great things matter
  2. Small things with great love
  3. Nothing without money
  4. Only what leaders tell them

  1. Do whatever they want
  2. Through love serve one another
  3. Avoid all responsibility
  4. Judge those who do wrong

  2. Texting an encouraging word to a struggling friend
  3. Bragging about your charity work online
  4. Ignoring a lonely coworker

  3. They are easier than big acts
  4. They count more than big acts

  1. Small acts are a waste of time
  2. Only leaders should perform them
  3. A small act done in love can echo for generations
  4. Small acts should be done only once a year

Week 18 · The Upper Room and the Basin (John 13)

Wednesday – Looking Up, Not Down

"A proud man is always looking down on things and people; and, of course, as long as you are looking down, you cannot see something that is above you." — C.S. Lewis
Bible (ESV): Philippians 2:3-5 — "Do nothing from selfish ambition or conceit, but in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others. Have this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus,"

Pride has a way of bending our necks downward. When we think we are better than others, we start looking down on them. We notice their mistakes. We feel annoyed by their weaknesses. We keep score of how much we do compared to everyone else. C.S. Lewis warned that as long as we are looking down, we cannot see what is above us. We miss God, and we miss the good in other people.

Jesus did the opposite in the upper room. He was above everyone in that room. He was the Son of God. He had every right to be served. Instead, He got down low. He counted His disciples more significant than Himself. He looked not only to His own interests, but also to theirs. That is the mind Paul tells us to have: the same mind that is ours in Christ Jesus.

What does this look like in real life? It looks like letting someone else go first, even when you were there first. It looks like admitting you were wrong instead of defending yourself. It looks like asking a quiet person for their opinion because their thoughts matter too. It looks like doing a task well even when someone with less experience gets the credit.

Counting others more significant than yourself does not mean thinking you are worthless. It means thinking of yourself less and thinking of others more. When we stop looking down on people, we start seeing them the way God sees them: precious, loved, and worth serving. That is how we become better people to those around us, one humble choice at a time.

Prayer:

Father, forgive my proud heart. Too often I look down on people, the slow cashier, the struggling student, the person who does not think like me, and while I am looking down, I cannot see You. Today, lift my eyes. Help me count others as more important than myself, the way Jesus did. When pride whispers that I am better, silence it with humility. I pray for everyone crushed by someone's contempt today. Lift their heads. Teach me to look up to You and across at others with love. Amen.

Study questions

1According to C.S. Lewis, what is the problem with looking down on people?

  1. It hurts your neck
  2. You cannot see what is above you
  3. People will look down on you back
  4. It makes you shorter

2Philippians 2:3 tells us to do nothing out of:

  1. Love and kindness
  2. Selfish ambition or conceit
  3. Prayer and fasting
  4. Hard work

3What mind are we told to have in Philippians 2:5?

  1. The mindset of a champion
  2. The same mind as Christ Jesus
  3. The mindset of a ruler
  4. The mindset of a scholar

4Which action shows "valuing others above yourself"?

  1. Taking the biggest portion for yourself
  2. Letting someone else go first
  3. Reminding people of your achievements
  4. Correcting everyone's mistakes publicly

5How did Jesus show humility in the upper room?

  1. He demanded the best seat
  2. He got down low and washed His disciples' feet
  3. He told the disciples to serve Him
  4. He left before the meal ended

Week 18 · The Upper Room and the Basin (John 13)

Thursday – Losing Yourself to Find Yourself

"The best way to find yourself is to lose yourself in the service of others." — Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): Matthew 20:26-28 — "It shall not be so among you. But whoever would be great among you must be your servant, and whoever would be first among you must be your slave, even as the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

Many people spend their whole lives asking, "Who am I? What is my purpose?" They look for the answer in success, in money, in popularity, or in doing whatever makes them happy. But Gandhi pointed to a surprising answer: you find yourself by losing yourself in serving others. Jesus said the same thing long before Gandhi. Whoever would be great must be a servant.

Why does this work? Because we were made in the image of a serving God. Jesus said He came not to be served but to serve. When we serve, we are living the way we were designed to live. It is like a fish finally getting into water. Suddenly everything makes sense. The emptiness fades because we are doing what we were made for.

Think of a time you helped someone and walked away feeling more alive than when you started. Maybe you volunteered at a food bank, or helped a friend move, or spent time with an elderly person who was lonely. You gave your time and energy, but you received something back: purpose, joy, and a quiet knowing that your life matters.

This calls us to be better people because it shifts our focus outward. Instead of asking, "What can I get today?" we ask, "Who can I help today?" That one question changes everything. It changes how we treat our families, our coworkers, and even strangers. A life poured out for others is never a wasted life. It is the fullest life there is.

Prayer:

Lord, I hold my plans so tightly, my schedule, my comfort, my way. Today, teach me to loosen my grip. When someone needs me and it ruins my plans, help me choose them with joy. Show me the strange truth Jesus taught: that losing my life is how I find it. I pray for everyone clinging to control today. Free us. Let me discover the deep happiness that comes from giving myself away, at home, at work, wherever You send me. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Gandhi's quote, how do you find yourself?

  2. By losing yourself in the service of others
  3. By traveling the world alone
  4. By earning as much money as possible

2In Matthew 20:26, Jesus says whoever would be great must be:

  1. A ruler over others
  2. Your servant
  3. Wealthy and famous
  4. Free from all work

3Why did Jesus say He came, according to Matthew 20:28?

  1. To be served by His followers
  2. To serve, and to give His life as a ransom for many
  3. To build an earthly kingdom
  4. To avoid all suffering

4Why does serving others give us a sense of purpose?

  1. Because people will praise us
  2. Because we were made in the image of a serving God
  3. Because it makes us richer
  4. Because it is required by law

  1. "What can I get today?"
  2. "Who can I help today?"
  3. "How can I look better than others?"
  4. "Why should I bother?"
