Week 18 · The Upper Room and the Basin (John 13)

Friday – Built for Others

"The church is the church only when it exists for others." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:43-44 — "But it shall not be so among you. But whoever would be great among you must be your servant, and whoever would be first among you must be slave of all."

Dietrich Bonhoeffer was a German pastor who stood up against great evil during World War II. He believed that a church that only takes care of itself has forgotten what it is for. The church, he said, only truly becomes the church when it exists for others. The same is true for any team, any family, and any individual follower of Jesus. We are at our best when we are turned outward.

Think about the hospitality team at a church. The team brews coffee, sets out tea and water, greets people at the door, and prepares communion. None of those jobs are about the team members themselves. Every single task exists for someone else: the guest who is nervous about visiting, the tired parent who needs a warm smile, the lonely person who needs to feel welcome. When the team remembers it exists for others, even pouring coffee becomes an act of love.

This is the lesson of the basin. Jesus did not wash feet to build His own reputation. He did it for His friends. He was thinking of them, not Himself. When we live for others, we become more like Him.

Being better people to those around us starts with this simple shift: stop asking what the group can do for me, and start asking what I can do for the group. Stop asking what the church can give me, and ask whom the church can serve through me. A life built for others is a life built on the rock. It will stand, it will bless, and it will look like Jesus.

Prayer:

God, forgive me for making church, and my life, all about me: my preferences, my comfort, my growth. You said the church exists for others. Today, turn my eyes outward. Show me the visitor sitting alone in the back row, the new believer full of questions, the neighbor who has never heard Your name. Help me build my days around serving them. I pray for every church tempted to exist for itself. Wake us up. Make my life, like Yours, built for others. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Bonhoeffer, when is the church truly the church?

  1. When it has a beautiful building
  2. When it exists for others
  3. When it has many members
  4. When it follows strict rules

2In Mark 10:44, Jesus says whoever would be first must be:

  1. Slave of all
  2. Leader of all
  3. Richer than all
  4. Praised by all

3Which hospitality task shows "existing for others"?

  1. Greeting a nervous guest with a warm smile
  2. Saving the best seats for yourself
  3. Complaining about the coffee duty
  4. Leaving early before cleanup

4What did Jesus think about while washing feet?

  1. His own comfort
  2. His friends and their needs
  3. How to gain more followers
  4. When the meal would end

5What simple shift makes us more like Jesus?

  1. Asking what the group can do for me
  2. Asking what I can do for the group
  3. Asking for a higher position
  4. Asking others to serve us first
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay Question: Bonhoeffer said the church only truly becomes the church when it exists for others. Think about a group you belong to (your family, your church team, your workplace, or your friends). Write a short paragraph describing one specific way you could help that group become more “for others” this week. What would you do, who would it help, and how does it reflect Jesus washing His disciples’ feet?

Looking back

This week you knelt at the basin in the Upper Room. Greatness wears a towel. Small acts, done with great love, change everything. Look up, not down — lose yourself to find yourself. You were built for others, called to freedom so you can serve in love. Next week, the night grows darker: you will follow Jesus into the garden of Gethsemane.

Week 19 · Gethsemane: Submission to the Father's Will

Monday – Love Deeper Than the Pit

Week opening

In the garden, Jesus fell on His face and prayed, ‘Father, if you are willing, remove this cup from me. Nevertheless, not my will, but yours, be done.’ This week you will sit with Him in His hardest hour. You will learn what submission really looks like — weak body, willing spirit — and how to take one step in the dark when you cannot see the way. Courage with trembling hands still counts as courage.

"There is no pit so deep that God's love is not deeper still." — Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Hebrews 5:7 — "In the days of his flesh, Jesus offered up prayers and supplications, with loud cries and tears, to him who was able to save him from death, and he was heard because of his reverence."

The Garden of Gethsemane was Jesus' darkest night. He knew the cross was coming. The Bible says His soul was very sorrowful, even to death, and He prayed with loud cries and tears. If anyone has ever been in a pit, it was Jesus that night. He felt the full weight of fear, sadness, and dread.

But here is the beautiful truth Corrie ten Boom learned in her own suffering: there is no pit so deep that God's love is not deeper still. Jesus was heard, the verse says, because of His reverence. He did not get rescued from the cross. He got something better: the nearness of the Father in the middle of it. An angel came and strengthened Him. God did not remove the cup, but God never left Him alone in the garden.

This matters for us because we all visit dark gardens. Maybe yours is a hospital waiting room, a broken relationship, a season of depression, or a worry that keeps you up at night. In those moments it can feel like God is far away. But the story of Gethsemane tells us He is closer than ever. His love goes deeper than your deepest pit.

A servant leader does not pretend to have no fears. Jesus Himself cried and trembled. What makes Him our example is that He brought His fears to the Father instead of running away. When we do the same, we find strength to keep going, and we become people who can sit with others in their dark gardens too. We can say with honesty, "I know what it feels like. And I know God is still here."

Prayer:

Lord, I am in a pit right now, grief, fear, pain that will not lift. But Your love is deeper still. Today, when I cannot feel You, help me trust You. Like Jesus in Gethsemane, I bring You my loud cries and my tears; hold them gently. I pray for everyone in their own deep pit tonight, the hospital room, the empty house, the anxious mind. Reach down with Your deeper love. Remind us that no pit is too deep for You. Amen.

  2. He offered up prayers and supplications with loud cries and tears
  3. He pretended nothing was wrong
  4. He blamed His disciples

2According to Corrie ten Boom's quote, how deep is God's love?

  1. Only as deep as our happiness
  2. Deeper than any pit we fall into
  3. Too shallow to reach us
  4. Only for people who never struggle

3Why was Jesus "heard" by the Father?

  1. Because He demanded a miracle
  2. Because of His reverence
  3. Because He was never afraid
  4. Because the disciples prayed for Him

4What does Gethsemane teach us about dark times?

  1. God abandons us when we are afraid
  2. Strong believers never cry
  3. God is near to us even in our deepest pain
  4. Prayer does not help

5How can Jesus' example help us care for hurting people?

  1. By telling them to stop crying
  2. By sitting with them, since we know God is near in dark times
  3. By avoiding sad people
  4. By pretending their pain is not real

Week 19 · Gethsemane: Submission to the Father's Will

Tuesday – The Painful Best

"We are not necessarily doubting that God will do the best for us; we are wondering how painful the best will turn out to be." — C.S. Lewis
Bible (ESV): Luke 22:42 — "saying, 'Father, if you are willing, remove this cup from me. Nevertheless, not my will, but yours, be done.'"

Jesus knew the Father's plan was the best plan. He never doubted that. What made Him sweat drops like blood in the garden was knowing how painful that best plan would be. The cross was coming, and He could feel it. C.S. Lewis put words to the feeling many of us know: we trust God, but we are honest enough to wonder how much it is going to hurt.

This is one of the most human moments in the whole Bible. Jesus did not pretend the cup was easy to drink. He asked, "Father, if you are willing, remove this cup from me." He was honest about His pain. And then He said the words that changed everything: "Nevertheless, not my will, but yours, be done." He chose the Father's will over His own comfort.

Submission does not mean we enjoy the pain. It means we trust the Person who allows it. Think of a parent who holds a child still for a painful shot at the doctor. The child does not understand, and it hurts. But the parent knows the shot will protect the child from something worse. In the same way, God sometimes leads us through painful things because He sees what we cannot see.

How does this call us to be better people? First, it teaches us to be honest with God. We can tell Him we are scared. Second, it teaches us to trust Him anyway. And third, it makes us gentle with others who are suffering. Instead of judging someone who is struggling to obey God, we can remember Gethsemane and say, "I understand. This is hard. But God is good, and He will carry you through."

Prayer:

Father, I believe You want the best for me, but honestly, I am wondering how painful the best will turn out to be. The diagnosis, the job loss, the broken dream: they hurt. Like Jesus praying "not my will, but Yours," I surrender my plans today. When the cup does not pass, hold my hand through drinking it. I pray for everyone facing a painful best today. Give us grace to trust You in the dark. Turn our tears into testimony. Amen.

Study questions

1What did C.S. Lewis say we wonder about God's best plan?

  1. Whether God really has a plan
  2. How painful the best will turn out to be
  3. Whether we deserve it
  4. How fast it will happen

2In Luke 22:42, what did Jesus pray?

  1. "My will be done no matter what"
  2. "Father, if you are willing, remove this cup from me. Nevertheless, not my will, but yours, be done"
  3. "I refuse to suffer"
  4. "Let the disciples suffer instead"

3What does Jesus' prayer teach us about submission?

  1. We must enjoy pain to please God
  2. We can be honest about pain and still trust God's will
  3. We should never tell God how we feel
  4. God's will is always easy

4Why does God sometimes allow painful things, based on this lesson?

  1. Because He does not care about us
  2. Because He sees what we cannot see, like a parent giving a child a needed shot
  3. Because He wants to punish us
  4. Because He is not paying attention

5How should Jesus' example change the way we treat suffering people?

  1. We should judge them for struggling
  2. We should be gentle, remembering how hard obedience can be
  3. We should tell them to stop complaining
  4. We should avoid them

Week 19 · Gethsemane: Submission to the Father's Will

Wednesday – One Step in the Dark

"Faith is taking the first step even when you don't see the whole staircase." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Matthew 26:39 — "And going a little farther he fell on his face and prayed, saying, 'My Father, if it be possible, let this cup pass from me; nevertheless, not as I will, but as you will.'"

In Gethsemane, Jesus could not see past the cross. He knew the suffering that was coming, but the resurrection was still hidden from His human eyes in that dark moment. He did not have the whole staircase in view. He only had the next step: pray, surrender, and trust. So He took it. He fell on His face, prayed honestly, and said, "Nevertheless, not as I will, but as you will."

That is what faith looks like. Faith is not having all the answers. It is not seeing the whole plan laid out like a map. Faith is taking the first step when you can only see one step. Martin Luther King Jr. knew this well. When he marched for justice, he could not see how it would all turn out. He just took the next faithful step, and then the next.

Most of us want the whole staircase. We want God to show us how everything will work out before we obey. We want to know the ending before we take the first step. But God usually gives us just enough light for the step we are on. He asks us to trust Him with the rest.

This calls us to be better people in two ways. First, we learn to obey God one step at a time, even when we are afraid. Maybe that step is forgiving someone, or telling the truth, or volunteering when you feel unqualified. Second, we become encouragers to others who are stuck. When someone is frozen with fear about the future, we can gently say, "You don't have to see the whole staircase. Just take the first step. God will show you the next one."

Prayer:

God, I cannot see the whole staircase, just the next dark step. A move, a hard conversation, a new beginning: it frightens me. Today, give me faith for one step. Like Jesus walking toward the cross in the dark, help me walk toward what You ask, trusting You see what I cannot. When my imagination runs wild with worst cases, quiet it with Your presence. I pray for everyone frozen at the edge of a dark step today. Take our hands. Walk us through, one step at a time. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Martin Luther King Jr., what is faith?

  1. Seeing the whole staircase clearly
  2. Taking the first step even when you don't see the whole staircase
  3. Never feeling afraid
  4. Knowing all the answers in advance

2In Matthew 26:39, how did Jesus pray in the garden?

  1. Standing tall with confidence
  2. Falling with His face to the ground
  3. Silently without moving
  4. Walking away from the disciples

3What did Jesus NOT have in Gethsemane?

  1. Honest prayers
  2. Trust in the Father
  3. A full view of everything beyond the cross
  4. Sorrow

4What does God usually give us, according to this lesson?

  1. The entire plan in advance
  2. Just enough light for the step we are on
  3. No guidance at all
  4. A guarantee of no pain

5How can we encourage someone who is frozen with fear?

  1. Tell them to figure out the whole future first
  2. Remind them they only need to take the first faithful step
  3. Tell them faith means never feeling afraid
  4. Advise them to wait until they feel ready

Week 19 · Gethsemane: Submission to the Father's Will

Thursday – Weak Body, Willing Spirit

"Strength does not come from physical capacity. It comes from an indomitable will." — Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): 2 Corinthians 12:9 — "But he said to me, 'My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness.' Therefore I will boast all the more gladly of my weaknesses, so that the power of Christ may rest upon me."

In the garden, Jesus told His disciples, "The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak." He knew exactly how they felt. His own body was shaking under the weight of what was coming. His spirit was fully surrendered to the Father, but His flesh, His human body and emotions, felt weak and afraid. Jesus understands the gap between wanting to do right and feeling strong enough to do it.

Gandhi said true strength does not come from muscles or physical capacity. It comes from a will that refuses to give up. Jesus showed that kind of strength in Gethsemane. His strength was not in feeling no fear. His strength was in His surrendered will: "Not my will, but Yours be done." And God met Him in His weakness. An angel came and strengthened Him. God's power was made perfect in His weakness.

This is good news for every one of us. You do not have to feel strong to be strong. You do not have to wait until you feel brave to obey God. His grace is enough for you right where you are, weakness and all. When you feel too tired to keep serving, too hurt to keep forgiving, or too afraid to take the next step, that is exactly where God's power shows up best.

How does this make us better people? It makes us honest. We can stop pretending we have it all together. It makes us dependent on God instead of ourselves. And it makes us compassionate, because when we know what weakness feels like, we stop judging others for theirs. Instead of looking down on someone who is struggling, we can say, "Me too. Let's lean on God's strength together."

Prayer:

Lord, my body is weak today, tired, aching, running on too little sleep. The kids still need me, the work still waits, and I feel empty. But Your power is made perfect in my weakness. Fill my willing spirit with Your strength. Help me be gentle when I am exhausted and patient when I am worn thin. I pray for every weary caregiver, every sick worker, every tired parent tonight. Sustain them. Let Your grace carry us where our bodies cannot. Amen.

Study questions

1What did Jesus say about His disciples in the garden?

  1. "You are all very strong"
  2. "The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak"
  3. "You will never struggle"
  4. "Go home and rest"

2According to Gandhi, where does true strength come from?

  1. Physical capacity and muscles
  2. An indomitable will that refuses to give up
  3. Never feeling afraid
  4. Avoiding all hardship

3What does 2 Corinthians 12:9 promise?

  1. God will remove all weakness
  2. God's grace is sufficient and His power is made perfect in weakness
  3. Strong people do not need God
  4. Weakness is a sin

4How did God strengthen Jesus in the garden?

  1. He removed the cross from the plan
  2. An angel came and strengthened Him
  3. He made Jesus feel no emotions
  4. He woke the disciples to fight

5How should our own weakness change the way we treat struggling people?

  1. We should judge them for being weak
  2. We should stop pretending and show compassion, leaning on God together
  3. We should hide our weakness from everyone
  4. We should only help strong people

Week 19 · Gethsemane: Submission to the Father's Will

Friday – Courage With Trembling Hands

"I learned that courage was not the absence of fear, but the triumph over it." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): John 14:27 — "Peace I leave with you; my peace I give to you. Not as the world gives do I give to you. Let not your hearts be troubled, neither let them be afraid."

Nelson Mandela spent 27 years in prison for standing against injustice. He knew fear well. He said the brave person is not the one who feels no fear, but the one who conquers it. Jesus proved this in Gethsemane. He said, "My soul is very sorrowful, even to death." His hands may well have trembled. But He still prayed, still surrendered, and still walked toward the cross. That is courage: trembling hands that keep holding on to God.

Many of us think courage means feeling confident. We wait to act until the fear goes away. But the fear may never go away, and waiting means we never move. Jesus shows us a better way. He felt the fear fully and obeyed anyway. His peace did not come from the absence of trouble. It came from the presence of the Father. That is the peace He promises us in John 14:27, a peace the world cannot give and the world cannot take away.

This calls us to be better people to those around us in a powerful way. When we are honest about our own fears, we give others permission to be honest about theirs. A leader who says, "I was scared too, but God carried me," is far more helpful than one who pretends to be fearless. Our courage can become someone else's comfort.

So do not wait until you feel brave. Take the trembling hands you have, lift them to God in prayer, and take the next step. Courage is not the absence of fear. It is fear that has been handed to God, and hands that keep serving anyway.

Prayer:

God, I am afraid, and my hands tremble at what I must do: the apology I owe, the truth I must tell, the stand I must take. But courage is not the absence of fear; it is going forward anyway. Fill my trembling hands with Your peace, the peace Jesus promised, not the world's. Steady me. I pray for everyone facing something frightening today. Turn their fear into triumph. Let me do the scary right thing, trembling and all, trusting You hold me. Amen.

  2. The triumph over fear, not the absence of it
  3. Avoiding all danger
  4. Pretending to be confident

  2. He felt deep sorrow yet still surrendered and obeyed
  3. He escaped before the soldiers came
  4. He fought His way out

  3. Peace only for people with no problems
  4. Temporary peace

If you cannot think of a personal example, describe how you would encourage a friend who is too afraid to take a faithful next step.

Looking back

This week you knelt with Jesus in Gethsemane. You saw love deeper than the pit and learned that the painful best is still the best. Weak body, willing spirit — take one step in the dark and trust the Father. Courage with trembling hands is still courage. ‘Not my will, but yours.’ Next week, you will stand with Jesus in the trials — silent under false accusation.

Week 20 · The Trials: Silence Under False Accusation

Monday – No Payback

Week opening

They lied about Him. They struck Him. They mocked Him. And Jesus remained silent. ‘When he was reviled, he did not revile in return; when he suffered, he did not threaten.’ This week you will learn the hardest lesson of all: no payback. You will drop the poison of revenge, fight hate with love, and find courage at the testing point. The silent Lamb shows us that true strength does not need to shout.

"An eye for an eye only ends up making the whole world blind." — Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): 1 Peter 2:23 — "When he was reviled, he did not revile in return; when he suffered, he did not threaten, but continued entrusting himself to him who judges justly."

During His trials, Jesus was lied about, insulted, spit on, and beaten. False witnesses twisted His words. The religious leaders who should have recognized Him treated Him like a criminal. He had every right to fight back. With one word He could have called down angels. Instead, He did nothing to get even. When He was reviled, He did not revile in return. When He suffered, He did not threaten. He entrusted Himself to God.

Gandhi saw the same truth: if everyone pays back hurt with hurt, the whole world ends up blind. Revenge feels good for a moment, but it never heals anything. It just spreads the pain around. Jesus broke the cycle. He absorbed the hate and answered with silence and trust.

This is one of the hardest lessons in the Christian life. When someone lies about you, gossips about you, or treats you unfairly, everything inside you wants payback. You want to defend yourself, set the record straight, or make them feel what you felt. Jesus shows us another way: entrust yourself to the One who judges justly. That does not mean we never tell the truth or never seek justice. It means we do not take revenge into our own hands. We leave the final judgment to God.

How does this make us better people? It frees us. The person who must have payback is a prisoner of the person who hurt them. The person who entrusts the hurt to God is free. And it shows the world something beautiful: a person who has been wronged yet refuses to become bitter looks exactly like Jesus.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, when they insulted You, You did not insult back; when You suffered, You did not threaten. Today, when the coworker mocks me, the driver cuts me off, or the family member twists my words, help me choose no payback. Swallow my sharp reply before it leaves my mouth. Trust You to make things right. I pray for everyone nursing revenge fantasies tonight. Release them. Teach me that refusing to retaliate is not weakness. It is walking in Your footsteps. Amen.

  1. Justice is perfectly served
  2. The whole world ends up blind
  3. People become happier
  4. Nothing changes

  1. He retaliated with harsher insults
  2. He did not retaliate
  3. He ran away
  4. He called down angels to punish them

3What did Jesus do instead of seeking revenge?

  1. He forgot about it immediately
  2. He entrusted Himself to Him who judges justly
  3. He planned His comeback
  4. He told His disciples to fight

4Why does refusing revenge make us free?

  1. Because the other person always apologizes
  2. Because we are no longer prisoners of the person who hurt us
  3. Because revenge is illegal
  4. Because people will admire us

5Does "no payback" mean we can never tell the truth or seek justice?

  1. Yes, we must stay silent about everything
  2. No, it means we do not take revenge into our own hands but leave final judgment to God
  3. Yes, justice does not matter
  4. No, it means we should get even quietly

Week 20 · The Trials: Silence Under False Accusation

Tuesday – Courage at the Testing Point

"Courage is not simply one of the virtues but the form of every virtue at the testing point." — C.S. Lewis
Bible (ESV): Matthew 26:63 — "But Jesus remained silent. And the high priest said to him, 'I adjure you by the living God, tell us if you are the Christ, the Son of God.'"

There is a moment in every virtue when it gets tested. Love is tested when someone is unlovable. Patience is tested when everything goes wrong. And courage is tested when staying quiet costs you everything. C.S. Lewis said courage is what every virtue looks like at its testing point. Jesus showed this during His trial. Falsely accused, surrounded by enemies, He remained silent. That silence took more courage than any speech.

We often think courage means speaking up loudly. Sometimes it does. But sometimes the bravest thing is to stay quiet when every part of you wants to argue, defend, and explain. Jesus knew who He was. He did not need the high priest's approval. He did not need to win the argument. He trusted His Father's plan, and that trust gave Him the strength to be still.

In real life, we face smaller testing points every day. Someone criticizes our work unfairly. A family member misunderstands our motives. A coworker takes credit for what we did. In those moments, we have a choice: explode with angry words, or respond with quiet strength. Not every accusation deserves an answer. Not every criticism needs a defense. Sometimes the most powerful response is calm trust.

This calls us to be better people because it teaches us self-control. Words spoken in anger can wound people for years. Silence rooted in trust can disarm a conflict. When we learn to be still like Jesus, we become safe people for others to be around. We show them that our worth does not depend on winning every argument, but on belonging to God.

Prayer:

God, I am at my testing point, accused, misunderstood, pressured to defend myself with angry words. Like Jesus standing silent before His accusers, give me the courage to stay steady. Keep my voice calm and my heart clean. Help me trust that You see the truth even when no one else does. I pray for everyone falsely accused today. Be their defender. Teach me that sometimes the bravest thing is not to argue, but to stand quiet and let You fight for me. Amen.

  1. A separate virtue from all others
  2. The form of every virtue at the testing point
  3. Only needed by soldiers
  4. The same as being loud

  1. He argued loudly
  2. He remained silent
  3. He called His disciples for help
  4. He escaped the courtroom

  1. He did not care what happened
  2. He knew who He was and trusted His Father's plan
  3. He had no answer to give
  4. He was afraid to speak

4Which situation is a "testing point" for patience and courage?

  1. Being praised for good work
  2. A coworker taking credit for what you did
  3. Receiving a gift
  4. Taking a vacation

  1. It proves we are always right
  2. It keeps us from wounding others with angry words and makes us safe to be around
  3. It shows we do not care
  4. It helps us win every argument

Week 20 · The Trials: Silence Under False Accusation

Wednesday – Dropping the Poison

"Resentment is like drinking poison and then hoping it will kill your enemies." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): Romans 12:19 — "Beloved, never avenge yourselves, but leave it to the wrath of God, for it is written, 'Vengeance is mine, I will repay, says the Lord.'"

Nelson Mandela spent 27 years in prison because of injustice. If anyone had a reason for resentment, it was him. Yet when he walked free, he chose not to seek revenge. He understood something powerful: holding on to resentment is like drinking poison and hoping your enemy dies. The only person it destroys is you.

Jesus lived this truth during His trials. He was beaten by soldiers, mocked by crowds, and condemned by leaders who knew He was innocent. He had every reason to be bitter. But there was no bitterness in Him. On the cross He would pray, "Father, forgive them." He refused to drink the poison.

Resentment is sneaky. It starts as a small hurt. We replay what someone did. We imagine what we wish we had said. Slowly the hurt hardens into a grudge, and the grudge starts to poison everything: our sleep, our joy, our relationships, even our health. We think we are punishing the person who hurt us, but they have moved on with their life. We are the ones still drinking the poison.

God gives us a better way in Romans 12:19. We do not have to pretend the hurt did not happen. We do not have to say it was okay. We simply hand the judgment over to God and say, "This is Yours now, not mine." That is not weakness. That is wisdom. It sets us free from carrying a weight we were never meant to carry, and it makes us kinder, lighter people to be around.

Prayer:

Lord, I have been drinking poison, replaying the hurt, rehearsing what I would say, hoping they feel my pain. But the poison only kills me. Today, help me set the cup down. I leave vengeance to You and choose to let it go. When the bitter thoughts return tonight, remind me I already dropped them. I pray for everyone poisoned by resentment. Heal them. Fill the empty space where anger lived with Your peace, and teach my heart to rest. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Mandela, what is resentment like?

  1. Drinking poison and hoping it will kill your enemies
  2. A healthy way to process hurt
  3. Something that only affects others
  4. Easy to carry

2What does Romans 12:19 tell us not to do?

  1. Tell the truth
  2. Take revenge
  3. Pray for others
  4. Trust God

3Who does God say vengeance belongs to?

  1. Our friends
  2. The government
  3. Himself: "Vengeance is mine, I will repay, says the Lord"
  4. No one

4How does resentment harm us, according to this lesson?

  1. It has no effect on us
  2. It poisons our sleep, joy, relationships, and health while the other person moves on
  3. It makes us stronger
  4. It punishes the person who hurt us

5What does handing judgment over to God require us to do?

  1. Pretend the hurt never happened
  2. Say the wrong was okay
  3. Trust God with justice instead of carrying revenge ourselves
  4. Confront the person angrily

Week 20 · The Trials: Silence Under False Accusation

Thursday – Fighting Hate With Love

"Darkness cannot drive out darkness; only light can do that. Hate cannot drive out hate; only love can do that." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Matthew 5:44 — "But I say to you, Love your enemies and pray for those who persecute you,"

When Jesus stood trial, He was surrounded by hate. The religious leaders hated Him. The crowd that shouted for His death hated Him. The soldiers who beat Him treated Him with cruelty. If hate could have been defeated by hate, Jesus had the power to do it. Instead, He chose love. He did not curse His accusers. He prayed for them. He proved Dr. King's words: only light can drive out darkness, and only love can drive out hate.

This goes against everything our instincts tell us. When someone hates us, we want to hate them back. When someone is cruel, we want to be cruel in return. It feels like justice. But hate never heals hate. It only makes the darkness darker. Think of a fire: you cannot put out a fire by throwing more fire on it. You need water. Love is the water that puts out the fire of hate.

Jesus took this further than just a feeling. He said to love our enemies and pray for those who persecute us. Praying for someone who hurt you is one of the hardest things you will ever do. But it is also one of the most freeing. When you pray for your enemy, something changes in your heart. You start to see them as a person God loves, not just as a person who hurt you.

How does this make us better people? It makes us peacemakers. In a world full of arguing, outrage, and division, a person who answers hate with love stands out like a light in a dark room. You may not change your enemy, but you will change yourself. And sometimes, by God's grace, love does what hate never could: it melts a hard heart.

Prayer:

God, someone in my life feels like an enemy right now, and hate is knocking on my heart's door. But hate cannot drive out hate; only love can. Today, teach me to love my enemy the way Jesus commanded: pray for the bully, bless the critic, do good to the one who hurt me. When my feelings refuse, let my actions lead. I pray for every heart full of hate tonight. Flood it with light. Turn my enemies into friends, starting with my own changed heart. Amen.

  4. Stronger insults

  3. Hate those who hate us
  4. Only love people who love us

  2. Because hate only makes the darkness darker, like throwing fire on a fire
  3. Because love is weak
  4. Because God forbids all conflict

  2. We start to see them as a person God loves, not just someone who hurt us
  3. We become angrier
  4. We forget what happened

  2. He called for revenge
  3. He did not curse them but prayed for them

Week 20 · The Trials: Silence Under False Accusation

Friday – The Silent Lamb

"Don't raise your voice, improve your argument." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): Isaiah 53:7 — "He was oppressed, and he was afflicted, yet he opened not his mouth; like a lamb that is led to the slaughter, and like a sheep that before its shearers is silent, so he opened not his mouth."

Hundreds of years before Jesus was born, the prophet Isaiah described Him as a lamb led to slaughter, silent before its shearers. On the day of His trials, that prophecy came true. Jesus was oppressed, and He was afflicted, yet He opened not His mouth to defend Himself. Like a lamb, He was quiet. Not because He was weak, but because He was strong enough to trust His Father completely.

Desmond Tutu's words fit this moment in a surprising way. Jesus did not raise His voice. He did not need to. His whole life was the argument. His love, His miracles, His kindness to the poor, His forgiveness of sinners, all of it spoke louder than any courtroom speech could. When the truth of who you are is clear, you do not need to shout.

There is a deep dignity in this kind of silence. It says, "I know who I am. I know who my Father is. I do not need your verdict to tell me my worth." Most of us get our sense of worth from what people think of us. So when people think badly of us, we panic and start shouting our defense. Jesus shows us a better foundation: our worth comes from God, and no false accusation can take it away.

This calls us to be better people by teaching us restraint and confidence. Restraint, because we learn to control our tongues when we are attacked. Confidence, because we learn that God's opinion of us matters more than anyone else's. A person who is secure in God's love does not need to win every argument. Like the silent Lamb, they can be still, and let their life do the talking.

Prayer:

Lord, like a lamb before its shearers, Jesus opened not His mouth. Today, when the argument heats up and my voice wants to rise, help me stay silent, not sulking, but peaceful. Improve my argument by improving my love. When I am tempted to win with words, teach me to win with gentleness. I pray for every home filled with shouting tonight. Bring quiet. Show me that silence under pressure is not defeat; it is the strength of the Lamb living in me. Amen.

  1. Stay silent forever
  2. Improve our argument
  3. Give up
  4. Shout louder

  2. His whole life of love and kindness already spoke for Him
  3. He had no supporters
  4. He was not allowed to speak

  1. People's opinions of us
  2. Winning every argument
  3. God, and no false accusation can take it away
  4. Our achievements

  1. To never speak at all
  2. Restraint with our words and confidence in God's love
  3. To ignore all problems

Then describe one specific way you want to respond more like the silent Lamb the next time you face unfair criticism.

Courage at the testing point looks like silence, not shouting. The Lamb was silent, and His silence shook the world. Next week, you will stand at the foot of the cross.

Week 21 · The Crucifixion: Forgiving from the Cross

Monday – Forgive Anyway

Week opening

Nails in His hands, thorns on His head, mockers at His feet — and Jesus prayed, ‘Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do.’ This week you will stand at the cross and learn the deepest lesson of servant leadership: forgive anyway. You will find the power to forgive, the strength to forgive when it is hard, and the love that changes enemies into brothers. Forgiveness is an act of the will — and the will can choose.

"Forgiveness is an act of the will, and the will can function regardless of the temperature of the heart." — Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Luke 23:34 — "And Jesus said, 'Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do.' And they cast lots to divide his garments."

Corrie ten Boom knew about forgiveness. She survived a Nazi concentration camp where her sister died. After the war, she met one of her former guards face to face, and he asked her to forgive him. Her heart felt cold. Every feeling in her screamed no. But she chose to extend her hand anyway. She later wrote that forgiveness is an act of the will, and the will can work even when the heart feels cold.

Jesus proved this on the cross. Nailed to wood, bleeding, mocked by the crowd, He prayed, "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do." He did not feel warm toward the soldiers. He chose to forgive them. Forgiveness was not a feeling for Jesus. It was a decision.

This changes everything about how we forgive. Most of us wait until we feel like forgiving. We wait for the anger to fade, for the apology to come, for our hearts to warm up. But feelings are slow, and sometimes they never come. Jesus shows us we do not have to wait. We can choose to forgive as an act of the will, trusting God to warm our hearts later.

What does this look like in real life? It looks like saying, "I forgive you," even when your voice shakes. It looks like praying for someone who hurt you even when you do not feel like it. It looks like refusing to bring up the old hurt in the next argument. You may not feel the forgiveness fully yet, and that is okay. The will can lead, and the heart will follow. That is how we become better people: not by waiting for perfect feelings, but by making brave choices like Jesus did.

Prayer:

Father, they do not deserve my forgiveness, and that is exactly the point. Jesus forgave from the cross while they were still driving the nails. Today, help me forgive anyway: the debt unpaid, the promise broken, the wound still fresh. My will can choose what my heart cannot yet feel. I pray for everyone waiting for an apology that will never come. Free them to forgive first. Set my heart free as I set them free. Teach me that forgiveness is my choice, not their earning. Amen.

  3. Something only possible after an apology
  4. Forgetting what happened

  1. "Father, punish them for what they did"
  2. "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do"
  3. "Father, save me from this pain"
  4. Nothing at all

3What was Corrie ten Boom's experience with forgiveness?

  1. She never forgave anyone
  2. She forgave a former prison guard even though her heart felt cold
  3. She only forgave her friends
  4. She waited until she felt like forgiving

4Why don't we have to wait for warm feelings before forgiving?

  1. Because feelings do not matter at all
  2. Because forgiveness is a choice of the will, and the heart can follow later
  3. Because the other person deserves it
  4. Because God forces us to feel happy

  1. Waiting years until the anger fades on its own

Week 21 · The Crucifixion: Forgiving from the Cross

Tuesday – The Power to Forgive

"We must develop and maintain the capacity to forgive. He who is devoid of the power to forgive is devoid of the power to love." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Colossians 3:13 — "bearing with one another and, if one has a complaint against another, forgiving each other; as the Lord has forgiven you, so you also must forgive."

Dr. King said something striking: if you cannot forgive, you cannot truly love. Forgiveness is not just a nice extra in the Christian life. It is the engine that makes love possible. Without it, every relationship eventually breaks down, because every person you love will hurt you at some point. Husbands and wives, parents and children, friends, coworkers — all of them will let you down. The only question is whether you will forgive.

Jesus forgave from the cross, and He calls us to do the same. Paul writes that we must forgive each other "as the Lord has forgiven you." Think about how much God has forgiven you. Every wrong thing you have ever done, every selfish choice, every unkind word — all forgiven because of Jesus. When we remember how much we have been forgiven, forgiving others becomes not just possible but natural. How can we hold a small debt against someone when God cancelled our enormous one?

Developing the capacity to forgive is like building a muscle. It gets stronger with practice. Start with small things. Forgive the driver who cut you off. Forgive the friend who forgot your birthday. Forgive the family member whose comment stung. Each small act of forgiveness builds your capacity for the bigger ones.

This calls us to be better people because unforgiveness poisons us from the inside. A person who cannot forgive becomes bitter, suspicious, and lonely. A person who forgives becomes free, warm, and full of love. Choose forgiveness, and you choose love. Choose love, and you look like Jesus.

Prayer:

Lord, I feel powerless to forgive. The complaint keeps replaying, the irritation keeps rising. But You say forgiveness is power, and its absence leaves me powerless. Today, fill me with Your power to release the coworker who wronged me, the friend who disappointed me. Help me bear with others the way You bear with me. I pray for everyone who feels devoid of the power to forgive. Empower them. Let my forgiving heart prove that Your strength lives in me. Amen.

  3. They become stronger
  4. Nothing happens

  2. As the Lord has forgiven us
  3. Only small offenses
  4. Only after the person earns it

  1. It makes our hurts seem smaller by comparison
  2. It reminds us how much we were forgiven, making it natural to extend forgiveness
  3. It excuses the other person's behavior
  4. It means we do not need to forgive

4How is forgiveness like a muscle?

  1. It gets weaker the more you use it
  2. It gets stronger with practice, starting with small hurts
  3. Only athletes need it
  4. It works without any effort

Week 21 · The Crucifixion: Forgiving from the Cross

Wednesday – Strong Enough to Forgive

"The weak can never forgive. Forgiveness is the attribute of the strong." — Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): Matthew 6:14-15 — "For if you forgive others their trespasses, your heavenly Father will also forgive you, but if you do not forgive others their trespasses, neither will your Father forgive your trespasses."

Many people think forgiveness is weakness. They think it means letting someone walk all over you, or saying what they did was okay. Gandhi saw the opposite truth: only the strong can forgive. It takes no strength to hold a grudge. Anyone can stay angry. But it takes real strength to look at someone who hurt you deeply and choose to release them.

Jesus showed this strength on the cross. It would have been easy for Him to curse His killers. He had all power. Instead, He used His strength to forgive. That is what makes the cross so powerful. It was not weakness that kept Jesus on that cross. It was strength, love strong enough to forgive the very people driving the nails.

Jesus also taught something serious in Matthew 6:14-15. Our forgiveness of others is tied to God's forgiveness of us. This does not mean we earn God's forgiveness by forgiving others. It means a heart that has truly received God's forgiveness will naturally overflow with forgiveness toward others. If we refuse to forgive, it may show we have not really understood how much we have been forgiven.

What does strong forgiveness look like in daily life? It looks like a wife forgiving a husband who spoke harshly, not because it did not hurt, but because love is stronger than pride. It looks like a worker forgiving a boss who was unfair, and still doing excellent work. It looks like forgiving a parent for old wounds, and choosing to build something new. This kind of strength does not come from us. It comes from God. Ask Him for it, and He will give it.

Prayer:

God, they say the weak can never forgive, that forgiveness belongs to the strong. Today, make me strong. Give me the strength to forgive my spouse's harsh words, my friend's betrayal, and my own repeated failures. When I forgive others, You forgive me. Let that promise make me brave. I pray for everyone who thinks they are too weak to forgive. Strengthen them. Prove in my life today that forgiveness is not for the soft, but for the strong. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Gandhi, who can forgive?

  1. Only the weak
  2. Only the strong
  3. Only perfect people
  4. No one

2Why is forgiveness a sign of strength, not weakness?

  1. Because it lets people walk over you
  2. Because anyone can stay angry, but it takes real strength to release someone who hurt you
  3. Because it means the hurt did not matter
  4. Because strong people never get hurt

3In Matthew 6:14, what does Jesus promise those who forgive others?

  1. They will become famous
  2. Their heavenly Father will also forgive them
  3. They will never be hurt again
  4. They will receive money

4How did Jesus show strength on the cross?

  1. By escaping His suffering
  2. By using His strength to forgive the people hurting Him
  3. By fighting His enemies
  4. By calling down angels to punish them

5Where does the strength to forgive deeply come from?

  1. From our own willpower alone
  2. From God, when we ask Him
  3. From ignoring the pain
  4. From waiting until we forget

Week 21 · The Crucifixion: Forgiving from the Cross

Thursday – When Forgiving Is Hard

"Everyone says forgiveness is a lovely idea, until they have something to forgive." — C.S. Lewis
Bible (ESV): Ephesians 4:32 — "Be kind to one another, tenderhearted, forgiving one another, as God in Christ forgave you."

C.S. Lewis was honest about something we all feel. Forgiveness sounds beautiful when we talk about it in general. We nod along in church. We agree it is a lovely idea. But then someone actually hurts us, deeply and personally, and suddenly forgiveness does not feel lovely at all. It feels impossible. Lewis knew that gap between the idea and the reality, and he named it honestly.

Jesus closed that gap on the cross. He did not just teach forgiveness as a lovely idea. He lived it in the hardest possible moment. Betrayed by a friend, denied by another, abandoned by almost everyone, beaten and crucified, He still said, "Father, forgive them." If anyone had a reason to say forgiveness was too hard, it was Jesus. Instead, He made it real.

Paul tells us in Ephesians 4:32 to be kind, tenderhearted, and forgiving, "as God in Christ forgave you." Notice the order. Kindness comes first. Before we can forgive the big hurts, we practice being kind in small moments. Tenderheartedness comes next. We let our hearts stay soft instead of hardening them with bitterness. Then forgiveness flows more naturally, because it grows out of a kind and tender heart.

So how do we become better people when forgiving feels hard? We start where we are. We admit to God that it is hard. We ask Him to make our hearts kind and tender. We take one small step: a prayer for the person, a refusal to speak badly about them, a decision not to replay the hurt today. We do not have to do it perfectly. We just have to start. Jesus will meet us in the trying.

Prayer:

Lord, everyone says forgiveness is lovely, until I actually have something to forgive. Today it is hard: the wound is deep, the person is unrepentant, my heart is tender. Make me tenderhearted anyway, kind anyway, forgiving anyway, just as You forgave me. When the pain flares at dinner or in the quiet night, wrap Your arms around my aching heart and soothe it with Your presence. I pray for everyone for whom forgiving feels impossible today. Do the impossible in us. Amen.

Study questions

1What did C.S. Lewis honestly admit about forgiveness?

  1. It is always easy
  2. Everyone says it is lovely until they actually have something to forgive
  3. It is not important
  4. Only pastors need to practice it

2How did Jesus close the gap between the idea of forgiveness and the reality?

  1. By only teaching about it
  2. By living it on the cross in the hardest possible moment
  3. By avoiding people who hurt Him
  4. By waiting until He felt like it

3In Ephesians 4:32, what comes before forgiveness in Paul's list?

  1. Wealth and success
  2. Being kind and tenderhearted
  3. Being tough and guarded
  4. Ignoring others

4What is a good first step when forgiving feels hard?

  1. Pretend you are not hurt
  2. Admit it is hard to God and take one small step, like praying for the person
  3. Wait ten years and try again
  4. Tell everyone how hard it is

5What does "as God in Christ forgave you" teach us?

  1. God's forgiveness is our model and our motivation for forgiving others
  2. We must earn God's forgiveness first
  3. Only God needs to forgive
  4. Forgiveness is optional
paragonpdx104

Week 21 · The Crucifixion: Forgiving from the Cross

Friday – Love That Changes Enemies

"Love is the only force capable of transforming an enemy into a friend." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Romans 5:8 — "but God shows his love for us in that while we were still sinners, Christ died for us."

Dr. King lived this truth. He was hated, threatened, and attacked for standing up for justice. He could have answered hate with hate. Instead, he insisted that only love can transform an enemy into a friend. He learned this from Jesus, who died for the very people who were killing Him. While we were still sinners, still God's enemies, Christ died for us. That is the most stunning love in history.

Think about what happened after the cross. One of the soldiers who stood at the foot of the cross watched Jesus die and said, "Truly this was the Son of God." An enemy became a believer. Love did what force never could. It changed a heart.

This is how servant leaders change the world. Not by defeating their enemies, but by loving them. That does not mean we approve of wrongdoing or pretend evil is good. It means we refuse to dehumanize the people who oppose us. We see them as people God loves. We pray for them. We treat them with dignity. We look for chances to do good to them.

How does this call us to be better people? It challenges us to love the difficult people in our lives: the rude neighbor, the harsh boss, the family member who always criticizes, the person who believes the opposite of everything we believe. We do not have to agree with them. We do have to love them. And when we do, something miraculous can happen. Not always, but sometimes, love transforms an enemy into a friend. Even when it does not change them, it changes us. We become more like Jesus, who loved His enemies to the very end.

Prayer:

God, only love can turn an enemy into a friend, and You proved it by dying for me while I was still Your enemy. Today, help me love the person who feels like my enemy: the hostile neighbor, the rival at work, the family member who chose the other side. Bless them genuinely. Pray for them sincerely. I pray for every bitter feud tonight, in homes, in workplaces, in nations. Send Your transforming love. Let my changed heart be the first miracle. Amen.

  2. Love
  3. Revenge
  4. Ignoring them

  1. After we became perfect
  2. While we were still sinners
  3. Only for good people
  4. After we earned it

3What happened to one of the soldiers at the cross?

  1. He remained an enemy forever
  2. He watched Jesus die and declared, "Truly this was the Son of God"
  3. He led the mocking
  4. He left before the end

4Does loving our enemies mean approving of their wrongdoing?

  1. Yes, love means agreeing with everything
  2. No, it means refusing to dehumanize them and treating them with dignity
  3. Yes, we must pretend evil is good
  4. No, we should avoid them completely

5Even when loving an enemy does not change them, what does it do?

  1. Nothing at all
  2. It changes us, making us more like Jesus
  3. It wastes our time
  4. It makes us weaker

Write a short paragraph describing one specific, practical way you could show love to that person this week (praying for them daily, doing something kind for them, speaking of them with dignity). How does this reflect the way Jesus loved His enemies from the cross?

Looking back

This week you stood at the cross and heard the hardest prayer ever prayed: ‘Father, forgive them.’ Forgive anyway. Find the power to forgive even when it is hard — because he who is devoid of the power to forgive is devoid of the power to love. Love that forgiving changes enemies. Next week, you will learn what that death purchased: the ransom paid, the ultimate service.

Week 22 · The Ransom Paid: Death as Ultimate Service

Monday – Called to Die to Self

Week opening

‘For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many.’ This week you will face the full weight of those words. Jesus did not just serve — He gave His life. You will learn what it means to die to self daily, to put love into every act, and to lead from behind like a shepherd rather than strut in front like a king. ‘Greater love has no one than this, that someone lay down his life for his friends.’

"When Christ calls a man, he bids him come and die." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:45 — "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

Those are strong words from Bonhoeffer: when Christ calls us, He calls us to die. He did not mean we all die physically like Jesus did. He meant we die to ourselves. We die to selfishness, to pride, to the constant demand to have our own way. Jesus showed us what this looks like. He came not to be served but to serve, and He gave His life as a ransom for many. A ransom is the price paid to set someone free. Jesus paid with His life to set us free.

Dying to self sounds scary, but it is actually the doorway to the best life. Think of a seed. A seed must fall into the ground and die before it can grow into a plant. If it refuses to die, it stays a lonely little seed forever. In the same way, when we hold tightly to our own way, our own comfort, and our own plans, our lives stay small. When we let those things die, God grows something beautiful in us.

What does dying to self look like in real life? It looks like giving up your right to be first. It looks like forgiving when you would rather stay angry. It looks like serving when you would rather rest. It looks like telling the truth when a lie would be easier. Each time we choose God's way over our own way, a little piece of selfishness dies, and a little more of Jesus comes alive in us.

This calls us to be better people because it frees us from the prison of self. The most miserable people are the ones who think only of themselves. The most joyful people are the ones who have died to self and live for God and others. Jesus paid the ransom. Now He invites us to live the ransomed life.

Prayer:

Lord, when You call me, You bid me come and die, die to my selfish plans, my demand for comfort, my need to be in charge. Today, help me die a little: give up my seat, my schedule, my right to be right. When self screams, let Your Spirit whisper louder. I pray for everyone clinging to self today. Show us the freedom on the other side of surrender. Teach me that dying to self is not losing life. It is finally finding it. Amen.

Study questions

  1. A call to comfort and ease
  2. A call to come and die to self
  3. A call to ignore others
  4. A call to seek power

2In Mark 10:45, why did the Son of Man come?

  1. To be served by others
  2. To serve, and to give His life as a ransom for many
  3. To avoid all suffering
  4. To build an earthly throne

3What is a ransom, as described in this lesson?

  1. A gift given to a king
  2. The price paid to set someone free
  3. A type of prayer
  4. A church offering

4What is the seed illustration meant to teach?

  1. Seeds are more important than people
  2. Like a seed must die to grow, we must die to self for God to grow new life in us
  3. Farming is the best career
  4. We should avoid planting seeds

5Which action shows "dying to self"?

  1. Insisting on having your own way
  2. Forgiving when you would rather stay angry
  3. Serving only when it is convenient
  4. Telling a lie to protect yourself

Week 22 · The Ransom Paid: Death as Ultimate Service

Tuesday – How Much Love We Put In

"Love begins at home, and it is not how much we do... but how much love we put in that action." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): John 15:13 — "Greater love has no one than this, that someone lay down his life for his friends."

Jesus said the greatest love is laying down your life for your friends. He did exactly that on the cross. But Mother Teresa reminds us that laying down our lives does not always mean dying. Most days, it means putting love into the small, ordinary actions right in front of us, starting at home. It is not how much we do. It is how much love we put into what we do.

Two people can do the exact same task, and one act can be full of love while the other is empty. One person washes dishes with grumbling and resentment. Another washes the same dishes thinking, "I love my family, and I want them to come home to a clean kitchen." Same dishes, completely different acts. The difference is the love put into it.

This changes how we see our daily work. The parent making lunches, the worker doing a repetitive job, the volunteer setting up chairs, the friend listening to the same story for the third time — all of these can be acts of great love if love is put into them. Jesus took the lowest job, washing feet, and made it holy by the love He put into it.

How does this call us to be better people? It teaches us that no task is too small to matter. We stop rushing through our duties and start filling them with love. We stop counting how much we do and start asking how much love we are putting in. A home, a workplace, or a church filled with people who put love into everything they do becomes a little picture of heaven. And it all starts with the example Jesus set when He laid down His life for His friends.

Prayer:

God, it is not how much I do, but how much love I put into it. Today, at home where no one applauds, help me love the ordinary: the laundry folded with care, the dinner cooked with joy, the floors swept with a glad heart, the listening ear for my tired spouse. When housework feels invisible, remind me heaven sees. I pray for every weary homemaker and parent tonight. Bless their hidden love. Let my home be the first place my love shows up. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Mother Teresa, what matters most in our actions?

  1. How much we do
  2. How much love we put into what we do
  3. How fast we finish
  4. How many people notice

2In John 15:13, what is the greatest love?

  1. Loving only your family
  2. Laying down your life for your friends
  3. Becoming wealthy to help others
  4. Traveling the world

3What makes the same task (like washing dishes) an act of love?

  1. Doing it as fast as possible
  2. The love put into it while doing it
  3. Having expensive dishes
  4. Making sure others watch you do it

4Where does Mother Teresa say love begins?

  1. At church
  2. At home
  3. At work
  4. On vacation

5How can ordinary daily work become holy?

  1. By only doing religious tasks
  2. By filling everyday duties with love, following Jesus' example
  3. By quitting your job
  4. By doing less work

Week 22 · The Ransom Paid: Death as Ultimate Service

Wednesday – A Life That Counts

"What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): 1 John 3:16 — "By this we know love, that he laid down his life for us, and we ought to lay down our lives for the brothers."

Nelson Mandela spent the best years of his life in prison, yet his life counted for more than most. Why? Because he measured his life not by how long he lived or how much he owned, but by the difference he made in the lives of others. Jesus is the perfect example. His earthly life lasted only about 33 years, but no life has ever made a bigger difference. He laid down His life for us, and that one act changed the world forever.

John writes that because Jesus laid down His life for us, we ought to lay down our lives for others. Most of us will not die as martyrs. But we can lay down our lives in daily pieces. We lay down our time when we help someone who needs us. We lay down our comfort when we serve in hard places. We lay down our pride when we admit we are wrong. We lay down our plans when God interrupts them with someone else's need. Piece by piece, day by day, we give our lives away, and that is what makes a life count.

Here is the question that calls us to be better people: when your life is over, what will have counted? Not your bank account. Not your follower count. Not how comfortable you were. What will count is the difference you made in the lives of others. The meals served, the lonely visited, the young mentored, the hurting comforted, the truth spoken in love. A life poured out for others is never wasted. It echoes into eternity. Jesus proved it. Mandela proved it. Now it is our turn.

Prayer:

Lord, what will count at the end of my life is not what I collected, but what difference I made. Today, help me lay down my time for others, the afternoon given to a lonely senior, the evening spent mentoring a teen, the lunch hour shared with a struggling friend. When I am tempted to measure my day by productivity, measure it by love instead. I pray for everyone wondering if their life matters. Show them it does. Make my life count where it counts most. Amen.

  4. How famous a person becomes

  3. By feeling happy
  4. By being loved by many people

  3. Avoid all sacrifice
  4. Only love ourselves

Week 22 · The Ransom Paid: Death as Ultimate Service

Thursday – Report for Duty

"Don't bother to give God instructions; just report for duty." — Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): Philippians 2:8 — "And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross."

Corrie ten Boom learned this lesson the hard way. She had plans for her life, and God had different plans that included a concentration camp. She discovered that the best prayer is not telling God what to do, but simply showing up and saying, "I am here. Use me." Just report for duty.

Jesus is the perfect picture of this. The verse says He humbled Himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross. Jesus did not give the Father instructions. He did not negotiate the terms. He did not ask for an easier assignment. He simply reported for duty and obeyed, all the way to the cross. His obedience was not partial or conditional. It was complete.

Most of us do the opposite. We come to God with a list of instructions: "God, here is what I need You to do, and here is how I need You to do it." We treat God like our assistant instead of our Commander. Then we wonder why our prayers feel unanswered. Corrie's wisdom turns it around. Our job is not to instruct God. Our job is to show up, ready to obey.

What does reporting for duty look like in real life? It looks like starting the day with, "Lord, what do You have for me today?" instead of, "Lord, here is my plan, please bless it." It looks like serving where you are needed, not just where you prefer. It looks like saying yes to the hard assignment, the awkward conversation, or the unseen task, because God asked. This makes us better people because it kills our demand to control everything. We become servants instead of managers, soldiers instead of generals, and we discover the deep peace of simply obeying a Father we trust.

Prayer:

God, I will not give You instructions today. I simply report for duty. Show me my assignment: the task at work, the chore at home, the errand run with kindness, the person who needs me. Like Jesus, obedient even to death, help me obey in the small things with a glad heart. When duty feels dull, remind me who I serve. I pray for everyone dragging through their duties today. Renew their strength. Let my daily obedience be my worship, offered with joy. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Corrie ten Boom, what should we do instead of giving God instructions?

  1. Stop praying altogether
  2. Just report for duty
  3. Tell God exactly what to do
  4. Wait for life to fix itself

2In Philippians 2:8, how far did Jesus' obedience go?

  1. Only to easy things
  2. To the point of death, even death on a cross
  3. Only to preaching
  4. Only when people agreed with Him

  1. Yes, He asked for an easier assignment
  2. No, He humbled Himself and obeyed completely
  3. Yes, He refused the cross
  4. No, He ran away

  2. Giving God a list of demands
  3. Only serving when it is convenient
  4. Avoiding hard assignments

5How does surrendering control make us better people?

  2. It kills our need to control everything and brings the peace of trusting God
  3. It means we never make decisions
  4. It removes all responsibility

Week 22 · The Ransom Paid: Death as Ultimate Service

Friday – Leading From Behind

"It is better to lead from behind and to put others in front, especially when you celebrate victory when nice things occur." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): Philippians 2:5-8 — "Have this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus, who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men. And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross."

We have reached the end of Week 22, and Mandela gives us the perfect summary. The best leaders do not push themselves to the front. They lead from behind. They put others in front. When something good happens, they let others take the bow. This is exactly what Jesus did. He was in the form of God, the highest position there is. But He did not cling to it. He emptied Himself, took the form of a servant, and humbled Himself to the point of death on a cross. The King of the universe led from behind all the way to Calvary.

Leading from behind is the opposite of how the world works. The world says to promote yourself, take the credit, and make sure everyone knows what you did. Jesus says to empty yourself, lift others up, and let God handle the recognition. This does not mean leaders are passive or weak. Mandela was not weak. Jesus was not weak. Leading from behind takes more strength than leading from the front, because it requires killing pride every single day.

Think about what this looks like for you. As a parent, it means celebrating your children's wins more than your own. As a team member, it means making your coworkers look good. As a church volunteer, it means being happy when someone else gets thanked. As a friend, it means listening more than talking. Each time you put someone else in front, you are thinking with the mind of Christ.

This is the ultimate demonstration of servant leadership. The cross is the final proof that greatness is found in giving, not getting. Carry this mind with you: empty yourself, serve others, and trust God with the rest. That is how Jesus led. That is how we lead too.

Prayer:

Lord, teach me to lead from behind, to put others in front, especially when good things happen. Today, when the team succeeds, let me celebrate them, not me. When someone else gets the praise, help me cheer the loudest. Empty me of the need to be first, the way Jesus emptied Himself. I pray for every leader tempted to take the credit tonight. Humble us. Show me the quiet joy of watching others shine, knowing I helped lift them there. Amen.

  3. Taking all the credit
  4. Avoiding all responsibility

  1. He clung tightly to His position
  2. He emptied Himself and took the form of a servant

  4. He avoided becoming human

3Why does leading from behind take more strength?

  1. Because it is physically harder
  2. Because it requires killing pride every single day
  3. Because no one respects it
  4. Because it means doing nothing

4Which action shows "leading from behind"?

  1. Making sure everyone knows what you did
  2. Making your coworkers look good and celebrating their wins
  3. Taking the best seat for yourself
  4. Correcting others publicly

5What is the ultimate proof that greatness is found in giving, not getting?

  1. A successful career
  2. The cross, where Jesus gave His life for others
  3. Winning an award
  4. Having many followers

This week you stood before the ransom. Called to die to self — not once, but daily. It is not how much we do, but how much love we put in. A life that counts is a life poured out. Report for duty, and lead from behind. The cross is the ultimate service. Next week, the stone rolls away — vindicated humility rises.

Week 23 · The Resurrection: Vindicated Humility

Monday – God Lifts Up the Lowly

Week opening

They humbled Him to the grave. God lifted Him to glory. ‘He humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death... therefore God has highly exalted him.’ This week you will celebrate the resurrection — proof that humility wins in the end. You will learn that God lifts up the lowly, that love is stronger than hate, and that small kindnesses add up to a big victory. He is risen — so rise and serve.

"Not all of us can do great things. But we can do small things with great love." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Philippians 2:8–9 — "And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross. Therefore God has highly exalted him and bestowed on him the name that is above every name."

Humility means not making yourself the most important person in the room. It means putting others first and being okay with nobody noticing. Jesus is the greatest picture of this. He was God's own Son, but He came to earth as a servant. He washed His friends' feet. He let people hurt Him. He died on a cross, even though He had done nothing wrong. That is what the verse above is talking about. But the story does not end there. God raised Jesus from the dead and gave Him the highest place of all. That is what "vindicated humility" means: God showed that Jesus was right all along by lifting Him up.

This is good news for us. You do not have to push your way to the top. You do not have to brag, argue, or prove you are important. When you quietly do the right thing — helping a neighbor, forgiving someone who hurt you, doing a hard job without complaining — God sees it. He may not lift you up the way the world lifts people up, but He honors a humble heart.

Living this out looks simple. It looks like letting someone else take the credit. It looks like saying, "I'm sorry," first. It looks like choosing the small job nobody wants, like Mother Teresa said, and doing it with great love. It can feel hard when others get noticed and you don't. But the resurrection tells us the truth: humble love wins in the end. God never forgets the servant. Keep serving with a soft heart, and trust Him to do the lifting.

Prayer:

Father, You humbled Yourself and then God lifted You up, and You promise to lift the humble too. Today, I choose the low place: the last seat, the unseen task, the quiet apology. When the world tells me to promote myself, help me trust Your promotion instead. I pray for every lowly, overlooked person tonight, the dishwasher, the nursing aide, the single mom. Lift their heads. Teach me that the way up in Your kingdom is down, and help me walk it gladly. Amen.

  3. Fred Rogers
  4. Desmond Tutu

  2. He humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross
  3. He avoided all suffering
  4. He ruled as an earthly king

3What happened because Jesus humbled himself (Philippians 2:9)?

  1. God exalted him to the highest place
  2. Everyone forgot about him
  3. He stayed in the tomb
  4. He lost all his followers

4What does "vindicated humility" mean in this lesson?

  1. Bragging about being humble
  2. God proving humble love right by lifting it up
  3. Never helping anyone
  4. Fighting for honor

5Which action shows humble service?

  1. Doing a small job with great love without being asked
  2. Taking credit for someone else's work
  3. Refusing to apologize
  4. Showing off your good deeds

Week 23 · The Resurrection: Vindicated Humility

Tuesday – Risen to Serve

"Do not waste time bothering whether you 'love' your neighbor; act as if you did." — C.S. Lewis
Bible (ESV): Matthew 28:6 — "He is not here, for he has risen, as he said. Come, see the place where he lay."

On the first Easter morning, women went to Jesus' tomb and found it empty. An angel said, "He is not here, for he has risen." Jesus had beaten death. But notice what Jesus did after He rose. He did not go find the leaders who hurt Him and get even. He did not show off His power. Instead, He kept serving. He met Mary Magdalene in the garden and called her by name. He walked with two sad disciples on the road and explained the Scriptures to them. He even cooked breakfast on the beach for His friends and ate with them. The risen King was still a servant.

That tells us something important. The resurrection was not just about Jesus getting His life back. It was about giving us a new kind of life — a life where love shows up in what we do, not just in what we feel. C.S. Lewis said not to waste time wondering if you feel love for your neighbor. Just act as if you do. Feelings can change from day to day, but actions build real love.

What does this look like in real life? It looks like visiting the neighbor who is lonely, even when it is not convenient. It looks like forgiving a family member before they say sorry. It looks like doing the dishes, mowing a lawn, or giving a ride without being asked. These are small acts, but they are resurrection acts — they bring life where there was sadness.

When we serve like this, people start to see Jesus in us. We become living proof that He is alive, because His love is alive in our hands and feet. The empty tomb was the start. Now our serving is the proof.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, You rose, not to be admired from a distance, but to send us out to serve. Your resurrection gives my ordinary Tuesday a mission. Today, help me act like someone alive: bring hope to the discouraged coworker, energy to the tired volunteer shift, joy to my own kitchen table. When I feel dead inside, resurrect my purpose. I pray for everyone who feels buried by despair. Roll away their stone. Let my risen life serve a risen Lord. Amen.

Week 23 · The Resurrection: Vindicated Humility

Wednesday – Love Is Stronger Than Hate

"Darkness cannot drive out darkness: only light can do that. Hate cannot drive out hate: only love can do that." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): John 11:25 — Jesus said to her, "I am the resurrection and the life. Whoever believes in me, though he die, yet shall he live."

The day Jesus died was the darkest day His friends had ever known. They had hoped He would save them, and instead they watched Him suffer. They went home sad and afraid. But three days later, the sun came up on a brand-new morning, and Jesus was alive. The darkest night in history ended in the brightest morning ever. That is what God does. He brings light out of darkness.

Martin Luther King Jr. said that darkness cannot drive out darkness — only light can. Hate cannot drive out hate — only love can. When someone treats us badly, our first thought is often to treat them badly back. We want to argue, get even, or shut them out. But Jesus showed us a better way. From the cross He prayed, "Father, forgive them." After He rose, He did not come back angry. He came back with peace.

Humility is a big part of this. A humble person does not have to win every fight or have the last word. A humble person can let love do the fighting. When a coworker is rude, you can answer with kindness. When a family member is unfair, you can stay calm and keep loving them. That does not mean you let people walk all over you. It means you trust God to be the judge, and you choose to be the light.

Try this in your own life. The next time someone is unkind, pause and ask, "What would love do here?" Maybe it means listening instead of yelling. Maybe it means helping someone who does not deserve it. These small choices push back the darkness. And remember: the resurrection proves that light always wins in the end. Love is stronger than hate, and life is stronger than death.

Prayer:

God, hate looks strong. It shouts, it breaks, it bullies, it wins the argument. But Your love is stronger: it rose from a grave. Today, when hate knocks, in the news, in my neighborhood, in my own heart, answer it with love. Help me bless the person who cursed me and pray for the one who hurt me. I pray for every heart poisoned by hate tonight. Overcome it with Your stronger love. Prove again that light always outlasts darkness. Amen.
