Week 23 · The Resurrection: Vindicated Humility

Thursday – Small Kindnesses, Big Victory

"Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): James 4:10 — "Humble yourselves before the Lord, and he will exalt you."

Desmond Tutu once said that when we each do our little bit of good, all those little bits put together can overwhelm the world. Think about that. You do not have to change the whole world by yourself. You just have to do your part, right where you are.

The Bible says, "Humble yourselves before the Lord, and he will lift you up." This is a promise. God sees the person who serves quietly. He sees the mom who gets up early to pack lunches. He sees the worker who does his job well when nobody is watching. He sees the teenager who is kind to the kid everyone else ignores. None of these acts make the news, but God notices every one of them.

Jesus lived this way. He spent most of His life as a carpenter in a small town, unknown to the world. When He finally became famous, He still chose the low road — washing feet, touching lepers, eating with people others looked down on. Then God lifted Him up higher than anyone, raising Him from the dead. Vindicated humility means God honors the lowly. The way down is the way up.

So what is your little bit of good today? Maybe it is a phone call to someone who is lonely. Maybe it is sharing your lunch. Maybe it is cleaning up a mess you did not make. Do it without telling anyone. Do it without posting about it. Do it for God and for the person in front of you.

You might feel small some days, like your kindness does not matter. But the resurrection says it does. Every small act of love is a seed, and God grows seeds into something big. Keep planting. He will do the lifting, in His time and in His way.

Prayer:

Lord, You promise to lift up those who humble themselves. Today, my victory will not come from being the loudest or the toughest. It will come from small kindnesses: the encouraging text, the held door, the sincere apology. When I want to fight for my rights, help me surrender them to You instead. I pray for everyone humbling themselves in hard places today. Exalt them in Your time. Let my small kindnesses be the weapons that win Your way. Amen.

Week 23 · The Resurrection: Vindicated Humility

Friday – Rise Again

"The greatest glory in living lies not in never falling, but in rising every time we fall." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): 1 Peter 5:6 — "Humble yourselves, therefore, under the mighty hand of God so that at the proper time he may exalt you."

Peter loved Jesus, but on the night Jesus was arrested, Peter was scared. Three times people asked him if he knew Jesus, and three times he said no. Then the rooster crowed, and Peter went outside and cried. He had failed his best friend. He probably thought his story was over.

But it was not over. After Jesus rose from the dead, He found Peter. On the beach, Jesus asked Peter three times, "Do you love me?" Three times Peter said yes. And each time, Jesus gave him a job: "Feed my lambs." "Tend my sheep." "Feed my sheep." Jesus did not throw Peter away. He lifted him back up and trusted him again. Peter went on to become a great leader in the early church.

Nelson Mandela said the greatest glory in life is not never falling, but rising every time we fall. We all fall. We say the wrong thing. We hurt people we love. We make choices we regret. Humility is what helps us rise again. A proud person hides the fall and pretends it never happened. A humble person says, "I was wrong. God, help me get up." And God does.

The resurrection is God's great promise that falling is never the end. Jesus went all the way down into death, and God raised Him up. Whatever pit you are in today, it is not too deep for Him. Humble yourself under His mighty hand. Tell Him the truth about where you are. Then take His hand and stand up. He will lift you in His perfect time, and He will give you a new beginning — just like He did for Peter.

Prayer:

God, I have fallen, the diet broken, the temper lost, the promise I failed to keep. But Your greatest glory is not in never falling; it is in rising every time. Today, help me rise again. Wash me clean, set my feet straight, and let me start fresh without shame. I pray for everyone who fell today and wants to stay down. Lift them. I humble myself under Your mighty hand, and in Your time, lift me up to try again. Amen.

Looking back

This week the tomb stood empty. God lifts up the lowly — the One who humbled Himself is now exalted. Risen to serve: the resurrection is not just a doctrine, it is a marching order. Love is stronger than hate, and small kindnesses win big victories. Rise again, every morning. Next week, you will walk a beach with Jesus as He restores a fallen leader.

Week 24 · Restoring the Fallen Leader (Peter on the Beach)

Monday – Jesus Comes to the Shore

Week opening

Peter denied Jesus three times. Then, at dawn on a beach, Jesus cooked him breakfast and asked three times, ‘Do you love me?’ This week you will learn that failure is not final. You will watch Jesus restore a fallen leader gently — no shame, no lecture, just love and a new assignment: ‘Feed my sheep.’ If you have ever failed, this week is for you. And it will teach you to restore others the same way.

"Forgiving is not forgetting; it's actually remembering — remembering and not using your right to hit back. It's a second chance for a new beginning." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): John 21:4 — "Just as day was breaking, Jesus stood on the shore; yet the disciples did not know that it was Jesus." John 21:7 — "That disciple whom Jesus loved therefore said to Peter, ‘It is the Lord!’ When Simon Peter heard that it was the Lord, he put on his outer garment, for he was stripped for work, and threw himself into the sea."

Think about what Peter had just done. A few days earlier, he had told three different people, "I don't even know Jesus." He was broken. He went back to fishing, back to the old life, probably sure Jesus would never want him again. But Jesus did not wait for Peter to come crawling back. Jesus walked out to the shore in the early morning and made breakfast. He came to Peter first.

That is what real forgiveness looks like. As Desmond Tutu said, forgiveness is not pretending the hurt never happened. It is remembering, and still choosing not to strike back. It is giving someone a second chance at a brand-new beginning.

When a leader falls, the world expects them to be finished. One big mistake and people say, "They're done." But Jesus does the opposite. He shows up at the shoreline of their life with food and warmth. Notice He did not start with a lecture. He started with breakfast. Care came before correction.

This teaches us something huge about how we treat people who have failed. We do not wait for them to fix themselves first. We go to them. We bring kindness first. If someone around you has messed up — a friend, a coworker, a family member — do not wait for them to earn their way back. Go to their shore. Show up. Offer a warm meal and a warm heart before you offer advice. That is how Jesus restores, and that is how He wants us to restore. A servant leader does not keep score of failures. A servant leader keeps showing up.

Prayer:

Lord Jesus, like You stood on the shore cooking breakfast for disciples who had failed You, come to my shore today. I messed up, the harsh words, the missed chance, the promise broken, and I feel like hiding. But You come close instead of condemning. Meet me in my ordinary morning: the kitchen, the commute, the quiet. I pray for everyone hiding in shame tonight. Walk onto their shore. Remind us that failure is never the end when You show up. Amen.

  3. Remembering, but not using your right to hit back — a second chance for a new beginning
  4. Waiting until someone earns your trust back

Week 24 · Restoring the Fallen Leader (Peter on the Beach)

Tuesday – Three Chances to Say Yes

"There is no pit so deep, that God's love is not deeper still." — Corrie ten Boom
Bible (ESV): John 21:15-17 — "When they had finished breakfast, Jesus said to Simon Peter, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me more than these?’ He said to him, ‘Yes, Lord; you know that I love you.’ He said to him, ‘Feed my lambs.’ He said to him a second time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ He said to him, ‘Yes, Lord; you know that I love you.’ He said to him, ‘Tend my sheep.’ He said to him the third time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ Peter was grieved because he said to him the third time, ‘Do you love me?’ and he said to him, ‘Lord, you know everything; you know that I love you.’ Jesus said to him, ‘Feed my sheep.’"

Peter had said "no" to Jesus three times. He denied Him once, then again, then a third time. That number must have echoed in Peter's mind like a drumbeat. Three times. So what did Jesus do? He asked Peter a question — three times. "Do you love me? Do you love me? Do you love me?" For every "no," Jesus gave Peter a chance to say "yes." Three failures were covered by three confessions of love.

This is no accident. Jesus was gently undoing Peter's shame. He was not rubbing it in. He was healing it, one question at a time, until the pain of the denials was replaced by the joy of declaring love. Peter was hurt when Jesus asked the third time — it stung — but that sting was the sting of a wound being cleaned so it could heal.

Corrie ten Boom understood this. She survived terrible suffering in a Nazi prison camp, and she said, "There is no pit so deep, that God's love is not deeper still." No matter how far we fall, God's love goes further down and lifts us back up.

Here is what this means for us. When someone has failed, they do not need us to keep reminding them of the failure. They need us to ask them, with love, "Are you still in this? Do you still love what is good? Do you still want to serve?" We give them the chance to answer yes again — not once, but as many times as it takes for their heart to believe it. And when someone around us fails, we can tell them the truth of this lesson: your lowest moment is not deeper than God's love. Say yes to Him again. He will meet your answer with open arms, every time.

Prayer:

Father, Peter denied You three times, and You gave him three chances to say "I love You." Today, thank You for my second chances: the fresh morning after yesterday's failure, the restored friendship, the forgiven debt, the clean slate. When shame tells me I am disqualified, let Your voice be louder: "Do you love me? Then begin again." I pray for everyone who thinks they have blown it for good. Give them another chance. Let my restored life restore others. Amen.

  4. Only strong people feel God's love

Week 24 · Restoring the Fallen Leader (Peter on the Beach)

Wednesday – Feed My Sheep

"Life's most persistent and urgent question is, 'What are you doing for others?'" — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): John 21:16-17 — "He said to him a second time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ He said to him, ‘Yes, Lord; you know that I love you.’ He said to him, ‘Tend my sheep.’ He said to him the third time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ Peter was grieved because he said to him the third time, ‘Do you love me?’ and he said to him, ‘Lord, you know everything; you know that I love you.’ Jesus said to him, ‘Feed my sheep.’"

After every one of Peter's answers, Jesus gave him a job. "Feed my lambs." "Tend my sheep." "Feed my sheep." Stop and think about how amazing that is. Peter had just failed in the biggest way. The natural thing would be for Jesus to say, "You are forgiven — now sit down and be quiet for a while." But Jesus did the opposite. He put Peter back to work. He trusted him with the most precious thing Jesus had: His people.

That is the deepest sign of forgiveness. Anyone can say the words "I forgive you." But when you hand someone a real job again, when you trust them with something that matters, that is forgiveness with muscle. Jesus was saying, "I don't just wipe your slate clean. I still believe in you. I still need you."

Martin Luther King Jr. once said that life's most persistent and urgent question is, "What are you doing for others?" Jesus answered that question for Peter on the beach. What Peter would do for others was this: feed them, care for them, lead them. Restoration is not about going back to the couch. Restoration is about getting back to serving.

This matters for how we treat people who have failed and come back. When someone has truly turned around, we do them no favor by keeping them on the sidelines forever. At some point, love says, "I trust you again. Here is something important for you to do." Serving others is part of healing. The fastest way to feel whole again is to pour yourself out for someone else. So if you have failed, hear Jesus' voice: your story is not over. There are sheep who need feeding, and He is asking you to feed them.

Prayer:

Lord, You did not just forgive Peter, You gave him a job: feed My sheep. Today, give me my assignment too. Show me who You have entrusted to me: my children, my team at work, the new believer, the lonely neighbor. Help me feed them, with truth, with encouragement, with practical help, with love. When I feel unqualified, remind me that love qualifies me. I pray for every shepherd of a small flock today. Strengthen our hands for the feeding. Amen.

Week 24 · Restoring the Fallen Leader (Peter on the Beach)

Thursday – Follow Me, Even When It's Hard

"I learned that courage was not the absence of fear, but the triumph over it. The brave man is not he who does not feel afraid, but he who conquers that fear." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): John 21:18-19 — "Truly, truly, I say to you, when you were young, you used to dress yourself and walk wherever you wanted, but when you are old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will dress you and carry you where you do not want to go. (This he said to show by what kind of death he was to glorify God.) And after saying this he said to him, ‘Follow me.’"

Right after restoring Peter, Jesus told him something hard. He said that one day Peter would be taken where he did not want to go — that he would die for his faith. Jesus did not sugarcoat it. Restoration did not come with a promise of an easy life. It came with two words: "Follow me!"

Think about what that took. Peter had just fallen apart under pressure. He knew how fear felt. And now Jesus was telling him, "The road ahead will be harder, not easier." Yet Peter said yes. He followed. And history tells us he did — Peter became a bold, fearless leader of the early church, and he never denied Jesus again.

Nelson Mandela knew about this kind of courage. He spent 27 years in prison for standing up for what was right, and he said that courage is not the absence of fear. Courage is feeling afraid and going forward anyway. Peter was afraid on the night he denied Jesus. But after the beach, he learned that fear does not get the final say.

This is a lesson for all of us. Following Jesus does not mean life gets easy. It means we get a Leader worth following through hard things. When we fail, the temptation is to quit — to decide that serving is too risky, that we will just play it safe. But restoration says the opposite: get back up, feel the fear, and follow anyway. A servant leader is not someone who never feels afraid. A servant leader is someone who follows Jesus even with shaking hands. Peter proves it can be done. If the man who denied Jesus three times could become the rock of the church, then your failure does not disqualify you from a brave future either.

Prayer:

God, following You will not always be easy. Jesus told Peter it would cost him everything. Today, when following means the hard road, telling the truth when lying is easier, staying when leaving is tempting, giving when keeping feels safer, help me follow anyway. When I want the comfortable path, remind me who walks beside me. I pray for everyone paying a price for following You today. Walk with them. Let my "yes" hold firm, even when it is hard. Amen.

  4. That he would become rich

  3. "Follow me!"

  4. Pretending problems do not exist

Week 24 · Restoring the Fallen Leader (Peter on the Beach)

Friday – Restoring Others Gently

"The first service one owes to others in a fellowship consists of listening to them." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): Galatians 6:1 — "Brothers, if anyone is caught in any transgression, you who are spiritual should restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Keep watch on yourself, lest you too be tempted."

Jesus restored Peter on the beach. But the story does not end there. Peter went on to restore others. In the book of Acts, he preached, he prayed for the sick, he lifted up the broken, and he led thousands of people to Jesus. The man who once fell became a man who helped others get back up. That is the full circle of grace: we are restored so that we can restore.

The apostle Paul tells us exactly how to do it. In Galatians 6:1, he says that when someone is caught in sin, we should restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Gently. Not loudly. Not with gossip. Not with a lecture. Gently — the way Jesus handled Peter, with breakfast first and kindness all the way through.

Dietrich Bonhoeffer, a pastor who stood up for what was right even when it cost him his life, said that the first service we owe to others is listening to them. Before we speak, before we advise, before we correct — we listen. Listening is the first act of restoration. When someone has fallen, the most healing thing we can do is sit down, be quiet, and truly hear them.

So how do we restore a fallen friend? First, we listen. We let them tell their story without interrupting. Second, we remember our own failures — we stay humble, because Paul warns us that we could fall too. Third, we speak hope, not shame. We tell them what Jesus told Peter: your failure is not the end, and there is still work for you to do. Fourth, we walk with them. We do not just say "good luck" and walk away. We stay.

This is servant leadership at its finest. A servant leader does not throw broken people away. A servant leader picks them up gently, the way Jesus picked up Peter, and helps them find their feet again. This week we learned that Jesus restores the fallen. Now the question is: who around you needs restoring — and will you be the one to listen first?

Prayer:

Lord, someone I know has fallen, caught in a mistake, crushed by shame. Teach me to restore them gently, the way You restored Peter: no lectures, no gossip, just breakfast on the beach and a question about love. Help me listen first, speak softly, and guard their dignity. Keep me from pride, because I could be them. I pray for every fallen one tonight. Send them a gentle restorer. Make me the safe person people can run to and confess to. Amen.

  2. Gently
  3. With gossip and rumors
  4. By ignoring them

  1. Giving them money
  2. Correcting their mistakes
  3. Listening to them
  4. Telling them what to do

  1. He quit serving
  2. He spent his life restoring others — preaching, praying, and leading people to Jesus
  3. He went back to denying Jesus
  4. He moved far away from everyone

  3. Because we should not waste our time

FRIDAY REFLECTION

This week you walked the beach with Peter. Jesus comes to the shore of our failures. Three denials met with three chances to say yes. ‘Feed my sheep’ — failure becomes assignment. Follow Him even when it is hard, and restore others gently, remembering how gently you were restored. Next week, the mission goes wide: you will learn to multiply servants.

Week 25 · The Great Commission: Leading by Multiplying Servants

Monday – The Commission Is to Make Makers

Week opening

‘Go therefore and make disciples of all nations.’ Jesus' final order was not ‘gather followers’ — it was ‘make makers.’ This week you will learn the highest form of servant leadership: multiplying yourself. The commission is to make makers. Everybody can serve, and everybody can teach somebody else to serve. The shepherd leads from behind, and little bits of good, put together, overwhelm the world.

"Not all of us can do great things. But we can do small things with great love." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): Matthew 28:19-20 — "Go therefore and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, teaching them to observe all that I have commanded you. And behold, I am with you always, to the end of the age."

Before Jesus went back to heaven, He gave His followers one big job. It is called the Great Commission. He did not say, "Go and gather a crowd." He said, "Go therefore and make disciples." A disciple is not just someone who watches. A disciple is someone who learns and then does. Jesus wants us to teach others to do the things He taught us: to love people, to serve people, to forgive people, and to welcome people. And here is the most important part. Jesus did not ask us to make followers of ourselves. He asked us to make followers of Him. A good leader does not build a fan club. A good leader builds more servants. Think of it this way. If you teach ten people to serve with love, and each of them teaches ten more, soon hundreds of people are serving. That is called multiplying servants, and it is exactly how Jesus spread His love. You do not have to do great things to be part of this. You can do a small thing with great love. You can show a new volunteer how to brew the coffee. You can stand beside someone at the door and teach them how to greet people with a warm smile. You can explain how to set out the communion cups with care. When you teach someone else to serve, you are living out the Great Commission right where you are. The love of Jesus grows every time one servant helps raise up another servant.

Prayer:

God, Your commission is not just to make followers, but to make makers, people who make more servants. Today, show me who I can invest in: the younger coworker, the new volunteer, my own child. Teach me to share what I know, model what I believe, and then step back and let them lead. When I am tempted to do it all myself, remind me that multiplying is Your math. I pray for every mentor pouring into someone today. Bless their investment. Make me a maker of servants. Amen.

Week 25 · The Great Commission: Leading by Multiplying Servants

Tuesday – Everybody Can Serve

"Everybody can be great, because everybody can serve. You don't have to have a college degree to serve. You don't have to make your subject and your verb agree to serve. You only need a heart full of grace, a soul generated by love." — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Mark 10:45 — "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

Martin Luther King Jr. taught a beautiful truth. You do not need a fancy degree to be great. You do not need perfect grammar or a big title. All you need is a heart full of grace and a soul filled with love. Anybody can serve, so anybody can be great. That is how Jesus led too. He was the King of kings, yet He came to serve instead of being served. He washed His friends' feet. He fed the hungry. He spent time with people nobody else would touch. And He asks us to do the same. When we lead by serving, something powerful happens. We show other people that they can serve too. Some people think, "I am not good enough," or "I do not know enough," or "That job is for someone else." A servant leader gently breaks that lie. They say, "Come with me. I will show you. You can do this." That is how we multiply servants. We do not hold the serving all to ourselves. We hand it out. We invite people in. We let them try, we cheer them on, and we watch them grow. Maybe you help with coffee or communion or greeting at the door. That is a real ministry. And when you teach a new person to do it beside you, you are making room for greatness in them. Every person God made is able to serve in some way. Our job as leaders is to help them see it and believe it. Greatness is not above anyone. It is within anyone who serves with love.

Prayer:

Lord, I do not need a degree, a title, or a perfect past to serve. Anybody can serve, which means I can serve. Today, free me from excuses. My hands can help, my ears can listen, my smile can welcome, my car can drive someone who needs a ride. When I think I am not qualified, remind me that willingness is the only requirement. I pray for everyone who feels too ordinary to matter. Show them they are exactly who You use. Let me serve, starting now. Amen.

  4. He asked for a palace

  4. Gives them money instead

Week 25 · The Great Commission: Leading by Multiplying Servants

Wednesday – The Shepherd Leads from Behind

"A leader... is like a shepherd. He stays behind the flock, letting the most nimble go out ahead, whereupon the others follow, not realizing that all along they are being directed from behind." — Nelson Mandela
Bible (ESV): 2 Timothy 2:2 — "and what you have heard from me in the presence of many witnesses entrust to faithful men, who will be able to teach others also."

Nelson Mandela gave us a wonderful picture of a leader. He said a good leader is like a shepherd. The shepherd does not always walk in front shouting orders. Sometimes the shepherd stays behind the flock, gently guiding, while the sheep walk ahead and grow confident. That is what multiplying servants looks like. You do not always have to be the one up front. A wise leader raises others up and then steps back so they can shine. The Apostle Paul did this with Timothy. Paul taught Timothy everything he could. Then he told Timothy to find reliable people and teach them too, so those people could teach others. That is four generations of servants: Paul taught Timothy, Timothy taught reliable people, and those people taught others. That is how the love of Jesus kept spreading long after Paul was gone. In real life, this means you teach someone to do your job well and then you let them do it. You stand behind them, ready to help if they need you, but you let them take the lead. Maybe you have greeted people at the door for years. Now you train a new greeter, stand beside them once or twice, and then step back with a smile. It can feel hard to let go. We like being needed. But a shepherd knows the flock is strongest when the sheep learn to walk. When you lead from behind, you are not losing your place. You are making more room in the Kingdom for others to find theirs.

Prayer:

God, teach me to lead like a shepherd, staying behind the flock, letting others go ahead, guiding gently. Today, when I lead the meeting, the team, or my family, help me push others forward instead of pulling rank. Celebrate their wins louder than my own. Entrust what I have learned to faithful people who will teach others too. I pray for every leader tempted to control everything. Loosen our grip. Show me the quiet strength of leading from behind. Amen.

1What picture did Nelson Mandela use for a leader?

  2. A shepherd who stays behind the flock
  3. A soldier giving orders
  4. A teacher with a ruler

  1. Keep everything Paul taught him to himself
  2. Entrust what he heard to reliable people who will teach others
  3. Stop teaching because he was too young
  4. Only teach his family

  1. One
  2. Two
  3. Four: Paul, Timothy, reliable people, and others
  4. None

  1. To hide and do nothing
  2. To raise others up, guide them gently, and let them take the lead
  3. To follow the crowd
  4. To quit your job

  1. Because we like being needed
  2. Because nobody else is willing
  3. Because the Bible forbids it
  4. Because it is always a mistake

Week 25 · The Great Commission: Leading by Multiplying Servants

Thursday – Loved and Capable of Loving

"The greatest thing we can do is to help somebody know that they're loved and capable of loving." — Fred Rogers
Bible (ESV): John 13:34-35 — "A new commandment I give to you, that you love one another: just as I have loved you, you also are to love one another. By this all people will know that you are my disciples, if you have love for one another."

Fred Rogers, the kind man from the television neighborhood, understood something deep about Jesus. He said the greatest thing we can do is help somebody know two things: that they are loved, and that they are capable of loving. That is exactly what making disciples is about. First, we help people know they are loved by God. When someone truly knows they are loved, it changes everything inside them. Fear goes down. Hope comes up. They start to believe their life has meaning. Second, we help them know they are capable of loving too. That is the multiplying part. They do not just receive love. They start giving it. They become servants themselves. This is what Jesus meant when He said, "Love one another. As I have loved you, so you must love one another." He loved us first, and now we pass that love along. Notice that Jesus says the whole world will know we are His disciples by our love. Not by our perfect words. Not by our big buildings. By our love. So when you teach someone to serve, you are really teaching them they are loved and capable of loving. Maybe you invite a shy new person to help set up tea. You show them how, you thank them warmly, and you tell them what a difference their help made. In that small moment, they learn they matter. They learn they are loved. And soon they will be the one inviting someone else. That is how the neighborhood of God keeps growing, one loved and loving servant at a time.

Prayer:

Lord, the greatest thing I can do is help someone know they are loved and capable of loving. Today, show me who needs to hear it: the insecure teen, the discouraged spouse, the coworker who thinks they are a burden. Help me say it, show it, prove it with my time and attention. When I doubt my own lovability, remind me how You love me. I pray for everyone who feels unlovable tonight. Wrap them in love through someone like me. Amen.

Week 25 · The Great Commission: Leading by Multiplying Servants

Friday – Little Bits of Good That Overwhelm the World

"Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." — Desmond Tutu
Bible (ESV): John 14:12 — "Truly, truly, I say to you, whoever believes in me will also do the works that I do; and greater works than these will he do, because I am going to the Father."

Desmond Tutu gave us a hopeful promise. You do not have to fix the whole world by yourself. You only have to do your little bit of good where you are. Then someone else does their little bit of good where they are. And when all those little bits come together, they overwhelm the world. That is the whole secret of the Great Commission. Jesus did not ask twelve men to save the planet alone. He taught them, and they taught others, and those others taught still more. Every little bit of good added together became a flood of love that is still moving today. And Jesus made an amazing promise in John 14:12. He said that whoever believes in Him will do the works He did, and even greater things. Greater things! How could anyone do greater things than Jesus? The answer is multiplication. One Jesus on earth could touch thousands. But millions of servants, all taught by one another across the centuries, can touch billions. That is you. That is us. Your little bit of good matters more than you know. When you welcome someone at the door with a real smile, when you pour a cup of coffee with care, when you set out communion with gentle hands, when you teach a new volunteer to do the same, you are adding your little bit to the great flood. Do not wait until you feel big enough. Do not wait until everything is perfect. Do your little bit of good today, right where you are, and teach someone else to do theirs. Together, servant by servant, we will overwhelm the world with the love of Jesus.

Prayer:

Father, You promised that whoever believes will do even greater works, and it starts with little bits of good. Today, I will do mine where I am: the kind email, the extra tip, the prayer for a stranger, the trash picked up. When these acts feel too small to matter, remind me they join millions of others to overwhelm the world. I pray for every small doer of good today. Multiply us. Let my little bit be part of Your big victory. Amen.

Week 26 · The Early Church Paradigm (Acts and the Epistles)

Monday – They Shared Everything They Had

Week opening

They shared everything they had. Nobody was left unseen. They chose shepherds, not kings. This final week you will step into the book of Acts and watch servant leadership become a way of life for a whole community. ‘And all who believed were together and had all things in common.’ You have walked 130 days — from Isaiah's Servant to the early church. Now go live it: set free to serve.

"The best way to find yourself is to lose yourself in the service of others." — Mahatma Gandhi
Bible (ESV): Acts 2:44-47 — "And all who believed were together and had all things in common. And they were selling their possessions and belongings and distributing the proceeds to all, as any had need. And day by day, attending the temple together and breaking bread in their homes, they received their food with glad and generous hearts, praising God and having favor with all the people. And the Lord added to their number day by day those who were being saved."

The very first church was a family that shared. When the believers came to faith in Jesus, they did not hold tight to what was "mine." They saw a brother or sister in need, and they sold what they had to help. Nobody was forced to do it. Their hearts were full of Jesus, so their hands opened wide. They ate together, prayed together, and made sure nobody went without. That is a beautiful picture of what serving looks like: noticing a need and filling it with what you have.

This is not just about money. Gandhi said we find ourselves when we lose ourselves in serving others, and that is exactly what the first believers did. They stopped living only for themselves and started living for each other. When we share what we have — our time, our food, our help — we discover a joy that keeping everything for ourselves could never give. Real life is not found in collecting. It is found in giving.

Today, you can live like the early church. Look around your home, your school, or your work. Who needs help? Maybe a neighbor needs a meal. Maybe a friend needs a ride. Maybe someone just needs your time. Sharing what you have does not make you poor. It makes you rich in the way that matters most. When you serve others freely, people see God's love in you, just like the world saw it in that first church. Be a sharer, not a keeper, and watch how your heart grows.

Prayer:

God, the early church shared everything. No one claimed their things were only their own. Today, loosen my grip on "mine." The extra bedroom, the second car, the pantry full of food: show me who needs what I have. Help me share without keeping score and give without announcing it. Forgive my clutching and my storing up. I pray for every needy person tonight. Move Your people to share. Make my home a little outpost of Your generous kingdom. Amen.

Week 26 · The Early Church Paradigm (Acts and the Epistles)

Tuesday – Nobody Left Unseen

"Life's most persistent and urgent question is, 'What are you doing for others?'" — Martin Luther King Jr.
Bible (ESV): Acts 6:2-4 — "And the twelve summoned the full number of the disciples and said, “It is not right that we should give up preaching the word of God to serve tables. Therefore, brothers, pick out from among you seven men of good repute, full of the Spirit and of wisdom, whom we will appoint to this duty. But we will devote ourselves to prayer and to the ministry of the word.”"

The early church grew fast, and a problem came up: some widows were being missed when food was handed out each day. Widows in that time had no one to care for them, so being missed meant going hungry. The believers did not shrug and say, "Not our problem." They noticed. The apostles asked the church to choose seven trusted men, full of God's Spirit, to make sure every widow was fed. Serving tables may sound like a small job, but the church treated it as holy work. Nobody was left unseen.

Dr. King said the most important question in life is, "What are you doing for others?" The early church answered that question with action. They did not just care about preaching. They cared about dinner. They cared that the quiet, forgotten people were being looked after. That is what servant leadership looks like: you pay attention to the people others overlook, and you make sure their needs are met.

Who are the "widows" in your world — the people who might be missed? Maybe it is the new kid at school eating alone. Maybe it is an older neighbor who never gets visitors. Maybe it is a coworker who looks tired and quiet. You do not need a big title to serve them. You just need to notice. Ask God to open your eyes today. Then do something small and kind. When nobody is left unseen, everyone knows they belong. That is the kind of family God wants His church to be.

Prayer:

Lord, when the widows were overlooked, the church stopped everything to make sure no one was forgotten. Today, give me eyes for the unseen: the quiet kid in the back, the widow eating alone, the new family nobody greeted, the coworker everyone ignores. Help me answer the question "What are you doing for others?" with action, not just good intentions. I pray for everyone feeling invisible tonight. Let them be seen, known, and cared for. Make me someone who notices. Amen.

  4. No one; they ignored it

  4. Where do you live?

Week 26 · The Early Church Paradigm (Acts and the Epistles)

Wednesday – Shepherds, Not Kings

"Do small things with great love." — Mother Teresa
Bible (ESV): 1 Peter 5:2-3 — "shepherd the flock of God that is among you, exercising oversight, not under compulsion, but willingly, as God would have you; not for shameful gain, but eagerly; not domineering over those in your charge, but being examples to the flock."

In the early church, leaders were called shepherds, not kings. A shepherd does not boss the sheep around. A shepherd feeds them, protects them, and walks with them. Peter wrote that leaders should serve because they are willing, not because they have to. They should not lead for money or power. They should lead by example, showing others how to love and serve. That is a very different picture of leadership than the world gives us. The world says leaders get the biggest office and the most praise. God says leaders get the towel and the washbasin.

Mother Teresa understood this. She served the poorest of the poor in Calcutta, doing small things — cleaning wounds, holding hands, feeding the hungry — with great love. She did not need a big title. Her love was the title. Real leaders do not ask, "How can people serve me?" They ask, "How can I serve people?" When you are willing to do the small jobs nobody sees, you become the kind of leader others want to follow.

You may not be a pastor or a boss, but you are a leader somewhere. Maybe you are the oldest child at home. Maybe friends look up to you. Maybe you lead a team at work. Lead like a shepherd. Do the small things with great love. Help without being asked. Admit when you are wrong. Put others first. People will remember your kindness far longer than your words. A leader who serves is a leader who lasts, because love is the strongest power there is.

Prayer:

God, You call leaders to shepherd, not to rule, to serve willingly, not for gain or power. Today, when I lead at work, at home, or at church, keep me from bossing and bullying. Help me guide with a willing heart: the quiet help, the patient listening, the encouraging word, the humble apology. When power tempts me, remind me of the Good Shepherd who laid down His life. I pray for every leader tonight. Make us shepherds with Your heart. Amen.

1What picture does Peter use for church leaders?

  1. Kings on thrones
  2. Shepherds caring for the flock
  3. Soldiers in battle
  4. Merchants making money

  1. Forced to by the rules
  2. Willing, eager to serve, and being examples
  3. Paid a lot of money
  4. Afraid of punishment

3What does Mother Teresa's quote teach us?

  1. Only big acts of service matter
  2. Small acts done with great love are powerful
  3. Love is not needed for service
  4. Serving others is a waste of time

4How does the world's view of leadership differ from God's?

  1. They are exactly the same
  2. The world says leaders get praise and power; God says leaders serve
  3. God wants leaders to be proud
  4. The world has no leaders

5What is one way to lead like a shepherd?

  1. Boss everyone around
  2. Do the small, unseen jobs with love and help without being asked
  3. Take all the credit
  4. Never admit you are wrong

Week 26 · The Early Church Paradigm (Acts and the Epistles)

Thursday – Look at Others With Love

"We must learn to regard people less in the light of what they do or omit to do, and more in the light of what they suffer." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer
Bible (ESV): Philippians 2:3-4 — "Do nothing from selfish ambition or conceit, but in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others."

Paul wrote to the church in Philippi with a simple but hard command: stop thinking only about yourself. Do not act from selfish ambition. Do not try to look better than others. Instead, in humility, count others more significant than yourself. That means treating other people as more important than you are. It means asking, "What do they need?" before asking, "What do I want?" This was the heartbeat of the early church. They did not just believe in Jesus. They put each other first, like Jesus put us first.

Dietrich Bonhoeffer, a pastor who stood up for what was right during a dark time in history, said we should look at people not by what they do wrong or right, but by what they suffer. That is a powerful way to serve. When someone is rude to you, it is easy to judge them. But what if you asked, "What are they going through?" Maybe they are scared. Maybe they are hurting. When you look at people through the light of their suffering, your heart softens, and serving them becomes natural.

This week, practice counting others more significant than yourself. Let someone else go first. Listen longer than you talk. When someone bothers you, pause and wonder what weight they might be carrying. Serving others in love does not mean you matter less. It means you love more. And that kind of love — the kind that puts others first — is exactly what Jesus showed us on the cross. Let His love change the way you see every person you meet today.

Prayer:

Father, forgive me for thinking of myself first, my wants, my plans, my pride. Today, teach me humility: to value others above myself, to look at their interests, not just mine. In traffic, let someone merge. In line, let someone go first. At home, listen before demanding. Give me new eyes to see people's suffering, not just their faults. Soften my heart toward the difficult people in my life. Fill me with Your love so I can look to the interests of others. Amen.

Study questions

1What does Philippians 2:3-4 tell us NOT to do?

  1. Act out of selfish ambition or conceit
  2. Pray for others
  3. Help the poor
  4. Read the Bible

2What does it mean to "count others more significant than yourselves"?

  1. To think you are the most important
  2. To treat other people as more important than you
  3. To ignore everyone else
  4. To compete with others

3What does Bonhoeffer's quote ask us to look at?

  1. What people do wrong
  2. What people suffer
  3. What people own
  4. What people look like

4When someone is rude, what should we try to ask?

  1. "How can I get revenge?"
  2. "What are they going through?"
  3. "Why am I so great?"
  4. "How can I avoid them forever?"

5How did Jesus show us this kind of love?

  1. By putting us first, even to the cross
  2. By staying far away from people
  3. By seeking power
  4. By thinking only of Himself

Week 26 · The Early Church Paradigm (Acts and the Epistles)

Friday – Set Free to Serve

"There are three ways to ultimate success: The first way is to be kind. The second way is to be kind. The third way is to be kind." — Fred Rogers
Bible (ESV): Galatians 5:13 — "For you were called to freedom, brothers. Only do not use your freedom as an opportunity for the flesh, but through love serve one another."

Jesus set us free. That is wonderful news. But freedom is not just about doing whatever we want. Paul told the church in Galatia that their freedom had a purpose: through love serve one another. Think about it this way. If you were locked in a prison and someone opened the door, you would not stay in your cell. You would walk out and live. In the same way, Jesus opened the door of our hearts. Now we are free to love people the way He loves us. Freedom is not a permission slip to be selfish. It is a calling to serve.

Fred Rogers, the kind man from the neighborhood we all remember, said there are three ways to true success: be kind, be kind, be kind. That is the whole secret. The early church understood this. They were free from the old rules and free from fear, so they used their freedom to care for one another. They fed the hungry. They welcomed strangers. They forgave each other. Their kindness was not a show. It was who they had become.

This week we have learned what the early church looked like: they shared everything, they made sure nobody was unseen, their leaders served like shepherds, and they put others first. Now the lesson comes full circle. All of that was possible because they were free — free to love. You are free too. So today, choose kindness on purpose. Smile at someone who is sad. Help someone who cannot pay you back. Speak gently when you could speak harshly. When you serve others humbly in love, you prove what your freedom is for. Be kind, be kind, be kind — and watch God use your kindness to change the world around you.

Prayer:

Heavenly Father, thank You for setting me free through Jesus. I am no longer chained by sin or fear, and I am so grateful. But Lord, do not let me waste my freedom on selfish things. Today, teach me to use it the way You planned, to serve others humbly in love. Make me kind in my words, kind in my actions, kind even when it is hard. When freedom tempts me toward self, turn me toward service. I pray for everyone still in chains tonight. Set them free, and set me free to serve. Amen.

Study questions

1According to Galatians 5:13, what is our freedom FOR?

  1. Doing whatever we want for ourselves
  2. Serving one another humbly in love
  3. Ignoring other people
  4. Following strict rules to earn God's love

2What does Fred Rogers say are the three ways to true success?

  1. Be rich, be famous, be powerful
  2. Be kind, be kind, be kind
  3. Work hard, rest hard, play hard
  4. Be smart, be fast, be strong

3What did the early church do with their freedom?

  1. They used it to care for one another — feeding the hungry and welcoming strangers
  2. They used it to be selfish
  3. They gave it back
  4. They kept it a secret

4What does "serve one another humbly" mean?

  1. To serve with pride so everyone notices
  2. To serve with a lowly, loving heart, not seeking praise
  3. To only serve important people
  4. To serve only when forced

5Which of these is an example of using freedom to serve?

  1. Helping someone who cannot pay you back
  2. Keeping everything for yourself
  3. Speaking harshly to get your way
  4. Ignoring a sad friend
FRIDAY REFLECTION

Essay question: Essay question: This week you learned about the early church — how they shared everything, made sure no one was unseen, followed leaders who served like shepherds, put others first, and used their freedom to serve in love. Write a short essay (5–8 sentences) describing ONE specific way you can live like the early church this coming week. Name the person or group you will serve, explain what you will do for them, and tell how your action connects to one of this week's Bible verses or quotes.

Looking back

This week you saw the finish line become a starting line. The early church shared everything, left nobody unseen, and chose shepherds, not kings. Look at others with love. You began with Isaiah's gentle Servant and end here — set free to serve. One hundred thirty days are behind you. A lifetime of leading by serving is ahead. Go, and wash feet.

Servant Leadership Curriculum — References

Every quote and every Scripture passage used in the 130 lessons, in lesson order.

Monday – Greatness Comes Through Serving - Quote: "Everybody can be great…because anybody can serve. You don't have to have a college degree to serve. You don't have to make your subject and verb agree to serve. You only need a heart full of grace. A soul generated by love." — Martin Luther King Jr., from his sermon "The Drum Major Instinct" (Ebenezer Baptist Church, February 4, 1968) - Scripture: "Behold my servant, whom I uphold, my chosen, in whom my soul delights; I have put my Spirit upon him; he will bring forth justice to the nations. ... A bruised reed he will not break, and a faintly burning wick he will not quench; he will faithfully bring forth justice." — Isaiah 42:1, 3

Tuesday – Called and Chosen for a Purpose - Quote: "I alone cannot change the world, but I can cast a stone across the waters to create many ripples." — Mother Teresa - Scripture: "The LORD called me from the womb, from the body of my mother he named my name. ... I will make you as a light for the nations, that my salvation may reach to the end of the earth." — Isaiah 49:1, 6

Wednesday – Steady When the Road Is Hard - Quote: "We must learn to regard people less in the light of what they do or omit to do, and more in the light of what they suffer." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer, Letters and Papers from Prison - Scripture: "I gave my back to those who strike, and my cheeks to those who pull out the beard; I hid not my face from disgrace and spitting. But the Lord GOD helps me; therefore I have not been disgraced; therefore I have set my face like a flint, and I know that I shall not be put to shame." — Isaiah 50:6–7

Thursday – Carrying the Pain of Others - Quote: "What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." — Nelson Mandela (90th birthday celebration of Walter Sisulu, May 18, 2002) - Scripture: "But he was pierced for our transgressions; he was crushed for our iniquities; upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed. All we like sheep have gone astray; we have turned—every one—to his own way; and the LORD has laid on him the iniquity of us all." — Isaiah 53:5–6

Friday – The Servant Who Looks Like Jesus - Quote: "Do not waste time bothering whether you 'love' your neighbor; act as if you did. As soon as we do this we find one of the great secrets. When you are behaving as if you loved someone, you will presently come to love him." — C.S. Lewis, Mere Christianity - Scripture: "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many." — Mark 10:45

(Philippians 2:6–7)

(Mark 10:45)

Wednesday – Living for Others - Quote: "The church is the church only when it exists for others." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer, Letters and Papers from Prison (written from his prison cell, 1944) - Scripture: "Do nothing from selfish ambition or conceit, but in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others." (Philippians 2:3–4)

Thursday – A Life That Lifts Others - Quote: "What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." — Nelson Mandela, at the 90th birthday celebration of Walter Sisulu, Johannesburg, May 18, 2002 - Scripture: "Have this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus, who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men. And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross." (Philippians 2:5–8)

(Philippians 2:9–11) "And your Father who sees in secret will reward you." (Matthew 6:4)

Monday – Loved Before You Ever Lift a Finger - Quote: "There is no pit so deep that God's love is not deeper still." — Corrie ten Boom, The Hiding Place - Scripture: "And behold, a voice from heaven said, 'This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased.'" — Matthew 3:17. "We love because he first loved us." — 1 John 4:19

Tuesday – Kindness Without Keeping Score - Quote: "There are three ways to ultimate success: The first way is to be kind. The second way is to be kind. The third way is to be kind." — Fred Rogers - Scripture: "For am I now seeking the approval of man, or of God? Or am I trying to please man? If I were still trying to please man, I would not be a servant of Christ." — Galatians 1:10

Wednesday – Ordinary People, God-Sized Purpose - Quote: "Everybody can be great, because everybody can serve. You don't have to have a college degree to serve. Truly, I say to you, they have received their reward. But when you give to the needy, do not let your left hand know what your right hand is doing, so that your giving may be in secret. And your Father who sees in secret will reward you.'"

Friday – Love That Changes Enemies - Quote: "Love is the only force capable of transforming an enemy into a friend." — Martin Luther King Jr. - Scripture: Romans 5:8 — "but God shows his love for us in that while we were still sinners, Christ died for us."

Monday – Called to Die to Self - Quote: "When Christ calls a man, he bids him come and die." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer - Scripture: Mark 10:45 — "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

Tuesday – How Much Love We Put In - Quote: "Love begins at home, and it is not how much we do... but how much love we put in that action." — Mother Teresa - Scripture: John 15:13 — "Greater love has no one than this, that someone lay down his life for his friends."

Wednesday – A Life That Counts - Quote: "What counts in life is not the mere fact that we have lived. It is what difference we have made to the lives of others that will determine the significance of the life we lead." — Nelson Mandela - Scripture: 1 John 3:16 — "By this we know love, that he laid down his life for us, and we ought to lay down our lives for the brothers."

Thursday – Report for Duty - Quote: "Don't bother to give God instructions; just report for duty." — Corrie ten Boom - Scripture: Philippians 2:8 — "And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross."

Friday – Leading From Behind - Quote: "It is better to lead from behind and to put others in front, especially when you celebrate victory when nice things occur." — Nelson Mandela - Scripture: Philippians 2:5-8 — "Have this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus, who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men. And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross."

Monday – God Lifts Up the Lowly - Quote: "Not all of us can do great things. But we can do small things with great love." — Mother Teresa - Scripture: Philippians 2:8–9 — "And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross. Therefore God has highly exalted him and bestowed on him the name that is above every name."

Tuesday – Risen to Serve - Quote: "Do not waste time bothering whether you 'love' your neighbor; act as if you did." — C.S. Lewis - Scripture: Matthew 28:6 — "He is not here, for he has risen, as he said. Come, see the place where he lay."

Wednesday – Love Is Stronger Than Hate - Quote: "Darkness cannot drive out darkness: only light can do that. Hate cannot drive out hate: only love can do that." — Martin Luther King Jr. - Scripture: John 11:25 — Jesus said to her, "I am the resurrection and the life. Whoever believes in me, though he die, yet shall he live."

Thursday – Small Kindnesses, Big Victory - Quote: "Do your little bit of good where you are; it's those little bits of good put together that overwhelm the world." — Desmond Tutu - Scripture: James 4:10 — "Humble yourselves before the Lord, and he will exalt you."

Friday – Rise Again - Quote: "The greatest glory in living lies not in never falling, but in rising every time we fall." — Nelson Mandela - Scripture: 1 Peter 5:6 — "Humble yourselves, therefore, under the mighty hand of God so that at the proper time he may exalt you."

Monday – Jesus Comes to the Shore - Quote: "Forgiving is not forgetting; it's actually remembering — remembering and not using your right to hit back. It's a second chance for a new beginning." — Desmond Tutu - Scripture: John 21:4 — "Just as day was breaking, Jesus stood on the shore; yet the disciples did not know that it was Jesus." John 21:7 — "That disciple whom Jesus loved therefore said to Peter, ‘It is the Lord!’ When Simon Peter heard that it was the Lord, he put on his outer garment, for he was stripped for work, and threw himself into the sea."

Tuesday – Three Chances to Say Yes - Quote: "There is no pit so deep, that God's love is not deeper still." — Corrie ten Boom - Scripture: John 21:15-17 — "When they had finished breakfast, Jesus said to Simon Peter, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me more than these?’ He said to him, ‘Yes, Lord; you know that I love you.’ He said to him, ‘Feed my lambs.’ He said to him a second time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ He said to him, ‘Yes, Lord; you know that I love you.’ He said to him, ‘Tend my sheep.’ He said to him the third time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ Peter was grieved because he said to him the third time, ‘Do you love me?’ and he said to him, ‘Lord, you know everything; you know that I love you.’ Jesus said to him, ‘Feed my sheep.’"

Wednesday – Feed My Sheep - Quote: "Life's most persistent and urgent question is, 'What are you doing for others?'" — Martin Luther King Jr. - Scripture: John 21:16-17 — "He said to him a second time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ He said to him, ‘Yes, Lord; you know that I love you.’ He said to him, ‘Tend my sheep.’ He said to him the third time, ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’ Peter was grieved because he said to him the third time, ‘Do you love me?’ and he said to him, ‘Lord, you know everything; you know that I love you.’ Jesus said to him, ‘Feed my sheep.’"

Thursday – Follow Me, Even When It's Hard - Quote: "I learned that courage was not the absence of fear, but the triumph over it. The brave man is not he who does not feel afraid, but he who conquers that fear." — Nelson Mandela - Scripture: John 21:18-19 — "Truly, truly, I say to you, when you were young, you used to dress yourself and walk wherever you wanted, but when you are old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will dress you and carry you where you do not want to go. (This he said to show by what kind of death he was to glorify God.) And after saying this he said to him, ‘Follow me.’"

Friday – Restoring Others Gently - Quote: "The first service one owes to others in a fellowship consists of listening to them." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer - Scripture: Galatians 6:1 — "Brothers, if anyone is caught in any transgression, you who are spiritual should restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Keep watch on yourself, lest you too be tempted."

Monday – The Commission Is to Make Makers - Quote: "Not all of us can do great things. And behold, I am with you always, to the end of the age."

Tuesday – Everybody Can Serve - Quote: "Everybody can be great, because everybody can serve. You don't have to have a college degree to serve. You don't have to make your subject and your verb agree to serve. You only need a heart full of grace, a soul generated by love." — Martin Luther King Jr. - Scripture: Mark 10:45 — "For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom for many."

Wednesday – The Shepherd Leads from Behind - Quote: "A leader... is like a shepherd. He stays behind the flock, letting the most nimble go out ahead, whereupon the others follow, not realizing that all along they are being directed from behind." — Nelson Mandela - Scripture: 2 Timothy 2:2 — "and what you have heard from me in the presence of many witnesses entrust to faithful men, who will be able to teach others also."

Thursday – Loved and Capable of Loving - Quote: "The greatest thing we can do is to help somebody know that they're loved and capable of loving." — Fred Rogers - Scripture: John 13:34-35 — "A new commandment I give to you, that you love one another: just as I have loved you, you also are to love one another. By this all people will know that you are my disciples, if you have love for one another."

And they were selling their possessions and belongings and distributing the proceeds to all, as any had need. And day by day, attending the temple together and breaking bread in their homes, they received their food with glad and generous hearts, praising God and having favor with all the people. Tuesday – Nobody Left Unseen - Quote: "Life's most persistent and urgent question is, 'What are you doing for others?'" — Martin Luther King Jr. - Scripture: Acts 6:2-4 — "And the twelve summoned the full number of the disciples and said, “It is not right that we should give up preaching the word of God to serve tables. Therefore, brothers, pick out from among you seven men of good repute, full of the Spirit and of wisdom, whom we will appoint to this duty. But we will devote ourselves to prayer and to the ministry of the word.”"

Wednesday – Shepherds, Not Kings - Quote: "Do small things with great love." — Mother Teresa - Scripture: 1 Peter 5:2-3 — "shepherd the flock of God that is among you, exercising oversight, not under compulsion, but willingly, as God would have you; not for shameful gain, but eagerly; not domineering over those in your charge, but being examples to the flock."

Thursday – Look at Others With Love - Quote: "We must learn to regard people less in the light of what they do or omit to do, and more in the light of what they suffer." — Dietrich Bonhoeffer - Scripture: Philippians 2:3-4 — "Do nothing from selfish ambition or conceit, but in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others."

Friday – Set Free to Serve - Quote: "There are three ways to ultimate success: The first way is to be kind. The second way is to be kind. The third way is to be kind." — Fred Rogers - Scripture: Galatians 5:13 — "For you were called to freedom, brothers. Only do not use your freedom as an opportunity for the flesh, but through love serve one another."
